SECRETARIAL EFFICIENCY INTRODUCTION Answering Your Natural Questions About Secretarial Efficiency 1. A Standard of Secretarial Service 

To set a high standard of secretarial service before the beginner, it is fair to give a foretaste of the vigorous passage of events in the office. Advance experience can be given in the true variety that gives interest to secretarial work and in the true monotony that may otherwise appall and annoy the insufficiently prepared girl in her first position. Drill in handling variety strengthens that everywhere useful trait, adaptability. Drill in carrying monotony gives practical test to both physical and mental endurance. Convinced that the vocation of being a secretary in however small a capacity is a challenging adventure, we have not only the right but the privilege of passing along to these apprentices the genuine spirit of that adventure.

In a way as fresh, vivid, and readable as possible, therefore, the textbook Secretarial Efficiency pictures for the apprentice the nature of secretarial work in process, with due regard to facts and principles that she must learn to apply to that work. The course is straightforward and not too difficult for the student to follow, provided that she has the making of a secretary in her. It is true that the name secretary is loosely applied to a widely varying vocation, from that of the minor stenographer to that of the major secretary. It is also true that there is no assurance that a poor stenographer can earn a living as such. Even the minor position needs a relatively good stenographer.

Though Secretarial Efficiency is simple enough in its essentials to be understood by the lesser stenographer, no teacher should be expected to strain himself or herself at the task of making the course fit the innately poor stenographer or secretary who turns to this vocation because she does not know what else to study--thus sacrificing the entire class. It is to be hoped that such unpromising pupils will have been diverted to other preparation for life even before reaching this final lap in secretarial training. It is not fair to the student to let her imagine that by holding a textbook such as this in her hand and receiving barely a passing mark she can have any assurance of a claim to a secretarial pay envelope containing even a small salary. If she shows little or no promise for this vocation, she should be discouraged from attempting this course and expert investigation of her case should discover in what direction she should be turned.

Secretarial Efficiency is planned to lead the student to her own personal understanding of that elastic word secretary. Differing from the more frequent custom, this textbook does not open with a definition of that word. It is left to the apprentice as her work progresses to construct her own picture of the secretary, so that at the end of the course of study she can give her independent definition of this much-discussed word. Her application of the principles in Secretarial Efficiency will be tested by her own understanding of the seemingly simple fact that a secretary is one who does this and that, knows this and that, and is this and that. What she herself gives promise of doing, knowing, and being at the close of her preparation describes the secretary who will in her name apply for a position. Beyond and above that, she should have a clear vision of a larger definition toward which she will strive as her ideal. In this vocation, she will be constantly remaking herself as a secretary. She will learn the definition of this important word first through the experience that we can give her in the classroom and after that through the experience that she lays hold upon in the office.

INTRODUCTION 2. A New Approach to Secretarial Training, Drawn from Actual Experience 

In making this new approach to secretarial training, the author is simply passing on from actual experience what she herself wishes that she had known when she began to be a secretary. The longer she has worked and the more she has observed both the beginner in the office and the seasoned secretary, the more fully she has come to recognize that much more advanced preparation could and should have been given in both the larger guiding principles and the smaller everyday details of the vocation. Many of the items in this textbook she had to learn gradually from observation of others over a period of years.

Many she had to work out for herself to meet specific secretarial requirements--not that the methods were necessarily new, but that no one had taught them to her, with the result that she had to discover them for herself and often, no doubt, at the expense of her employers.

In offices, inquiries have been made of her by young stenographers who have frequently shown surprised appreciation at the simplest bits of advice about ways and means. "I never thought of doing it that way" or "That's really twice as easy, isn't it?"--these are typical comments. 

In a similar way, teachers are asking how certain quite commonplace things are done in the office. Even in matters that are discussed in textbooks, often no one standard is offered with a reason behind it so clear as to make good firm ground for teaching. 

So when the author came to talk with teachers about secretarial training, they exclaimed, "Why, you seem to know what we need!" It was plain that again and again on the office desk actual pieces of work had required what teachers everywhere knew should be taught, if only the material were available.

Early in her secretarial career the author would often have been glad to follow certain procedures, had she known the principles and reasons behind them. Sometimes she did not go ahead in the best way because she did not see why; not until later would that best way come to her as logical and therefore right. The girl just out of school too frequently shows the continued lack of education in these underlying principles, which some of us come to know only at the cost of long experience by the trial and error method--and some of us never discover at all. In the larger, well-organized offices the essential principles and best methods of procedure may be observed and learned with more or less precision under expert supervisors. But the girls entering the thousands of small offices have little or no opportunity to profit by direction or by "catching on" to how others proceed with diverse tasks.

Many procedures approved by the experience of many secretaries should be given to the apprentice secretary in training as the very stock in trade of her vocation. 

She should not have to wait until she is in a position to work out by individual experience the answers to such questions as: 

Why should I date everything, no matter how seemingly small? 

How should I keep my employer's calendar pad abreast of my own? 

How can I make equipment accelerate my work? ing?

Why should information be put into writ- 

Why am I expected to use colored paper for certain memorandums? office? 

How should I account for petty cash? 

What should I keep in the safe of a small 

And how? 

What is the quickest way to copy a short list alphabetically? person? sarily?

How should I read over copy with another How can I avoid working overtime unneces- 

What are the tricks of deciphering difficult handwriting? office? 

How shall I purchase supplies for a small

How can I stop handling a task several times when once will do? 

How can I find information for myself from the office manual, files, or other sources? 

Further than this, the graduate from secretarial training should have a clear-cut recognition of the continuity of business--of the single pieces of work that are but link upon link, which together form complete chains. Lack of such awareness of how business goes forward makes the new girl" singularly ignorant of the secretary's responsibility toward those links that

INTRODUCTION 

constantly pass through her hands. She suffers bewilderment, and the work suffers unintelligent handling on her part. To her each task appears as an isolated item to be checked from the day's list, without recognition of what caused that item, of what will probably grow out of it, and of what her share is in it all. 

The author's conviction of the definite need for this course today was spurred by conferences with both private and public school teachers; by her own recollection of freshly trained secretaries in the office; by conversations with young people who were already studying or were considering the study of this vocation; by a review of courses already in print, together with various aspects of her own training; and, above all, by acquaintance with the common complaint of the employer regarding the secretarial inefficiency of the so-called "trained" employee who has to be taught efficiency on the job at his expense. 

Granted the value of a new avenue of secretarial training, it is hoped that this course will help students enter good positions for their own sakes and also furnish reliable secretaries for employers whose own business or professional service can be definitely increased by competent assistants. Inasmuch as many young men and young women turn somewhat blindly to this brightly painted vocation, it is for us to give them all the light we can, firmly pointing them on the way we know. Into your hands as teachers the author confidently places the conduct of this course, trusting that this approach to the subject of secretarial practice is the natural, interesting, and essential one from the points of view of the teacher, the employer, the student, and the secretary. 

3. This Guide as a Help to the Teacher 

Secretarial Efficiency and this accompanying Guide to the student's Experience Manual bring together a variety of material for which the teacher has often had to handle many different volumes, or which she has been unable to find at all. The 

Guide offers realistic matter of so wide a nature as hardly to have been within the office experience of any one teacher. two parallel-running books--the Guide and the Manual with its accompanying Work 

The 

Sheets--are as comprehensive as space allows, without offering too long a course for the average college or advanced school.

In order to acquaint you with the new way of approaching what the usual syllabus calls "secretarial practice" or "secretarial training," the Guide purposely goes into detailed explanations. Both textbook and 

Guide are intended to supplement and to corroborate the teacher's own observation of what the employer needs and of what the classroom can do toward meeting that need.

It is hoped that the experienced teacher will find here the very course he or she has been looking for. 

We ask you not to be surprised at the somewhat obvious suggestions that are offered from time to time with the young teacher in mind--the teacher who may be undertaking a course in secretarial practice for the first time and may not have had so full or so varied an experience in actual office work as might be desired.

This key to the course offers suggestions for presentation and additional material running abreast of the sections in the textbook. In general this parallel material carries: a. Discussion of the text from the teaching point of view, giving special points to be brought out in class, especially in relation to home study of the text and the Test-yourself Questions in the student's Manual. b. Key to the exercises, called "Secretarial Experiences," with detailed suggestions about having them carried through and about their particular bearing on secretarial procedure. c. Straight dictation pertinent to the text of the sections, forming part of the Secretarial Experiences. Transcrip tions of this dictation will have a special value for the course notebook or Service

Book, which is planned for stimulating and informative reference even after a position has been taken.

The contents of the Guide are arranged in orderly detail for your easy hand-in-hand use with the textbook and Man- Each suggestion is printed at exactly the point where it will be needed for your ual.

INTRODUCTION 

help in creating and maintaining an atmosphere alive with fact and with clear-cut demands on the daily activity of your apprentices. The material throughout provides the much-demanded stress on traits. 

4. Training in Thinking Some 

Apprentices throughout the country will go out from this course to the establishments of tailors, wallpaper merchants, plumbers, manufacturers of and dealers in electric appliances, and what not. will serve officers of large companies where there are many on the staff and experienced supervisors clearly define uniform ways of following routine. Many others will go to the one-employee office where little direction is given as to procedure and independent decisions are continuously demanded. No matter what the line of business entered, much will have to be learned about the regular routine and about the business itself.

Further than this, the one element that all secretaries will find in common, and must therefore be prepared for, is the irregular duty. The apprentice must have every possible preparation for adapting her intelligence to the new situation, the peculiar duty that comes at no stated hour and in no stated way. It must be said by her employer, not only "She is good at routine," but "She is good at routine, and she knows how to use her brain."

Adaptability as a trait will depend on many other traits and on the student's thorough grasp of the principles that must be applied to one situation and another. We know that there is no such thing as a typical office. Professions and businesses vary. And, even in a given office, circumstances and demands differ in the progress of the week, the month, the year, so that rarely could an employer or a secretary select what might be called a "typical day.' 

If we say to a secretary, "What do you do in your position?" she will probably generalize about her routine work. But then she may add, "Today the oddest thing came up.

It is these "oddest" things that are not of routine (which is as a rule readily learned) for which careful advance experience must be given by drill and by a substantial background of problem situations demanding both knowledge and imagination for their solution. This course is planned for drill in 11 thinking, in adaptability, in sensing the continuity of business activities and the secretary's part in those activities. We must train the student to think out what a situation calls for, to carry out instructions by thinking, to think out the why and the how of her procedure. Intelligent thinking can then be adapted to the varied duties within any type of office. 

5. Putting Principles and Reasons into the 

Hands of the Apprentice 

Since we know that the secretary must think, we must help her to regard her work in terms of secretarial principles, which we can store actively in her mind while she is yet an apprentice. In Secre tarial Efficiency: 

PRINCIPLES ARE APPLIED TO 

(a variety of duties (the use of occupational ( skills and understand- (ing (the exercise of personal (traits These three factors of secretarial training are variously integrated within the parts of this course and cumulatively practiced in special "Chains" of business experience.

This presentation offers two outstanding advantages. One lies in the likelihood that many facts pertaining to business life and business procedure will have already been studied in courses in junior business training and in single courses such as accounting, business correspondence, and filing and therefore in their application here should appear only by way of reference and review. The other advantage lies in the very nature of the secretary's daily experience. To use a common figure, she should know what a nail is, what a screw is, what paint is. But the work on her desk demands an understanding far beyond this. It requires her to know for each duty what length of nail to choose and when, where, and how to use a nail; what size of hole to drill for her screws; what strength of paint to mix. And further still, for these activities she must know the principles of the use of the hammer, the awl, the screw-driver, the paintbrush.

The appropriate principle must be applied to the forwarding of any activity. What we are here calling "facts" are simply the

INTRODUCTION 

stock in trade for these activities. Where we find an individual student low in this stock, we must show her how to bring up her supply, whether she needs speed in shorthand or accuracy in typewriting or better spelling. But at this last stage of training, the time of the class as a whole should be devoted primarily to grasping all-essential principles.

Since a given principle may underlie many quite different duties, the duties should not be experienced for themselves but as illustrations of the principles applied. The principles are the tools in the kit of the secretary, and she must know which to choose for her next task, and why. 

The textbook bears out this attitude toward secretarial training by teaching a principle and relating text and drill and dictation to that principle, which of itself may be applied to any number of office situations throughout the business world. Often the titles of the parts and chapters, as well as the subheadings, emphasize secretarial principles, rather than secretarial duties for themselves, e.g.:

The secretary knows the importance of time 

Memorandums do not forget 

Watching the Links 

Helping your reader to think

Confidential means confidential The safe for safekeeping and safe finding 

The alphabet is your friend 

Your notebook is responsible 

Good judgment versus guessing 

Right the first time

The importance of paragraphing to filing 

The telephone commands attention

The file knows the whole story to date 

First things first, next things next 

Business promises do not take care of themselves

The written word clinches the spoken word 

Signatures carry weight

Tabulation speaks 

Opportunity can and should be given for thorough classroom acquaintance with the principles underlying certain homely incidents of secretarial life which we know occur frequently and which need not be left as a surprise for the beginner in the office, who has to undergo sufficient adjustment at best. What the particular applications of any one principle may later be for your Mary Smith no one can prophesy, but we can prepare her to think quickly and to act effectively in many kinds of situations involving that principle--that is teaching secretarial efficiency. Early in the course, emphasis should be placed upon independent decisions, on not looking around at others in class before giving an answer to class questions or entering into discussion, and on not trying to answer in such a way as to be "popular" for any one of many reasons.

The secretary who has the trait of "acting intelligently" is aware of cause and effect. She makes use of the reason why which guides her in her application of the right principle of procedure. Reasons motivate efficient secretarial activity. At every possible point, the apprentice should be asked, "How?" "Why?" And more than that, she should be taught to ask herself, "Why?" If she knows why, she will learn how much more quickly and her methods of procedure will show many desirable traits. 

Because the employer lists this kind of "curiosity" as one of the traits desirable in his secretary, we can afford to take time to ask and to answer many "why's. Why is good health essential? Why should a message be recorded in writing? Why take up this piece of work next? Why watch the calendar pad? 11 

Why and How are closely related, and work should never go ahead too swiftly for the class to understand the answers to both all-important questions. Secretarial 

Efficiency through textbook, Manual, and Guide supplies the teacher with principles and reasons. 

6. The "Chain" Principle Vital to Secretarial Efficiency 

The more we study secretaries in their initial positions, the more we are compelled to admit that they have seldom been made alive to relationships within the composite job. Preparatory experience must be given in carrying through complete business transactions from start to finish so that the student will see all the duties connected with each transaction as a single business unit, not as so many separate sec retarial duties. Secretarial Efficiency, therefore, offers straightforward insight into the theory that we shall call the "Chain principle." The apprentices are

INTRODUCTION 

led to recognize that any complete business "story" is a Chain made of many Links. The secretary deals with individual Links but keeps aware of their connection with each other and with the entire Chain so that she is intelligent about her own part in the This course is intended to deprocedure. velop the necessary "feeling" for the naturalness of the Links of business, as they grow into a variety of Chains, and an intelligent awareness of the relation of each task to Links that preceded and to Links that must follow. No matter from what side we approach the necessities of secretarial efficiency we turn to the use of the Chain idea as a practicable and all-embracing principle of office practice.

In order that the apprentice not only may understand the Chain idea but also may have a working experience of it, several sample Chains are included at the end of the Manual. At the same time, these act as a review of the secretarial practice and principles that have been studied. What the apprentice first learns by studying, she then learns by doing. Secretarial "science" has a normal right to this "laboratory method" of giving experience in specific lifelike problems from start to finish. By this method so-called Links can be assigned to the apprentice slowly enough and clearly enough to impress this Chain principle until the student is able to foresee approaching duties, precisely as she must be able to do in the office. The distance between the classroom desk and the office desk is so short that emphasis must be placed on realistic Chains as a whole and the secretary's share in handling them. This way of learning catches the attentive interest of the student because of its very naturalness and reasonableness. 

That secretarial training must include much ordinary factual information, together with well-planned, repeated drill in certain more or less isolated duties, will always prove true. Yet unless all this is carefully and sensibly integrated, it is like learning to play checkers alone.

It is as though you were given the black men and taught how to jump squares and how to move men forward until they became kings and yet were never allowed really to jump and pick up an opponent's man or--and this is most important--either to foresee or to accept the opponent's move. The real game would thus not be experienced step by step. 

So for secretarial training the Chain idea is essential, with its varied Link upon 

Link forged with full regard to what others are doing, thinking, planning. The next move is what business keeps its eye on, and the principles of secretarial instruction must rest on this attitude of anticipation. 

The Link principle gives particular expression to many of the most important traits ordinarily demanded by the employer --alertness, initiative, sense of responsibility, imagination, memory. It gives reason to many of the common procedures of the office, such as: the necessity of dating even the slightest memorandum; care in pushing through the transcription of all shorthand from the notebook so that no Chain is broken by neglect of a Link; and the need of filing with accuracy so that the history of each transaction stands as a unit the very moment it may be desired.

Not until the author began work on the textbook and its related exercises and attempted for this purpose to formulate a simple presentation of this principle did she realize how it dominates the daily experience of the secretary. Because the most common transaction may involve many 

Links, the most difficult Chains to prepare for classroom use are not the long ones but those that at first glance promise to be brief. That the secretary's daily business is dominated by this principle was confirmed for the author when she returned to actual files which she had handled over a period of years in a certain business office. There she discovered that what on first recollection would seem to have been a simple Chain in reality involved many Links and many duties--telephoning, an interchange of memorandums, perhaps a telegram, letters, conferences. Without question, she knew from her own experience that above all else the "feel" for this principle must become ingrained in the apprentice secretary and that in the process of acquiring that "feel" it would be not only possible but inevitable to integrate the three essentials: technical skill, understanding, and that intangible necessity called the "personal trait. An indispensable feature of Secretarial Efficiency became this principle of the normal succession of related Links in all their variety--the teaching of this principle to lay full stress on the 11

INTRODUCTION 

fact that it is the prime duty of the secretary to leave no Chain hanging unfinished, provided that she herself can do anything whatever to help form that next Link.

After a little study this new approach to secretarial practice seems so obvious that we may well wonder why we have not always given to our apprentices this real everyday taste of the vocation that they are choosing to enter. The Chain idea is developed for the student in Chapter 3 of the text, called "Watching the Links,' as well as in practical work. Chapter 16, "Carrying Through," is given midway in the course as a specific repetition and review, after the apprentice has from experience somewhat learned the significance of the principle.

The material for the six Chains (A to F) is placed at the close of the Manual and the Guide. The point at which each 

Chain should be assigned and carried through is indicated at the beginning of each Chain; also, by way of cross reference, note of such assignment is made in the Guide, at each point. It should be understood that teaching of the Chains is to be conducted abreast of other work in the course. They are printed together at the end of the book as a matter of orderly arrangement. It is assumed that, as suggested, the teacher will bring in the study of the Chains in the most advantageous places. It need hardly be said that it is a good idea to have material in such a manual so arranged that the pupil does not go from page 1 to the end without some break in the assignments to bring in other problems which sum up the ground covered so far. By putting the

Chains at the end and suggesting their assignment at the proper periods, the dull monotony of following the text straight through from beginning to end is eliminated and "breaks" will be inserted every now and then for a change in procedure. This should serve to increase the interest and hold the attention of the student. 

7. An Integrated Course for Office Practice 

As a book for the use of secretaries, Secretarial Efficiency may well be used as a reference work. As a textbook for students, Secretarial Efficiency gives a course in the principles of secretarial practice which should be studied consecutively, as the principles of shorthand or typewriting or filing are studied. In mak- ing assignments, it should be recognized that the course is planned with care to give thorough acquaintance with secretarial procedures and the reasons behind those procedures. It is wholly desirable for each student to have his or her own copy of the basic text, to which assignments refer for the Vocabulary Experiences, the Test yourself Questions, and the Secretarial Experiences. The assignments in the Manual can be intelligently carried through only after the study of the corresponding chapter of the textbook.

The course has been balanced with the full intention of giving the student insight into matters that can and should be understood before a position is taken. The somewhat easy style, as of one secretary talking with another, should not deceive the student into thinking that here is a book merely to be read. It is a text to be studied in preparation for laboratory work as one studies the principles of chemistry or physics in preparation for carrying through experiments in the laboratory. The textbook gives the essential principles; the Manual with its Work Sheets gives opportunity for expression of those principles. This should be studied daily by the individual, as the course goes forward, in home study that prepares for class discussion. The text is not supplementary material to the Manual; they go abreast as essential to one another. The course is planned, with consistent cumulative force, to arm the secretary-to-be for her first day's work in the office. The themes on which instruction is based, shown by the part and chapter titles and often by the subheadings, are all vital for learning this widely in clusive subject. Secretarial Efficiency is a mosaic of secretarial necessities, and all the pieces of this mosaic should be recognized as part of the pattern for secretarial training. Even the illustrations have been prepared in detail for intensive study. 

Each part of the text is bound by a single principle or group of related principles in order to make the principle itself the indelible concern of office practice. 

The parts are not of uniform length, nor are the chapters of those parts of uniform length. As much should be assigned as your students can take care of, both for homework and for schoolwork. Of course, no two

INTRODUCTION 

classes ever accomplish an identical amount of work in a given period; classes have individual differences just as the members of the class have varying abilities. Sometimes you will find the material rather more technical; sometimes for the sake of unity the chapter runs to greater length. 

Each chapter, however, is broken by subheadings into reasonable lengths for sustained interest.

Early in the textbook, we present the framework, showing the goal of secretarial efficiency as a whole. Then we help the apprentice to see how and where parts should be incorporated into this framework. 

This, as a matter of fact, is the manner in which the apprentice will learn in her first position--first seeing vaguely what the specific business she is assuming is about, then fitting details of her position more and more intelligently into the framework as a whole. This is the usual learning process, and our course gives ample play to the fitting of a multitude of important elements into the secretarial picture as an integrated whole. 

In order to give immediate insight into meeting actual office situations successfully, many important secretarial duties are taken up in relation to the particular work in process, rather than as a matter of theoretical study. This is not a course about office practice so much as a course in office practice. Even the study of English is made to serve this method. 

Filing is not taught in this course; it is used. In connection with that use, actual letters, which have been handled from the start, will be prepared for filing, drawn for necessary use from the file, accumulated into natural, complete Chains in the file. Secretarial Efficiency merely touches the necessary principles of alphabetical❘ filing so that in these months of practice all students may use a uniform method while carrying forward the actual filing. We assume that students have already studied the details of filing as a skill, either in a previous course in junior business training or in such a course as is suggested in the teaching material for Section 13. 

In a somewhat similar way, telephoning is not taught for the facts in themselves, though certain facts are looked up or reviewed with relation to Chapter 10, 

"Using the Wires--Why? When? How?" The 

One telephone conversation and the wrting of the telegram and the making out of checks and other such duties are emphasized not as factors offered in isolated drill but as factors linked with real situations. such situation will bring the purpose of a telegram more to life than the dictation of a series of telegrams with no evident relation to each other or to any other duty that is performed.

Other matters of factual learning are related as directly as possible to the necessity of learning-to-do. The caller comes in the employer's absence. What does the message the secretary takes down and then leaves on the employer's desk lead to?

To an appointment, a conference, a memorandum to some other official of the company, a purchase order, the payment of a bill, a telephone call to the man who called, the gathering of some informational data for the caller's use, a letter dictated to accompany that data? Always through Secretarial Efficiency the apprentice should experience linking the fact to the act and linking the act into the Chain of which it is a progressive part. In

Instruction in secretarial efficiency is a cumulative affair--a continuing thing, just as work in the office is a continuing process with interruptions overnight and over the week end. It is that continual flow which makes for results in what must be a progressive business. preparation for the composite job the apprentice must experience the everyday and every-hour fact of integration, accepting her training as one well-rounded whole. The daily use of double periods--a class period followed by a laboratory period--will permit your running dictation over into a second hour or allowing other tasks to extend beyond the limitations of a single hour.

This will stimulate the power of endurance for masses of work such as accumulate to challenge the secretary.

There is plenty of material here to occupy a semester, if the class meets every day for a double period, or for a whole year if the class meets two or three times a week for a double period. There will always be places where some classes may need to spend more time and where the teacher may wish to bring in extra practice on some particular type of problem; or, because of previous training, some experiences may be

INTRODUCTION 

passed over quickly. Much depends on the individual class and the amount of training in the various skill subjects that the student may have had before taking this course for the rounding-out, summing-up process. 

8. Meeting the Usual Syllabus for "Secre tarial Practice"

This course meets the essential requirements of the usual syllabus or outline for secretarial training, especially in the integration of skills, vocational knowledge, and traits. The class notebook that is expected of the student is here thoroughly outlined for its practical cumulative growth throughout the course. Vocabulary study and work in English are applied to individual needs. The usual duties, such as handling callers, planning itineraries, using office reference books, keeping a mailing list, telephoning, and attending to the mail, are taught through Secretarial Experiences based on the text itself. The essential subject of traits is dealt with in two chapters, one in the first and one in the last unit of work, but is more especially integrated with the study and performance of whatever the apprentice has to do. 

9. Traits as the Seasoning of Secretarial Efficiency 

Because the requirements of the employer must be our practical guide in training the secretary for employment, this course stresses the outstanding demand for "personality." Teacher and student together are challenged to take this demand seriously from the outset by thoughtful and untiring work to meet the standards set in Chapter 4, called "How Much Are Your 

Traits Worth?" The words that stand for secretarial traits or ways of behaving at work are scattered like seasoning throughout the text, the Test-yourself Questions, and the Secretarial Experiences. 

We must, indeed, be cautious about overdoing the word trait lest the necessary frequency of reference tend to bore or amuse the apprentice. 

The fact that personality has its commercial value in gaining and keeping a position and in meriting promotion is one that puts character development on a plane which can be understood by the student who knows that she must shortly go out to earn her living. After all, it is not an unpleasant ambition to wish to offer a personality that will prove acceptable to a desirable employer. This ambition should look possible of realization to the secretary-in-the-making when she recognizes that, if she works accurately, responsibly, quickly, co-operatively, attentively, honestly, courteously, industriously, and intelligently, you will be justified in recommending her as accurate, responsible, quick, cooperative, attentive, honest, courteous, industrious, and intelligent. Teacher, parent, and employer must all agree that much of a girl's career depends on the way she behaves in all parts of her work. so.

No task that is assigned in the Experience Manual is free from its share of trait development. You will find traits at the heart of your grading, intangible though they may seem to be. Whenever a trait is outstandingly exercised by the material given, we may call the attention of our apprentices to their cultivation of this specific asset. If the class in general shows deficiency in any one trait, watch every opportunity to stress that trait wherever it may be reasonable to do If a given piece of work has brought out a specific trait remarkably well in either the group or an individual, make public acknowledgment of this. If on the other hand a piece of work has been put through by the class inaccurately, too slowly, without attention to instructions, without imagination about the situation involved, here is an opportunity to lead the students to think of the impression this very example of the negative trait would have made, had this work come to an employer for his signature or immediate use otherwise. or One of the purposes of this course is to carry over to the would-be secretary not only the challenge to faithful and intelligent service, but also the atmosphere of truly attractive interest that she has reason to look for in her vocation. Many secretaries come out every morning from homes that, through no fault of their own, are uncongenial, difficult beyond their own power to change. Such young women have a right to a school preparation that will help them to approach work with eager interest and with self-confidence. It is not idly sentimental to foster enthusiasm about secretarial work as a whole or in its

INTRODUCTION 

parts. The secretary who has been shown how to "put her heart into her work" is not only the happier self, but also the more efficient employee. Vital interest, combined with intelligence, makes the indispensable secretary. She has a right to the pursuit of happiness within her workaday world.

In Chapter 23, the problem of emotional upsets is briefly presented to the student. Individual conferences about specific pieces of work can often reach important personality obstacles. The matter of dealing with "petty" hindrances to personality is a definite responsibility of the ready and wise teacher. You have it in your power to encourage the student to persistent effort in the direction of trait improvement. It may be helpful from time to time for you to look at each apprentice as if she were already in some office you know. How can you help a given apprentice to meet the demand of that office that she enough personality and not too much,' as employers put it? This course is a moral training ground in so far as it concerns the relation of personality to moneymaking efficiency. With all our intelligent modern phraseology, we should not lose sight of the time-old fact that an employer wishes an employee who "has character." 11 11 It is true that in the office much that is well done is accepted as a matter of course, but young people do need encouragement and the teacher with limited experience in this field may well make conscious effort to gain co-operation in this "model office" by giving praise at the right time, by using the "I-know-you-cando-it" attitude, instead of letting the student feel, simply because favorable criticism is seldom given, that the teacher employer is constantly disappointed. 

10. Correct English as the Sinew of Secre-. tarial Strength 11 In order to help our students break away from respect for trite phrases, we must keep them aware of the fact that there is no separate language such as that sometimes called "business English. The higher the quality of English provided in dictation, in text study, and in classroom speech throughout a secretarial training course, the more ready should the apprentice be to understand, to transcribe, and to speak good English. The classroom itself should offer an environment where the best speech--simple, clear, forceful, correct--is used. 

Secretarial Efficiency gives practice field for the application of principles of English that have been studied throughout previous education. The text deliberately refrains from reprinting rules of grammar and punctuation, which would consume space needed for teaching material not readily found elsewhere. At this point in secretarial training, if a pupil wishes to look up a rule or you believe that she should look up a given rule that she repeatedly breaks, let her become accustomed to hunting independently for just such a reference. Practice in looking up rules now will save her embarrassment when she has no longer at hand a teacher to correct and advise about either her written or her spoken English.

Correct English is a part of everyday tasks. For this reason the demand for good English should be integrated immediately with the Secretarial Experiences, as it is integrated with daily experience in the office. For habit formation, drill should be given to test the understanding of the concrete problems in hand. When a specific error appears quite generally in the work of the class, one or more students may be asked to find the rule and to bring it home to the remainder of the class through dictation of that rule and of examples. For the most part, as we well know, it will be the habit thus formed and not the rule itself that will stand by the student on the next occasion. "What sounds right?" "What looks right?" Good habits must give the correct immediate answer to these questions for use in meeting callers, in conducting telephone conversations, and in transcribing or composing usable typewritten work. 

11. Teaching Use of the Test-yourself Questions

The Test-yourself Questions are planned to give the student a chance to prove her power of independent thinking, not to test her word-for-word memory of the text. 

To answer these questions, she must combine her study of the material in the textbook with her observation and her experience. As in any course, more obvious questions may be devised for class use. The

INTRODUCTION 

11 students themselves may be asked to think out such questions--for a searching question cannot be constructed without a full understanding of the answer. Both types of question should be discussed in class and tested by brief written quizzes. The meaning of the text must be mastered by each apprentice since the principles embodied in the text are the very foundation of secretarial practice. These must be completely understood before the student can apply herself intelligently to a set of Experiences in a given section.

The majority of space in the Guide Key is devoted to practical suggestions for handling the Secretarial Experiences be cause they show better than any questions whether or not the student has grasped the principles of a chapter of the text. The 

Key to the Test-yourself Questions tends merely to give direction to class discussion--to indicate what we are after in applying such questions to the study of the text. It is helpful to have students interview members of their families or friends who are secretaries or employers from whom actual problems can be brought in. 

The teacher will have no difficulty in selecting or combining questions for tests of text study. It will lend interest and be of cumulative value to the student if you work out ways of integrating these tests with various skills and traits. This is not so difficult as it sounds, and you will find that the preparation of such tests will give you added confidence in your ability to teach by this integrated method of Secretarial Efficiency. As with all such school tests, the object of your test of text study is to discover whether, as we say, the student has "studied her lesson" and to discover any point that has not been made clear or presented thoroughly so that it may be worked in again. You can at the same time give a test in some one other secretarial necessity, such as: a. Alertness. Give your test orally, in this case, so that there is a definite time limit for writing the answer.

This will give experience in answering an employer's question immediately, without having to "hunt around" for the answer. If the student has really learned the text of a chapter, there should be no hesitation about the answers to simple questions. State b. Vocabulary and grammar. to the students that the forthcoming test on a given chapter will be graded one-half on what they write and one-half on how they write it, both as to choice of words and as to correct and forceful expression. of course, correct use of words necessitates correct spelling of those words. c. Punctuation, paragraphing, word division. Demand that these be given special attention while the student is taking a certain test that is answered on the typewriter. d. Taking dictation to the typewriter, and composing nicely on the typewriter. Dictate a question to be typed; then pause while the answer is written, allowing a reasonable time, and gauging somewhat by the gradual decrease of active typewriters. State that not only the answer itself, but the correctness of this "first copy," from the point of view of typewriting, will be graded. e. A test taken in handwriting should stress the importance of legible penmanship, as well as the content of the answers. Penmanship should, of course, be readable at all times. f. Dictation for shorthand integration. Dictate the questions in succession at a normal speed, giving them by number.

Then expect the answers to be written out in longhand or on the typewriter, without first transcribing the questions. This trains in actual use of shorthand as it stands--a use often made when instructions are given or messages dictated for immediate transmission without opportunity for intervening transcription of the notes. In addition, this kind of exercise gives elasticity to the handling of shorthand as a ready-to-hand tool.

Many such tests may be devised to meet your special class problems; no two groups are alike, and drills cannot be stereotyped. These integrated tests will keep before the student the fact that even the results of studying a text assignment cannot be separated from the way those results are used. Let a very brief test give experience in the concentrated task of the

INTRODUCTION 

office; let a long test try out the endurance; plan a monotonous test; make out a test that requires imagination to answer. 

Call attention to the traits that have been brought out in various types of tests--patience, intelligence about facts, intelligence about principles, reasoning ability, honesty, orderliness. You have an opportunity to give to your students an assurance in meeting these tests, which give experience in the combined requirements of "tests" in the office itself. In so far as we can keep aware of this principle of integration, in oral and in written work, we shall have the continued advantage of watching the individual not as a student of isolated factors in secretarial practice, but always as an apprentice in the experience of secretarial efficiency. 

12. Suggestions about Dictation for This 

Course a. Manner of Dictating. For secretarial efficiency, shorthand should be made a live factor of classroom procedure; elasticity will thus be given to its use for the briefest possible purposes, as well as for long periods of endurance. The student should feel that this is true to life. Instructions should be dictated. Class discussion should sometimes be based on matter while it is still in untranscribed shorthand so that this medium may come to seem a natural one. During the course, dictation is not graded according to difficulty in respect to length, speed, or vocabulary. We assume that the students possess facility in the use of shorthand and typewriting and we must now give them opportunities to use that skill in situations that correspond to those of the average office, where work is easy or difficult according to the way the day happens to go. The apprentice must learn to take dictation with no special regard as to what is "hard" and what is "easy." 

One of the greatest kindnesses you can do these apprentices of yours is to break them immediately of the habit of expecting a rhythmic, timed, word-correct dictation. Assured of both technique and speed, they should now be given "natural" dictation with all its irregularities, all its interest. Toward the end of the course, speed drills may be given if you feel them to be necessary for your specific class or for individual students. Speed once attained can always be regained. We must keep in mind that this course admits students not to renewed drills in technical skill so much as to an integration of those already-acquired skills with the other factors of actual secretarial activity. We must now urge the student to shift intelligent application of shorthand to all sorts of normal contingencies.

The students should fully understand the "natural" method of giving dictation, which you will pursue during the course. For your aid in this particular, dictated matter furnished in the Guide for your use in the Secretarial Experiences has been "ticked" at no prescribed dictation rate, but rather with such phrasing as the employer normally uses when he is thinking out" what he wishes to have transcribed.

Throughout correspondence given for dictation the sign (P) indicates one correct way of paragraphing. Often you may wish to mention the word paragraph, as many employers do. Frequently, however, you should imitate the employer who relies on his secretary to "sense" his paragraphs, in order to give experience in this important duty. In grading papers given by this latter method, it is fair to remember that any reasonable breaking down of matter into paragraphs is correct; the student must prove that she is thinking of the content of the letter, or, as the text teaches in Chapter 19, is "helping her reader to think." In important letters, like sales letters, the dictator normally indicates the separation of specific points; you can judge when such mention is appropriate. In dictating, inject as much irregularity as your own class can possibly meet, by: patience

Long periods of dictation to test endurance 

Series of brief matters 

Sudden gushes of dictation 

Then the more drawling intervals that tax

Dictating indistinctly or back to the class or walking about the room 

Interruptions that demand "keeping the thing in mind" 

Changes in order of sentences or paragraphs

Added inserts and postscripts 

Making of errors in construction or vocabulary.

INTRODUCTION 

13 In such ways as this you have an opportunity to prevent the panicky feeling that accompanies the first dictation in the actual office. A strange man, strange surroundings, strange voices, and quite probably unusually long periods of dictation using a specialized vocabulary meet the new secretary. More detailed suggestions about dictation are given in the Guide, Section 5, 

"Dictation Means Responsibility." The nat ural manner of dictating should be used not only for reception in shorthand, but also for reception directly on the typewriter or on the dictating machine or stenotype. When dictating to the typewriter, it is important to pause until every typist has stopped. Attempting to hear over the clatter of even a few machines makes good work difficult, even though it is desirable to maintain a speed that will test the average student. 

b. Material. Dictation material is placed throughout this Guide at the points where the student will most profit by the significance of the matter and by the experience involved in handling the transcription. This includes straight matter and letters, memorandums, telegrams, and rough data. The dictation involved in what are called the "Chains" leads in importance. You will be using this course not for shorthand or typewriting drill for themselves, but for the application of active office principles to the use of these skills. It is necessary for the student to take dictation at a good speed, but in actual office experience it is fully as important for the secretary to know what a dictated memorandum is about, why it should be transcribed promptly, how to watch for a reply. 

At any time you may feel justified in dictating direct from the textbook itself for transcription. If you dictate from a chapter not yet studied, you will give a foretaste of some important principle. If you dictate from chapters that have been studied, you will be giving review, which should be transcribed readily because the vocabulary will have been learned. If you dictate from the particular chapter under study, this will be comparable to dictation in reply to an incoming letter, which the secretary has just opened and placed on her employer's desk so that she is familiar with the matter itself. 

Any such material that you choose may be transcribed on punched paper for the Service Book. 

For the transcription of material dictated from the textbook, a high sense of honor in the matter of independent honest work should be expected of the apprentice. 

The textbook should not be used as an aid by any student. Correctness of copy and good independent work are two of the requisites of this course as a vital part of secretarial experience. c. Knowing the Content. One of the most important elements of this course is the demand that apprentices know what is being dictated. They must follow thoughtfully. From the very start this expectation should be stressed. At the close of the very first paragraph you dictate, ask to have notebooks closed and catch your "woolgatherers." Give a moment for them to think about what has just been taken down. 

Then call on several for their impressions. 

Point out to the class the good points of these answers--the fact that Miss S gained a clear impression of the entire paragraph, that Miss J caught the main point well.

Such watchfulness should be developed intelligently throughout the course. We need never consider the moment wasted that is spent on getting the apprentice to think what he or she is about. This habit of alert attention or "interest" is an essential among traits required by employers. 

Have someone write on the board in shorthand:

Always gather the meaning of dictation The apprentice should write shorthand that can be read by others in the office, when need arises. d. Uses of Dictation. Matter that is dictated from your Guide under numbered Experiences has instructions for the student in the Manual under the corresponding numbers. The procedure with transcription is varied to cover different secretarial practices set in different patterns. This integrates the work for your convenience. 

The material may be used in other ways in accordance with your judgment and the time limitations for your conduct of the entire course.

INTRODUCTION 

These pieces of straight dictation are by no means printed to pad out the course. They contain many essentials for the knowledge and the practice of the apprentice that belong to the textual study but are given in this form to vary the method of presenting instructional matter. 

They may be used with elasticity, but they should not be considered supplementary in the sense of being extra material to be used or not. 

If you have extra time during the course or feel the need for your class, an article may be dictated rapidly one day, with the requirement that notes be taken as of a speech. Reporting gives exercise to attention, judgment, and independent thinking. And reporting cannot be done properly without an ample vocabulary and concise English construction. On a later day, the same article, with which the students are now somewhat familiar, may be given to them at a fairly good rate of dictation to be taken word for word. 

If there is any article that the entire class has not time to take, you may run off a typewritten copy from your Guide for the following uses. Assign the study of the article to apprentices who can take care of additional work, to give experience in gathering data in condensed form. Let these advanced students make a brief résumé, and call on one to give this before the class by way of report or for dictation in this abbreviated form. This will exercise judgment, responsibility, intelligent grasp of outstanding facts, and power to put those facts clearly before others. copy may be used for exercise in taking and transcribing notes. Let the students rotate as dictators on different occasions, releasing you for more painstaking drill and individual work with the slower students. Work that can be done independently gives assurance and demands keen attention on the part of the student dictator. She must command the co-operation of her classmates as she must command the co-operation of her office associates.

It may be noted that, when advanced students transcribe matter with a content value for their Service Books, the slower student has missed something that she may need for later reference. Here, then, is an opportunity to integrate an essential trait with a piece of work. In the office there must be mutual respect and helpfulness between employees of differing abilities. As an illustration of this, let your high-grade students graciously make carbon copies of this extra material for the lower grade students. The latter should accept such copies in an appreciative spirit. Such sharing will give experience not only in making several worthy carbon copies--an exercise that time and expense allow all too little in school--but also in co-operation and helpfulness between office associates. 

It also gives a taste of the habit of sharing information in an office. Often a fellow member of the staff sees the value of making an extra copy of information to pass along with, "Thought you might like to know about this. Regard of this kind makes for a closely knit organization, and this may be made an opportunity for you to teach this fact. 11 

13. Grading, the "Pay Envelope" of the Apprentice Secretary

The task of grading in any course in secretarial training is no easy one for the teacher, for so many factors force themselves on the attention simultaneously --above all, that intangible factor of personality. The grade becomes comparable to the amount in the pay envelope for the secretary herself. Indeed, the student should understand from the start that the written or oral recommendation she will win from you depends on what she entitles her own self to, in the light of her grasp of the entire course. 11 a. Grading for efficiency as a whole. The title of Part I of the textbook is purposely chosen to give foreknowledge of this important fact of the vocation: "The Secretary Is Paid for Efficiency as a

Whole." We shall go far toward helping the apprentice to devote herself wholeheartedly to tasks she is "not good at" or "does not like, if we can hold before her the desirable picture of the well-rounded secretary as the one whom the employer must choose. 

We can lead her to realize that she is not an A student in typewriting unless she is also an A student in the shorthand that pours into typewriting and in the spelling and grammar that make for mailable transcriptions. This does not mean that she can be perfect in every particular, for

INTRODUCTION 

15 there are diversities of gifts, but she cannot isolate her expert skill in shorthand from her inexpert recognition of words that are dictated to her. It is upon this cumulative integration, as we have said, that this course is based. Grading of review work will always take this into consideration. b. Grading for ability to follow instructions. The demand of employers for secretaries who can follow instructions should be given special regard throughout this course by calling definite attention to concrete instructions that either have or have not been heard or interpreted correctly. Notations of the following kind on papers, transcriptions, tests will help to drive this home: carefully. 

Good answer; you read the instructions

This work shows that you were listening when instruction regarding the margin was given. 

If you had listened to the instructions attentively, you would not have made this error. 

You usually follow orders more intelligently. Look back at your notes to see why you neglected to make this double-space, as instructed. 

A carbon copy should have accompanied this. 

Go back to see whether you (1) did not hear instruction, or (2) did not write it down, or (3) did not heed it after writing it down. 

In an office your failure to mark this for the attention of Mr. Brown, as instructed, would make a serious difference.

Reread the instructions regarding preparing sheets for the Service Book. Instructions given once for all must be remembered in the office.

This piece of work shows improvement in your ability to carry out detailed instructions. 

Such work takes times but rewards the teacher in her effort to teach these intangible but essential traits. 

You may find it valuable to make a numbered list of such notations. These may be dictated to your class. When you are grading papers, you need to write down only the notation number. In referring to the correction intended, the student will be gaining experience in looking up such matters as form paragraphs or letters that have code numbers. 

In large classes a system of this kind proves helpful. c. Grading for over-all time taken to complete a job. Early in the course, emphasis should be placed on the timing of all work of a specific nature. The student must become time-conscious if she is to know how to plan and to accomplish her work on the job. If possible, have work marked with time begun and time finished-or else have approximate over-all time giv- It should be clear to the student that the time taken for an assignment includes all time--finding supplies, reading the directions, carrying through to the very finish. Only in this way shall we be able to grade the student on speed of motion, of getting at a given piece of work, in seeing through that piece of work. en.

The time limits that you establish for assigned work depend on your judgment of the ability of the class as a whole, and it is true that everything should be done on time. Notwithstanding the need of speed, you should try to get students to relax, to throw off tension, except just enough to keep up reasonable intensity of effort, which is exactly the secretarial experience they must have or they will not be able to stand severe, continuous work. They should not be crowded; neither should they be allowed to loiter at their work. 

Individual motion study of the apprentices may be made so that oftenrecurring waste motions may be corrected. 

Orderliness will help speed. And again and again you can encourage the right attitude toward difficulties that must be expected, welcomed, diagnosed, and overcome, so that fear and hesitation do not retard accomplishment. Analysis of work habits will contribute largely toward completion of assignments, whether carried through at school or at home. Attention, interest, clear purpose, industry, "stick-to-it-iveness" are some of the traits the teacher may continually help to cultivate. In such a course as this, the pupil must assume increased responsibility for the proper use of her class time and homework time, if she is to order her work properly in the office. 

Three time elements enter: (1) preparation to do a job; (2) getting the finished job ready to deliver; (3) changing from that job to the next. 

In giving a foretaste of secretarial experience, it is important to grade the

INTRODUCTION 

student not only on the single task but on a full day's work for which she must shortly stand the test. One of the most difficult adjustments for the beginning office worker is to the long hours and to the fact that they are inevitable. No free afternoons except Saturday. No free day except 

Sunday. And sometimes overtime work. It should prove extraordinarily helpful if the students could be earnestly urged, if not required, to spend, toward the end of the course, one or more entire days a week at their desks. In this way they would become somewhat hardened. Not only would their endurance be tested and trained, but they would come to know how much (and how little) can be put through in a day.

This suggestion is made here with the thought that the teacher may be able to work toward this possibility. For such days, it would be entirely practicable from the point of view of definite preparation for a secretarial position if class appointments outside this course could be suspended for your apprentices. Long periods of dictation should be given with little cessation. And then long periods of transcription should be expected, so that the day's work will be in its way typical. 

Jerky periods of varying demands should be "experienced" also, to train in the trait of poise. Interruptions and distractions of a typical nature might well be planned. 

Full successive hours, with an hour out at noon, would give an advance taste of "what it is going to be like." This would be true-to-life "office practice" and should be graded as such. d. Grading from the point of view of traits. Nothing is more important or more difficult than the grading of traits toward a judicial record to be kept in the office of your school for use in the recommendation of each apprentice. If you stay in the same school for many years and if you have an extraordinary memory about the scores of individuals whom you help to train, you may be relied on for direct recommendations. But every student has a right to a fair written estimate of her personality as well as her work itself so that five years or more after her graduation an employer can be told from the school records what promise she gave in her school days as a candidate for the position he is trying to fill. 

Contingencies of many kinds bear on such grading. For instance, the apprentice who is obliged to use the least good typewriter for a quiz that is being graded for speed is handicapped. But if she meets that handicap with patience and resourcefulness, those traits help to balance the time discrepancy. The girl who regularly sits beside the apprentice with whom the class in general finds it "difficult" to get along may have an extra tax on her poise, her courtesy, and her attention. The girl who returns to school after the death of her father may be handicapped by loss of sleep, nervous strain, and weight of new responsibilities, as well as the gap in the continuity of the course, which she has to meet under these very conditions. The same "feelings" would carry into return to office work. The girl who has a conspicuous physical defect has the harder fight for courage and self-confidence. Each one of these cases sounds remote from the teaching of the ordinary schoolroom. But vocational training must take into consideration these contingencies that are a living part of the stuff of which secretarial service is made. They require thoughtful weighing and cumulative recording if we are to "rate" the graduates of this course helpfully for themselves and for their prospective employers. How well does a given student make adjustments in her own behavior in the interests of even conduct of her own work in the face of her own obstacles?

The possibility for growth shown by the individual student is the most important aspect in the grading of traits. The apprentice who does not show improvement in personality can hardly expect to hold her own as a secretary, for the employer must have subordinates who become increasingly dependable, increasingly intelligent, increasingly able to accomplish work with dispatch. If we help the apprentice to look objectively at her own capacities for poise, courtesy, initiative, and adaptability, she is set toward growth. Our ideal is to foster this attitude now so surely that a given apprentice of today will at the close of five years of secretarial work find herself looking on some difficult completed task and justly saying to herself, "I couldn't possibly have handled that with such accuracy, tact, speed, or intelligence

INTRODUCTION 

17 11 five years ago. Though we must have clearly in mind an absolute standard of qualifications for the graduate, the student who has shown growth in approaching that standard can be said to be a "promising" secretary. Many employers tell us that "the green girl is worth less than nothing," but they are willing to undergo a reasonable period of waiting for the promising girl to outgrow that worthless stage.

14. Individual Differences and the Final Recommendation

Early in the course, make an opportunity to explain to your apprentices your own intention of helping each one to bring her weak points up to the required level. 

It is advisable for the class as a whole to realize directly from you that some will need more practice than others in certain work and all must be willing to practice as much as is necessary. No one who is required to do extra practicing should feel that she is being discriminated against. No one who is relieved from such extra practice should feel either favored or superior; there is more than enough for even the best apprentice to learn in this course. It is important for the individual to expect individual treatment, for she is preparing for independent work and should now gain "experience" in not feeling that either favorable or unfavorable discrimination is being accorded her, since such a "feeling" can make her unhappy and therefore inefficient in the office itself. as 

The recommendation merited by Mary

Hayes at the close of this course makes, we all know, a heavy demand on your judgment. 

Her individual differences from Betty Lord may definitely qualify or disqualify one or the other for a given position. A particular burden is placed upon you as her teacher, in the fact that you do not know the employer under whom Mary is to enter secretarial work. What will that Mr. X expect of Mary? Does his business demand high speed of Mary's shorthand? Or does he have to think out serious problems with deliberation as he dictates, so that he needs a Mary who can sit patiently while she takes down careful phrasing? Will Mary's desk be in a large room where she must know how to concentrate against a variety of distractions of adding machines, typewriters, telephoning, and necessary conversation all about her? Or will she be alone in a small office so that she must learn rather to work ahead quietly by herself without dependence on the companionship she has enjoyed throughout school life? Will the dictation of Mr. X include many figures so that accuracy of that special sort must be at Mary's finger tips? Or does he need to have Mary ready with an apt vocabulary and quick sense for correct English so that his roughly phrased ideas will come through with nicety of transcription? Is Mr. X so pressed for moments that he will want on the instant a telephone number, a letter from the files, the name of the man who came to the office last week about the incinerator? Or is the nature of his work such that he gives a variety of instructions at one time, which may be carried out as soon as convenient"?

In other words, while we are regarding the "individual differences" of students, we cannot lose sight of the individual differences of employers. For a fair appraisal of Mary's title to enter the secretarial world, she must be graded in a large, all-round sense. But Mary should also be appraised just as Mary--as the Mary 

Hayes who will serve Mr. X admirably, though Mr. Y could not even give her a trial. The general principles of this course should be assimilated by all apprentices alike. Yet it is certain that, in order to satisfy the needs of a varied business world, Secretarial Efficiency must be allowed to mold a variety of types. 

Mr. X will value Mary for being "cautious" and "careful," whereas Mr. Y cannot hire Mary because for his particular work he finds her "dilatory, and "overconscientious. is for this reason that the training in adaptability becomes the serious object of the schoolroom. What Mary "has in her" to use on demand is the gauge of grading. How will she stand up under the daily exactions in the office of either a Mr. X or a Mr. Y?

The diversity of your students must pass muster not by a single unvarying standard, but as viewed against a background of the diversity of employers. 11 It 

15. Student Teachers and Personality Traits 

The full discussion of personality given to the apprentices in their textbook does not include an important factor in their training, namely, the personality of their instructors. 19 the whole secretarial list that cannot be the better learned by the apprentice if it is resident in the teacher-employer who is before her, day in and day out. The teacher who adds to skilled instruction skilled personality gives students space to move around in. All these secretarial traits" about which so much is rightly said combine as richly with the classroom arts when displayed by the teacher as they do with office routine when practiced by the secretary. 11 Students who are preparing to teach
