16. How to Protect Your Students from Exploitation by Outside Work 

As you carry forward this course, you will doubtless echo the teacher's complaint: "Work demanded by the school offices never gives out, but we know that it passes beyond any instructional purpose for the given student. After a while, she is not learning any more. She needs variety."

In order to provide both extensive and intensive secretarial experience, this course has been planned as a continuous whole, with the expectation that successive class hours will be shared by all students. 

This brings before us the difficult question of work for the individual supplied by members of the school staff and by outside people or organizations--a question that each teacher has to meet personally on her own ground, relative to the needs of her students and to the needs of these various "causes" that lie in wait to "train" her students for her. The student must be given the primary consideration, if she is to profit by the cumulative force of instruction during her apprenticeship. 

If Elizabeth Brown is particularly apt at putting through work provided by members of the staff or in picking up private paid work, special care must be taken to avoid excessive demands on her time. 

Elizabeth's indispensable last months of school must not be sacrificed to however

INTRODUCTION 

good a cause. Such work should never be allowed to interfere with the consistent progress of her studies. Work not in the course of study should be done, if at all, during hours that will not conflict with the regular classwork, unless it is like the work being done in class. In any case, it should be strictly supervised as part of the "office experience" of the apprentice.

Work for the various officials of the school should be carefully cleared through one teacher on the staff, who is firm about meeting time-consuming demands. The supervisor of such a clearinghouse will in turn find out from other teachers which students, if any, are available and at what time, without imposing on their rights. 17. The Contract Plan Questioned 

The centralizing teacher will realize that whatever details of instruction or discussion or dictation or transcription Elizabeth misses today in class cannot be made up to her in undirected outside work, matter how "practical" and "real" the outside experience of itself may promise to be. fronting her in her first position? 

Outside work that does not interfere with the student's classwork may thus be integrated with the course to advantage. 

As Elizabeth's teacher, however--the teacher who will all too soon have to recommend her for a money-paying position--you have a right to protect her from being stolen from your well-defined classwork with its carefully related homework. And for the sake of the remainder of the class you have also a right to protect yourself from having Elizabeth ignorant of important consecutive discussion, which she should not be called upon to sacrifice. no Whenever such work must be done, the student should be directed to watch situations, to bring back to class matters of pertinent interest. What has led up to the letter that Mr. Carr dictated? What will the recipient probably do about this letter? This does not mean that Mr. Carr's personal or school affairs should be reported to the class, but in this outside work the student must stay conscious of the fact that here she is handling Links in the Chains of actual life and is touching into action the principles of Secretarial 

Efficiency. If Elizabeth Brown is asked to arrange the school catalogs alphabetically in the office of the dean of your school or college, your concern should not be for the orderliness of the dean's office. Your inquiries should be: Did Elizabeth use good judgment in applying the principles of Secretarial Efficiency to this concrete task? Can she tell the rest of the class what she learned about handling this definite problem without waste of motion so that she could now file a series of bulletins in an office more swiftly because of this experience? If she took dictation from a department head, what difficulties did either he or Elizabeth find with her work? How can she study to prevent these very difficulties from con- This matter of outside work leads to a brief word regarding the method of the contract plan for secretarial-practice courses. Though special projects have their distinct value for the entire class and for individuals who are capable of extra or advanced work, independent work cannot be used exclusively in the effective development of vital secretarial experience. Daily instruction and guidance are essential. Pupils cannot be expected to practice in absolute conformity with essential techniques without some supervision. Hence the teacher's presence during practice is quite necessary. However, as soon as correct habits have been formed, a greater degree of independence should be permitted the pupils, and to a certain extent the contract plan may be put into operation at this point. In general, it may be adopted as a principle that, in teaching a subject, or a part of a subject, which has more to do with training people to do things than it has with imparting knowledge, the contract plan is not acceptable as an exclusive method of instruction. 

The "Experience Manual for Secretarial Ef ficiency" demands independent thinking on the part of the apprentice, but training on the prolonged contract plan would form an advanced course for experienced secretaries toward the more important positions as highgrade private secretaries. Responsibility must be thrown on our beginners, but it must be supervised responsibility. 

18. Materials and Equipment for Secretarial 

Efficiency The materials and equipment listed

INTRODUCTION 

21 The ap- under A and B below are desirable and for the most part necessary for profitable experience in this course. Secretarial practice demands secretarial tools. prentice cannot learn to use these tools theoretically; she must acquire ease in handling the things that are the most common servants of secretarial procedure, such as carbon paper of different weights and envelopes of various sizes. The apprentice who fortunately goes into the well-equipped office, where supplies and mechanical tools are adequate in both quantity and quality, should be prepared to use these naturally and intelligently for efficient work. apprentice who enters an office where sparing purchases are made should be so familiar with standard materials and equipment that she is ingenious in making supplies and tools of less adequate quantity and quality serve her work efficiently. The

Inferior materials are a brake on progress in school as well as in the office. For instance, it is poor economy not to use adequate paper, shorthand notebooks, fountain pens, and typewriters for the work in hand.

Paper. A reasonably good bond paper should be used for as much of the work as the budget of school or student can permit. The reason for this is economy of both time and instruction. (a) A neat erasure cannot be made on cheap paper, and therefore such corrections cannot be fairly judged by the teacher. (b) Typewriter ribbons must be changed more frequently for legibility on a soft surface. 

Shorthand notebooks. 11 A cheap quality that scuffs up" is poor economy toward (a) fostering speed of writing shorthand, (b) allowing attention to content of dictation while taking notes, and (c) preserving the pen point. 

Fountain pen. In the long run a fountain pen is more economical for dictation than a pencil. At the start of this course, emphasis should be placed on the desirability of owning a good pen. Confusion regarding mislaid pens can be avoided if you advise each apprentice to write her name on adhesive tape and attach this to the screw top. Teach habits of regularity in filling pens (preferably before school hours begin). With good care a pen should last for several years. 

Typewriters. It is valuable for the apprentice to be required to adapt herself to a wide variety of typewriters, as to make and condition. There is not the least certainty that a first position will offer her a typewriter of the make she has used most in school or that her typewriter will be in perfect condition. Brief experience with, or at least demonstration of, various makes should be given. The secretary should be ready to handle:

Standard or portable machines, with different carriage widths

Machines of different models in a single make, including somewhat outdated models as well as the latest 

Noiseless machines 

Varied devices, such as bail and paper fingers and left- and right-hand carriage levers 

Different ways of putting on ribbons for different machines 

In her study of Chapter 17, "Efficient 

Equipment as an Accelerator," the apprentice is expected to become thoroughly informed about such matters as the use of the red and black ribbon, the brass roller, and different carriage widths. 

Experience in using inferior typewriters develops adaptability and helps to prevent confusion later, in a first position, in the face of strange equipment. For the bulk of work in the course, however, the best possible machines should be provided so that the tool itself is forgotten and the apprentice may concentrate fully on the work in hand, as she must do when she has become familiar with "her" machine in an office. Typewriter ribbons should be changed as often as is necessary so that a consistent standard of appearance of work is experienced.

Note. Accessibility of Supplies and Guarding against Waste. In so far as the school provides materials for the use of the entire staff of apprentices, the easy accessibility of supplies is very important to the progress of the work. But pupils should understand from the beginning of their training that supplies are to be economically used. Wasteful practices should be carefully guarded against, not only as a necessary economy to the school or to the student, but also as a pertinent part of secretarial efficiency.

GUIDE AND KEY TO EXPERIENCE MANUAL UNIT I. 

THE SECRETARY IS PAID FOR EFFICIENCY AS A WHOLE SECTION 1. THE PERFECT SECRETARY Key to Vocabulary Experience 1 

From 

Because of the employer's demand that the secretary shall have an intelligent command of words, this Experience purposely makes word study the cornerstone of the course. In assigning Vocabulary Experience 1, state that the rough draft is. to be handed in for your inspection. this you will learn how your class in general and the individual in particular is going about the vocabulary study, for this will be the method of preparing to type lists throughout the course. Orderly thinking should show even in the rough draft, if the final copy is to be smooth. 

Secretarial Experience 2-6, in Section 2 of the Manual, will instruct the student about typing this rough draft of Vocabulary Experience l as returned and approved by you.

In that Experience a definite standard is given to the student with regard to vocabulary work; the principle that vocabulary building is a gradual process--that the dependable growth is the steady growth--is thus emphasized. Since a secretary cannot hold a position unless she can handle words correctly as to spelling, meaning, and grammar, we must assign a generous place to word study in daily work.

The vocabulary lists are chosen from the respective chapters of the text and include only such words as might naturally be dictated by any employer who makes an ordinarily careful use of English. Since we recognize that it is far more difficult to transcribe a strange word from its phonetic shorthand outline than it is to understand that same strange word when it is before the eye in a printed sentence, we are under obligation to introduce the apprentice to as many words as possible, by varied devices. Teachers in other departments may be glad to provide lists of "word difficulties" every few weeks; these are certain to be pertinent to the needs of the apprentice secretary. Vocabulary opportunities should be given through dictation drills based on the text itself. The customary practice of giving dictation in specialized vocabularies, such as those of banking, insurance, law, medicine, and special manufacturing, is also excellent, especially with respect to local industries, which absorb many of your graduates. But the apprentice should be urged to realize that, quite apart from these special words with which she is bound to become familiar in whatever line she enters, it is true that every profession or business uses a common, a basic language, known simply as "good English. It is for us who know this to safeguard the student from finding words a stumbling block to able work. 

The secretary has three vocabularies: the one that she herself uses in common speech, which she should try constantly to increase; the still wider one that she knows how to use, though she seldom takes occasion to speak the words; and the one that comes to her with a familiar sound from others. This last group of words she knows well enough to spell (or at least to look up in a dictionary) and to transcribe in their correct sense. The apprentice must have a secure vocabulary footing when she steps from transcription that is corrected in school to transcription that must be correct of itself in the office. 

The following suggestions are given for your help in handling word work for 

Chapter 1 in class. These methods are here specifically applied to a single chapter to indicate how they may be applied throughout the course according to your judgment. Vocabulary drill should not be treated like the spelling lessons of previous schooling; elasticity of treatment will call out the trait of adaptability to different methods of approaching similar pieces of work. Frequent reviews should be given, based on chapters already covered and including such 24

SECTION 

25 other words as your own class is frequently misspelling in transcription.

Method 1. Dictation for transcription in columns. Dictate isolated words for transcription in two or more columns. This will give excellent experience in reading single shorthand words into single typewritten words, training the student in deciphering difficult outlines when the context fails to make them clear. This requires both accuracy and imagination--two desired secretarial traits. 

Method 2. Dictation for meaning as well as spelling. Dictate a short list of words, asking to have each one transcribed in a sentence. This is a good method for review to test the progressive growth of a working vocabulary. 

Method 3. Dictation direct to the typewriter. When it is feasible, dictate single words to the typewriter and whole sentences in which difficult words appear.

This will help to develop correct spelling for direct dictation, which some employers give with more or less frequency; such typing requires poise on the part of the secretary, when difficult spelling cannot be looked up. 

This method simulates dictaphone work, although the record cannot be "turned back."

Method 4. Dictation with words from the vocabulary lists used in sentences. Intensive dictation of a few sentences may be made to include many words that have been studied. For instance, such sentences as the following may be devised from Vocabulary List 1: In composing letters the secretary unconsciously reflects the style of the correspondence she receives. The employee must effectively conceal confidential matters from her associates. 

Students who are able to handle extra work may be asked to construct intensive dictation of this kind from the vocabulary of the text. This may then be dictated to the class, which is at liberty to question the usage or the pronunciation of any words. This discussion should take place immediately, directly from the shorthand notes as they have been taken. This gives experience in paying attention to what is being dictated and also in acquiring familiarity with shorthand as a usable basis for discussion without transcription. 

The construction of these sentences requires imagination as well as an exact knowledge of the various meanings of a given word. 

Method 5. 

Dictation for pronunciation.

The so-called "diacritical" marks in the dictionary are often difficult for the average student to understand or at least to understand quickly. Help should be given to your class until they can distinguish the ordinary marks intelligently for their own personal study. Otherwise, looking up words for pronunciation fails. Accents should be caught at first glance, and class drill can train your apprentices in this necessary aspect of dictionary use. When dictating words from the Vocabulary Helps, ask from time to time to have the proper accent of a difficult word indicated in the student's transcription. Let the students themselves dictate from the lists with the expectation that the class will call attention to errors in pronunciation. Emphasize that shoddy pronunciation means doubtful spelling and also a poor impression given to employers and to others with whom the secretary must talk. 

Method 6. Drill for related words. Sentences may be formed to emphasize knowledge of related words in different forms:

The secretary must do effective work hour after hour. phone.

She must speak effectively over the tele- She must make her share in a transaction effectual toward a desired outcome. 

A weak vocabulary makes for ineffectual correspondence.

Method 7. Study of notable word difficul ties. Questions of spelling, confounded meanings, and so forth, such as are taken up in the better spelling books, should be brought into classwork in connection with actual words that appear. For instance, in 

Chapter 1 we have emergencies, bringing up the question of forming plurals from words ending in y. When the word imply or implication occurs, discuss infer and inference. 

Such discussion of words that are actually being used brings to life the necessary theoretical discussion of the spelling book or book of English composition.

UNIT I 

Method 8. Word drill transcribed on small pieces of paper. This is one of the opportunities for use of small pieces of paper in the typewriter. The student becomes thoroughly accustomed to inserting fullsize or half-size sheets readily into the machine. But there are many occasions for the use of odd sizes of cards and paper, which also must be readily handled, including paper for brief memorandums, small printed blanks, and small labels. Training should be given by requiring the transcription of dictated word lists, to be handed in with the name of the student and the date on such paper as comes in pads 7" x 4" or even as small as 3" x 5", in single space, thus: 

Laura Brown--5/4/39 interrupt procedure unique acquaintance etc.

Dictation of a sufficiently long list will give practice in not allowing the paper to slip as the typing approaches the lower edge. Show the class the most efficient way to insert small paper and hold it firm. 

This skill must be thoroughly learned. 

You will of course devise other ways of testing vocabulary attainments from your recognition of the part that the intelligent use of words plays in the secretary's activities. 

See Teaching Use of the Test-yourself Questions, Introduction, p. 10. 

1. In order to obtain a position, to keep a position, or to earn promotion, favorable personality traits are everywhere acknowledged to be more important to the secretary than specific skills. In so far as fortunate traits are missing or unfortunate traits are present in a student, her earning power will be affected and your recommendation of her for an initial position must carry corresponding reservations. 

At the start of the study of Secretarial 

Efficiency the importance of personality traits should be clearly emphasized so that the apprentice will immediately face the practical need of matching her skillful handling of herself to her skillful handling of shorthand or typewriting or filing. Discussion of traits in certain motion-picture stars or in characters in fiction or real life should stir the imagination of the apprentice to the trait requirements of the secretary. 

2. The "perfect secretary" can well be thought of as a desirable model, for she is attractive in personality as well as able in her work. 

3. Would the apprentice prefer to get along with other people or to be a hermit? Secretarial work brings both enjoyable and difficult association with other people. 

4. Answers here will help acquaint you with the best avenues of approach to the immediate interest of your class. If several in the class show special interest in the same topic, choose a bit from that part of the text for swift straight dictation in order to hold whatever enthusiasm has already been aroused. 

5. Drill in using the Index should make the student feel at home with the index principle so that as a secretary she will naturally turn to its aid. As the contents of this book become increasingly familiar to your apprentices, they should come to appreciate how an index acts as a guide to known material, and from that they should be led to an appreciation of its importance in the finding of unknown material. 

6. Analyze the general arrangement of material in some other book that the class has at hand, bringing out the way to grasp the contents as a whole and the best method of finding detailed information in that book. Give the feeling of the usefulness of books, if they are handled intelligently, for this is important in office work. 

7. Some of the commonplace duties of school that will carry over to the office are: sharpening pencils, getting to work on time, following instructions.

27 Discuss the questions with the office in mind. 

8. This is purposely given as a separate question, for the student must never overlook the essential part that good 

English plays. 

9. Let the apprentice consider that secretarial work makes wide demands, each one of which is "specialized" in itself. Be 10. An apprentice at the board may put skills, understanding, and traits in three columns, or this may be turned into an exercise for transcription, by expecting the students to take notes on the discussion. The principle of integration should be made to seem not difficult, but natural--a bringing together of what the apprentice at this stage of her education has to offer to a realistic situation. sure that the more apparent as well as the less apparent necessities for the secretary are brought out clearly at the start of this course: spelling, accurate typing, punctuation, and some of the commonplace traits, such as neatness, patience, industry, interest. Suggest that the secretary can verify this name and address, since it is in her own city, and may find that the road is Weston, rather than West. On the part of the secretary this calls for intelligence, "caring about" her work, and desire for accuracy. The analysis should carry through to getting the letter signed and mailed. 

11. In some instances, bring out the trait as of first importance. 

A few suggestions: (1) Hearing bell, though in the midst of task; swift, accurate shorthand; courtesy; quick finding of address; distinct, low telephone voice. (2) Poise; ready reading of shorthand notes under stress; spelling, so that delay is not made to look up words; accurate typing to prevent corrections on so many carbon copies; quick but careful reading over of letter before removing it from machine; courteous interruption of employer for signature to rush letter (possibly). (3) Willingness to be interrupted for more dictation, instead of having regular work period as during study of shorthand in school; quick response to wish of employer; orderliness so that notebook and pen are taken up without waste of motion. (4) Careful filing of a year ago, permitting ready finding now--involving knowledge of alphabet, close attention to work when filing; quick use of outslip to replace letter taken out; experience in saving motions. (5) Ability to push through a long piece of work while carrying along other work; prompt completion of work due at a special time; willingness to stay overtime (possibly); concentration; quick, accurate typewriting. (6) Courtesy; understanding of people; resourcefulness; quick return to work in hand after such an interruption. (7) Alertness; interest; sense of responsibility. (8) Cooperation; patience; intelligence about what is going on in the office. (9) Dependability; care in writing appointments on desk pad and then following them up. (10) Keeping up with work; industry; knowledge of filing and filing quickly. (11) Asking questions at the right time; interest in keeping track of small matters that keep the threads tied together. (12) Intelligence about giving employer necessary information without waste of words; memory; reliability. 

12. Class discussion of this should stir the ambition of the students to concentrate on the skills and traits in which they are weakest. Use a light touch, helping the students to deal with small matters close at home. For instance, why does 

Marion Jaynes feel that she will need to work hard on the matter of following instructions--where does she remember falling down on this? Did she forget the most important of all the errands she was sent to do? ("Who hasn't done that?") How could she have prevented that? Did she write a letter twice, wasting time and paper? Has any apprentice been in a car that ran out of gas at some difficult point in a trip? 

What would have prevented that? Was a student very awkward the first time she changed a typewriter ribbon? Why? Recall first knitting, first cooking, first work on a committee--or any near-at-hand matters that required practice, co-operation, foresight as to supplies, keeping things where they belonged. The objective in this course is to emphasize secretarial habits of working, of thinking, of behaving in essential ways.

UNIT I 

13. Discussion here offers a chance to bring out once more the necessity of being an "all-round" able person for secretarial work as opposed to the specialized office worker. 

14. Stress the never-ending process of maturing and the mature approach to work. 

What makes the first-year high school student seem "green"? This course is framed to keep the stenographer from seeming "green" in her first position. 

15. (1) The gardener may have skill and understanding about the mowing of lawns --but even his associate gardener recognizes that he needs his traits "with him." (2) This is the true story of a college graduate who went into a large insurance office. If you are using this course for girls with college training, tell them this. 

No matter what the educational background, the apprentice must see the point of this story. As is said elsewhere in the textbook, the secretary must be "good in everything" or else go into the class of specialized workers in some one line where she "is good." 

16. Bring this right into the schoolroom with: What was the homework for today? 

Who will volunteer to do some task you mention which you would like undertaken helpfully? Why must the name of the apprentice appear on all her written work? When should corrected work be handed in? Which typewriter do you like best, from your present experience? Where is the large wall calendar kept in the classroom? 

17. The words underlying principles apply to familiar and unfamiliar tasks, but the unfamiliar task requires the secretary to discover for herself what principles of procedure are needed and why. 

18. Suggested illustrations of seven principles in practice: (1) A piece of straight dictation may wait for transcription, if not due until next week, giving way to the transcription of more immediately pressing notes. (2) Answers to questions in written tests must be clear, showing careful thinking and not reliance on obscure bluffing. (3) Instructions from the teacher-employer should be taken in writing. (4) Shorthand notebook dated daily at first dictation of the day. (5) Every dictated letter must be transcribed and canceled from the notebook. (6) Order in school desk. (7) If apprentice handles class papers that have been graded by you, grades are to be kept confidential. 

(Note. 

The remaining questions refer to the introductory paragraphs of Section 1 of the Manual.) 

19. The four kinds of people to be observed are (1) the teacher-employer in demonstrations; (2) skilled secretaries, when possible; (3) other pupils; (4) the apprentice herself at work: (1) Demonstrate several small methods of doing things, and have some student repeat each one after you, to test observation. For instance, you might show the way to steady a pencil in the sharpener for least waste, or the way to remove a folder from a file and turn it immediately open on a flat surface for quick nonspilling finding, or the quickest way to change a typewriter ribbon. (2) If your school secretary is especially clever at some task, let her demonstrate it to a small group at a time. 

Ask her, or some other efficient secretary on whom you may impose for this, to think of some device or trick she has worked out or learned that may be passed on to your apprentices. At the start of the course a brief observation of someone in an actual office should be stimulating for its realism. Conversation with experienced secretaries should always be encouraged. (3) In all four of these types of observed people, warnings may be noted as well as examples. If any apprentice sees an opportunity to make a kindly, practical suggestion to another apprentice, such cooperation will be a test of the type of helpfulness that, rightly given, may make a girl an agreeable helpmate in an office. (4) The apprentice may omit to think of herself at work. If the majority do this, point out that Dorothy Warner is after all the one person whom Dorothy Warner

SECTION 

29 can (and must) observe all the time. (Make personal applications of this kind lightly and humorously. Humor in the classroom lessens tension, as it does in the office, also.) 

20. The object of this course is to turn out apprentices who can meet reasonable office requirements on the first day of employment. Emphasize the fact of "learning by doing" by comparing reading a textbook on typewriting with studying it by carrying out the exercises on the typewriter. 

21. The answers to these questions and the class discussion on the subject will give you an opportunity to discover certain individual tendencies in each apprentice. In elaborating on the goal of the course, you will learn whether the idea of service has yet found a place in the apprentice's mind. This is an appropriate place to bring out what is dealt with later in the textbook: (1) A worthy business or professional man or woman has his or her power to serve definitely increased by the help of a faithful, intelligent, reliable secretary, no matter how menial some of her tasks may appear--this is indirect service to the world. (2) A secretary who is living a useful, industrious life is happier than that same person would be in an idle existence--not wholly because she receives an independent income, but because she feels herself a part of the fabric of active civilization. Such truths as the se should be considered by the girl or boy who "wants to be a secretary." 

22. A good place to mention the rise of both young men and young women to places of high responsibility, if they prove fit. 

23. Teacher as employer, and so forth. The teacher can take the part of the employer by being serious, strict, demanding. One wishes that you might be allowed enough impatience in the schoolroom to simulate the actual situation as it is bound to appear at times! It is not in keeping with the traditions of education for the teacher to imitate the able but erratic and even "difficult" employer, whom it is often a pleasure to work for, notwithstanding. The teacher can, however, indicate errors that would have a right to annoy and irritate a busy employer. It is true that employers sometimes praise their secretaries, and this certainly can be imitated by the teacher-employer. Praise can be given for unusual show of industry or attention or imagination. Praise should be given as well to the student who does evenly good work--she will hold out on the job better than the sporadically brilliant. For most secretaries it is an exciting moment when a bit of spontaneous commendation comes from the employer. 

24. So far as possible, establish in the classroom "office" that happy mean between being reasonably helpful and allowing the "leeches" to be too dependent for their own good. Touch the imagination by asking what a school leech and an office leech would mean. That figure may make dependence seem unwholesome in a way that merely "catching the cheater" cannot do. 

In your correction of misread outlines in transcriptions during the days following discussion of this question, be oversure to justify the student's confidence in your helping her to profit by her own mistakes. 

25. Class discussion of this question should be thoughtful, especially since many of your apprentices may within a few months find themselves in a one-girl office, such as a dentist's or a small-town lawyer's or that of a small lumber concern. 

Self-reliance must be acquired to the fullest possible extent, as a protective measure for both the secretary and the employer of that secretary. 

26. The familiar words office practice show the apprentice learning through experiences. 

27. Discussion of this gives you an opportunity to talk about where supplies are to be kept and how they are to be taken or bought, as the case may be, and to give general warnings and directions you wish to give about this important aspect of secretarial efficiency. You may find it helpful to you and to the students to appoint apprentices in rotation to keep track of supplies that are shared by the class. 

The attitude toward diminishing supplies must be intelligent, as any or all of your

students may shortly have serious responsibility in this regard in an office. 

28. The choice and use of pens and pencils should be discussed from the point of view both of adequacy to the need and of economy in the long run. The pencil sharpener for use of the students should be kept in good condition so that pencils, rightly inserted, can be sharpened without waste. 

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 1-1 (Service Book IV). In order to get the apprentice into the habit of passing in work that is correct from the employer's point of view, and not to be corrected by him, detailed instructions are given to the student regarding this transcription, as a part of the program of the course to help the student avoid errors by recognizing error liabilities in advance.

Class discussion of transcription may well take up this initial secretarial attitude.

MY FIRST MORNING IN A POSITION 

by 

A Private Secretary

It was a trifle early/ when I stepped into the elevator of the Unity Building./ Several girls were entering the elevator/ and greeting the elevator man, who seemed to be named Tom./ The friendly atmosphere gave me a feeling of confidence,/ and I was glad to find/ that two of the girls/ were turning into the office of the Carson Company./ They spoke to me in a pleasant manner,/ saying that Miss Walker,/ the office supervisor,/ had asked them to look out for me./

After I had put away my things,/ I sat down in the lobby where I had waited for Mr. Carson/ on the day of that memorable interview/ when he had told me he would let me know within a few days./ I took five deep breaths/ and did my best to remain calm and confident./ I reminded myself that Mr. 

Carson had chosen me from several applicants/ and had looked up all my records carefully./ If he thought I could do the work I was about to start,/ I surely had no reason to be doubtful myself./

In a little while Miss Walker came/ and took me to the far corner of the main office,/ showing me my desk and typewriter./ She asked one of the girls to show me where to find supplies/ and then gave me a list of orders to copy, saying that I would be expected to make two carbon copies of all orders./ She told me what bells and buzzers to answer. I got out the supplies I would need,/ studied my typewriter/ to be sure I knew mechanical devices were,/ and put my pen and shorthand notebook could pick them up easily./ where all the where I

SECTION 

31 I had only started writing the orders/ when my ring came on the telephone./ a Mr. Lane/ asking for an appointment with Mr. Carson at 

10:30./ He asked me to call back if it would not be a convenient time for him to come in./ Just putting the receiver to my ear/ seemed to make my mind follow the familiar procedure, and I asked Mr. Lane the questions I would need to know/ to carry out his errand intelligently./ Then I made a typeof the message/ and went in to Mr. Carson's desk./ written memorandum 

He looked up and said, "Oh, good morning, Miss Banks." I realized then/ that there was no reason to be afraid/ and gave him the message/ as if I had been doing this for a year. When he said that the appointment was satisfactory,/ I entered it on his desk calendar/ and then went back to my own desk./ There I entered the same appointment on my calendar/ and then settled down to do the copying for Miss Walker./ 

After that/ the morning went very quickly./ I took some dictation from Mr. Carson,/ copied rough draft for Miss Walker,/ showed Mr. Lane into 

Mr. Carson's office,/ and took care of another caller./ With each duty/ I felt more at home/ and more certain that I would like the position./ I even began to think that Mr. Carson knew more about me than I did myself/ when he said that I was qualified for the work./ 

At noon/ three of the girls/ who had smiled at me when they passed my desk/ came and asked me to go to lunch with them./ They were about my age/ and seemed to enjoy the same things I did./ They were fun/ and seemed to be able to forget the office for the noon hour/ without getting noisy about it/ as some girls do./ When we came out from the tearoom,/ one of the girls explained,/ "We take ten minutes to walk around the block,/ when there is time."/ "It's good for our complexions,/ you know," said another./ "You mean it is good for our transcriptions," 

Sec. Exp. 1-2. The purpose of this Experi ence is to give an awareness that the secretary must be "good in many things" in order to carry through even one small task. 

Integration is characteristic of all living. The apprentice should see clearly for herself that much is demanded for the diversified work of the secretary besides accurate shorthand and high-speed typewriting. The written results of the Experience will cover a wide field and can be used to advantage as a basis for class discussion.

Sec. Exp. 1-3. The class discussion should said the third./ bring out the ways in which promise is shown by the secretary who can carry out such an unusual assignment well. The points listed below may help you to lead this discussion: women? 

Quickness: How long did it take her? 

Thoroughness: Did she go to enough shops? 

Judgment: Did she go to the right shops? 

Courtesy: How did she deal with the sales- 

Orderliness: Did she set all the samples together in a way to save the time of the doctor? 

Accuracy: Were the name of the store,

UNIT I 

price, and width with each sample? 

Willingness: Did she hesitate to do a kind of task that seemed to her unsecretarial? 

Following Through: Did she think to measure to see how many yards would be needed so that she indicated the total price for each kind of goods? Did she make sure later that the doctor remembered to place an order? And did she make sure that the delivery was made and that the details of the order had been carried out correctly?

We must break away early from the too prevalent idea that a secretary for the most part takes dictation, transcribes it, answers a telephone, and converses with callers. Elasticity must be cultivated as a vital part of the apprentice's attitude during her study of Secretarial Efficiency.

Secretarial Experience 2-6 in this section of the Manual. With each section give class drill in words, using one or more variations as suggested in the discussion for Vocabulary Experience 1. 

1. Pick out with the students some concrete instances in the office manual that require being read attentively and carried out faithfully. 

2. Again and again through this course, opportunity should be taken to help foster a ready spirit about taking and following instructions for difficult or apparently difficult work. "Moods" should be nipped in the bud. When you come across poor following of instructions, refer the apprentice to this chapter. 

3. See paragraphs in the textbook on How to Take Instructions. 

4. When the student really learns to do this, she saves the time of "doing over work" both before and after she passes it in. 

5. This is important material; you should thoroughly question your class on it, to find out whether they realize the function of an office manual. A few points to bring out are: the time saved the employer by giving one set of instructions for all the employees; the uniformity resulting from the same instructions being given to everyone; the convenience to the secretary in having the office manual to turn to instead of depending on notes or memory.

At the same time you discuss the need to follow an office manual exactly, make the students feel that the secretary must always call upon her good judgment in choosing how to perform some task for which there are no exact instructions or for which she is allowed a choice of methods. We should swing between the desire to have her follow instructions to the letter and the necessity for fostering independent thinking and initiative. 

6. As you will see, Secretarial Experience 2-2 in this section requires the students to copy the office manual. The mere copying should teach the details somewhat, but directed study for the understanding of the instructions should be adapted as your particular group may require. The principle of the use of an office manual should be thoroughly grasped, and the pages as copied should serve as ready reference sheets to guide regular work during the course. This office manual is intended to establish for your aid details about which many teachers say they are in doubt. The theory offered to you here is as follows: If one of the common standard practices is followed with regard to an ordinary secretarial procedure, a good regular working habit is established. 

If a given office then requires some other practice, the apprentice will have formed

SECTION 

33 the habit of following a given instruction consistently through her daily work. In either case, we shall have given her experience toward meeting the matter.

Practice in using these common procedures should be given. In so far as transcription goes, these pages summarize-for the most part, in outline form--the rules and style and practices studied in English courses about letter writing and exemplified in typewriting exercises and required transcription in shorthand courses. 

It is assumed that the student has this groundwork, which can now be integrated with the many other aspects of this final secretarial training.

A. Taking Instructions. Use parts of the office manual, such as the section about taking instructions, for brief speed drill in shorthand, if desired. We should remember, however, that speed can be whipped up at any time by the best available methods. This course is primarily intended to teach many other secretarial essentials that cannot be experienced in the mere skill courses, though these have their own importance.

B. The Date Always. Further empha sis on dating will be given in a later section, but the apprentice should immediately catch the vital necessity of dating everything. All teachers who have had varied office experience of their own will recognize the reason behind this. A doctor may find that a valuable notation regarding a case has become of little or no value because it was not dated at the time it was made.

C. The Right Form for the Right 

Purpose. All these matters of regular procedure may well be looked up by students in the textbooks about letters that you have at hand. It is the tenet of this course, however, to avoid filling time with elaboration on the many flourishes that can be given to correspondence. After all, some fastidious employer may demand that the date on his correspondence be centered under the letterhead or put block style at the left margin or written at the lower left corner of the sheet. But for uniform ease in filing and general handling the secretary should learn that the date on large and small sheets of paper should regularly appear at the upper right corner. Block style of paragraphing has increased in use until the eye of the average reader accepts it readily. Of course, it does save time to throw the carriage back to the same place for all lines of writing. Many employers still adhere to the five-space indentation of paragraphs, which simulates the paragraphing of most of the printed pages that are read. They often allow with this the block style of the lines of address. 11 11 This section C should be referred to when you are writing corrections on papers, e.g.: "See office manual re Memorandum. or "Where is your date? See office manual. Such references will prepare the student to adhere closely to directions of the kind in her actual office manual. This section is a skeleton of orders, many of which are developed in relevant places through the textbook. 

D. Set of Rules for Office Practice. 

Needless to say these rules should be watched, enforced, applied, drilled. Many of the Experiences will give practice in following these essential guides to office procedure and office behavior. 

Suggested approach to classwork with the detailed contents of the office manual: During the study of these important pages, ask the apprentices to bring to class lists of clear-cut questions that they themselves have based on the text. 

Have one or more students ask their questions of their classmates, showing them that a question cannot be framed intelligently unless intelligent study has been. made first. This method of having the class make up their own questions on the factual information in the text may be pursued from time to time during the course, especially with regard to close-packed paragraphs. This process should develop resourcefulness, imagination, attention, and lack of self-consciousness on the part of both the questioner and the answerer. The secretary must learn how to ask necessary questions of her office associates briefly and agreeably. Answers may be found in class by direct reference to the office manual, though many of the details should gradually become familiar for the practical use of each apprentice.

7. Emphasize in connection with this concrete illustration the necessity of: beginning at the bottom; observing what is going on as an understudy; willingness to take instructions; gradual growth of judgment as a requisite to a rise in position (for both men and women). Stress the necessity of honesty, accuracy, ability to keep private matters absolutely confidential for all those working in banks. In the picture should be noted: dial telephone, indirect desk light, buzzer used by man at desk behind the vice-president, large safe door, filing basket, fact that this executive in spite of having to keep up with his affairs does not have his desk strewn with work, and fact that these two men in the foreground are in no wise distracted by the bank customers or anything else that is going on about them.

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 2-1. The preparation of folders for this course is planned to give immediate experience in having right places for everything. Discuss this in class as parallel to the problem that confronts the beginning secretary in her new position: 

Where is everything? During the first days of adjustment there is a certain necessary bewilderment in getting one's bearing toward things. It is consistent with our plan for Secretarial Efficiency that we should start and continue the course with practical experience in organization--in understanding where and how things are to be kept. 

You will find that this principle of knowing where things are and why offers a natural approach to office practice, even though it may take time and patience for its thorough assimilation. Too often in secretarial training, keeping and filing have been taught toward the end of the course as a part of secretarial theory that is separate and capable of being attached to other secretarial duties like a sort of patch. Yet the teacher knows from her own office experience that they are the very beginning of orderliness and dispatch. 

So this first part of the course provides for several folders, which can for the time being serve to cover the various branches of orderliness necessary to carry on secretarial work of even the simplest nature. Definite chapters will deal further with the duties connected with what these folders are here made to stand for. The schedule of unfinished work should be watched by the teacher, and late turn-in of assignments should be traced back to see where the difficulty lay. Help your students to type and mount the labels neatly, as an essential experience. Many good stenographers in an office appear quite helpless as they fumble with little things. 

Printing may, of course, be done directly on the tab of the folder, but this Experience attempts to teach the convenience of using gummed labels.

According to the equipment and space at your disposal these folders may be kept by each student in a large extension envelope or in one of the following ways, when not in use:

Flat in the student's desk drawer Upright in a vertical filing cabinet, which all share, with the names of the students on guides alphabetically arranged 

Flat in an individual box with name label at end, to be stacked in its alphabetical order The main need is to have each apprentice feel her personal responsibility for keeping her own papers in accessible order. 

From time to time you will inspect and grade the condition of these folders, as a vital part of your individual work with the student. A great deal of the "slowness" of which employers justly complain has its root right here, and this course may lessen the occasion for this complaint from the employers for whom you are training your group. 

1. The ACTIVE folder of a student may disclose to you why work is dilatory about coming in. You may be able to help a girl who is suffering from emotional disturbances that prevent her orderly attack on current work. 

2. Instruct your students not to run to your desk with each little completed task but to hold finished work during the day, as you may wish, in the EMPLOYER folder until a group of papers can be handed in at one time, as in the office. This will help them to see the composite day's work as a whole.

SECTION 

35 

3. Matter in the FILE folder must at all times be in well-filed order. When the FILE folder becomes too full for convenience, four folders should be set up by alphabetical division, and into these will go inactive "transfer" material previous to a certain date. This experience will go far to impress on your apprentices the realistic purpose of those somewhat remote words transfer file, which should take on meaning when the students are transferring material that they themselves have typed and handled and that they really understand as to content. 

4. The PENDING folder will be definitely used in connection with the progress of Chains of correspondence, when an answer is being awaited. 

5. The SERVICE-BOOK folder is de scribed in Secretarial Experience 2-4. 

6. The SUPPLIES folder will give you a chance to make specific suggestions about care of materials, orderliness, and preparedness for work. 

This filing work may be a difficult part of the course for some of your students. It should not be hurried for the very reason that many secretarial principles are experienced in the systematic use of this practical, small-scale filing system.

Sec. Exp. 2-2 (Service Book IV). Special note to teachers. The reasons for giving this long piece of copying are three: (1) the facts of the office manual should become more memorable through copying; (2) the facts may thus be taken away from the course by the apprentice in her Service Book; and (3) long pieces of copying must be "experienced" as a vital part of the course. If, however, you are pressed for time or if ample typewriting is otherwise provided for your students, Section D (pages 33 to 34) only need be copied. This copy is used later in the course for cross reference, Secretarial Experience 18-2. The 

Experience may be used profitably in either one of the following ways: (A) Each student makes one copy of the office manual for her own Service Book. (B) Each student makes one carbon copy to enclose in the letter given under Part B below for your dictation, several important secretarial duties thus being added to the assignment. 11 

Part A. 

The Experience is planned to help the apprentice realize that secretarial typing includes more than transcribing from the shorthand notebook or the dictating machine record, as is pointed out in the chapter of the textbook, called "The Transcription Falls to You. Long pieces of intricate copying, often with many carbon copies, test endurance, and the student must be prepared to accept this kind of monotony. A spread of three or four days or even a week may be allowed for this assignment. During this time, other Experiences and assignments from the textbook will, of course, be carried forward. You may find it advisable to discover whether this assignment is really being taken care of from day to day, although later such tasks should be left wholly to the responsibility of the student as a definite part of office training. You may need to check the student's following of the instruction about using the elastic band; this is a good habit to establish, especially for books with tight bindings. 

It is true that the apprentice should be responsible for perfect copy; yet the whole office manual will become more memorable if several apprentices in succession are called on to read from the textbook so that the class may check the accuracy of their copying, especially for possible omissions. At that time, you may check the copy of the apprentice who is reading. 

Point out to the class that in an office the reading over of important documents is often done with a second person, as will be shown later in the course. For such classwork there is questionable value in exchanging papers; the secretary on the job has to catch and correct her own errors. Do not allow recopying for purposes of correction. Have all errors corrected by the student on this copy, either on the typewriter or by nice handwritten changes made in the margin. Neat correcting is a part of secretarial efficiency. Your own approval of these copies should be based on the proof the apprentice is here giving of her secretarial skill in typewriting. Help her to feel that in this assignment she is copying not as a typist in a typewriting course but as a secretary who must adapt her skill to

UNIT I 

a secretarial duty. It is in these little ways that we can help our apprentices to a mature approach to their work. 

Part B. In the Experience Manual it is suggested to the student that a carbon copy may be made. This provides you with the option of turning this Experience into a more highly integrated exercise for your apprentices by having a carbon copy of the office manual actually enclosed with the letter given below. A special use is thus made of the long carbon copy to justify trying for perfection. On the day you set for handing in this work the class checking should be done. Sec. Exp. 2-3 (Service Book II). This is typical of the way in which rules of grammar and punctuation can be integrated with the actual work of your apprentices while you are forwarding their practice of secretarial principles. This is an important single rule of punctuation that is often violated. These violations should be watched for in all transcription. The proof of the mastery of such rules is in their daily application when the student is off her guard about them. 

Class discussion of this rule will assure you that it has been understood by all. One of the most vital opportunities offered to the teacher of Secretarial Efficiency is this of integrating with daily work the practice of rules that of themselves seem dry-as-dust, if not actually offensive, to many students. They must get the "feel" of practicing the standard rules necessary, as contributing agents, to success in business, which is conducted in English. 

You may find it profitable here to dictate to the class a series of illustrations of this rule, which may be picked out in this book or elsewhere. The apprentice should here learn once and for all that it is correct to write: 

I have my hat, coat, and umbrella. 

I have no hat, coat, or umbrella.

The following, based on the text, are more complicated illustrations to be dictated: 

Her salary depends on the adequate way in which she types a letter, keeps affairs confidential, answers her buzzer, meets a new customer, and rises to every emergency.

In every secretarial activity, she finds bound together these three factors: the principle, the reason behind the principle, and the practice of that principle.

The lease, policy, bill, invoice, check, receipt, and form letter have their dates.

In the middle of a difficult piece of typewriting, she may have to attend to the telephone, a caller, dictation, or an associate's question. 

You will know what she depends upon for food, exercise, and sleep. 

It will be obvious to the teacher that this Experience may bring out illegible or careless handwriting of one or more apprentices. The school should in some way take immediate care of such cases, for good penmanship is rightly required of the secretary. 

Special note from author to teacher. You may use parts of the text itself for special study of spelling, punctuation, and paragraphing. It is well to invite the class to watch their own outside reading for errors as well as for correct usage; this will help to train the eye and the judgment in detecting errors in work handled in the office. The student may learn by warning, by example, and by her own practice of correct standards. 

Additional exercises to be followed through at any time: Keep newspapers and good periodicals on hand to give to students who can handle extra work. They should pick out samples of good or faulty punctuation, of mistakes in spelling, of words that are strangers to them (which should be studied for spelling and for meaning), of sentences that are especially clear or that are obscure, of too long paragraphs which should be broken (where?), and of incorrect use of words.

Sec. Exp. 2-4 (Service Book). This section of the Guide deals with the teacher's part in getting the Service Book under way and keeping it alive. The student's Experience 

Manual and this Guide combine to offer specific aid to the many teachers who are looking for suggestions as to practical material and setup adequate to meet the definite requirement of leading syllabuses in secretarial training that (to quote one) "a secretarial practice notebook should be prepared by each pupil."

Work for the Service Book threads the entire course, involves many interesting types of experience, and forms a regular responsibility for the apprentice. The cumulative growth of the contents of this 

Service Book has been planned in careful detail. The name Service Book avoids con fusion with the word notebook as commonly applied to the shorthand notebook, which also is a necessity to this course, and in addition gives significance to the usefulness of a permanent Service Book not only during the course but in the secretarial world later.

UNIT I 

From your own study of this Experience as outlined in the student's Manual you will see the vital importance of this element in Secretarial Efficiency. 

As you go forward with the course you will find ready in your Guide dictation and other material shaped for the Secretarial Experiences through which the apprentices prepare their pages for this Service Book. Here you find it possible to integrate many secretarial skills, duties, and traits in such a way that they will prove interesting to your apprentices and, it is believed, to you as well. The preparation of material for approved entry into the Service Book mingles in a natural way such varied secretarial requisites as the following:

Making and keeping an index 

Paging Parts by simple code system 

Keeping an office notebook for ready reference of the employer or secretary at irregular call 

Care in varying setup of typewriting to meet varied purposes

Initiative in looking for hints for vocational improvement 

Watching for and following brief notations from employer 

Quick finding of references 

Increasing of vocabulary 

Accumulating rules of grammer especially needed by the individual student 

Using punch, paste, scissors 

Keeping data up to date 

Showing patience, intelligence, judgment, alertness, industry, personal dependability

Keeping confidential matters confidential 

Following instructions 

Making corrections of various lengths in typewritten copy, without recopying (for example, inserts, interlineations, marginal additions) 

For the teacher, one of the supreme values of the Service Book lies in the certainty that it must bring to the student a gradual awareness that papers in the office really accumulate and are kept. She will be keeping a single but diversified task on her mind from day to day and week to week and will learn in the process that an employee must not "get behind in her work.' 

The Service Book should make the student conscious that secretarial work is made up of separate tasks and that each isolated task is carried through not for itself 11 alone but for the composite whole. The more clear we can make this to the apprentice, the more we shall quicken her desire to bring every secretarial skill and every secretarial trait into line with her highest ability so that she becomes the "allround" secretary, ready to handle her composite job. 

Details should be gone over unhurriedly with the entire class to make sure that all points are clearly understood and to help in starting the book in good shape. 

The study is intended as a definite "experience" in accepting and putting to use one by one what may at first seem to be complicated instructions. No graduate from this course should be dismayed by detailed orders, whether in oral or written form. 

Should be able to follow instructions"those words we see frequently in employers' lists of requirements. 11 For the help of teachers of Secretarial Efficiency, detailed discussion is here given in order to supplement fully what may have been the personal experience of one or another teacher with such notebook work in her own classes. Secretarial Experience 2-4 should be studied and discussed in class before the book is set up.

Allow the students to use their textbooks freely in class, pointing out to them that this is not a concession to the possibility of their not having fully studied the material. It is rather an opportunity for them to see how such instructions may be used for ready reference by a secretary until she gradually either (1) knows the instructions without looking them up; or (2) knows where to find a specific instruction swiftly at need. will train your apprentices in an important secretarial skill, without which much time can be wasted while the secretary wonders "how to go ahead. This skill of going ahead goes hand in hand with many traits that must be watched, including initiative, judgment, dependability, and intelligent This

39 interest about instructions. The following suggestions are offered for your aid:

A. Why a Service Book? 

The discussion here should focus on the opportunities for present experience that such a book offers and its future usefulness.

B. What Kind of Service Book? 

As is stated in the Manual, written work involving the Service Book will be so indicated either in the Experience itself or by you. Work that has met your approval for the Service Book should bear your "S.B." A rubber stamp bearing the words Service Book may be used for the sake of neatness. It will contribute to the finished appearance of the work if you choose a regular place for using the stamp and enter it evenly in line with the student's work. Everything should be done to give earnest of the value of this book. The standard of what you approve will, of course, be high. Ideally, only properly corrected material merits a place in the 

Service Book. Here is an opportunity to show the student in specific instances how corrections may be made satisfactorily for some purposes by means of interlineations, proper erasures, inserts, and so forth. 

At first, you may guide the apprentices to make sure that they immediately mark the words, Service Book, at the head of their notes when dictation is intended for the Service Book. Always the first transcription of something intended for the

Book should go on punched paper. This is an excellent opportunity to help the student out of one of the immature habits that cannot be allowed to hold over into the office, namely, transcribing and then copying the transcription before handing it in. 

This subject is treated later in the Manual, in Chapter 7 under "Right the First

Time. Here is a concrete chance for frequent application of that principle. Material should look well because the apprentice normally transcribes well, not because she can copy her copy well! 11 

Except in the part called "On My 

Own Initiative," most of the material should be prepared by all students. The mark of individuality will be and should be on the work; the books cannot be identical in appearance or in contents. The assignments and the material provided for your dictation, however, have a specific value for the secretary, who may need to refresh her mind as to certain principles after she has acquired a paying position. Therefore, whenever any member of the class is absent for a necessary reason, a volunteer should be called for to make a copy (at least a carbon copy) to help make the absentee's book complete. This may be done by the class in rotation or may be given as extra work for students who can well handle it. 

Or it may be given as a duty to successive members of the class to watch absences and either take care of making copies themselves or show executive ability in dividing this work among others. The spirit of this duty simulates the sense of responsibility that office employees feel for absentees who must "catch up" with the trend of affairs on their return, through the thoughtful aid of another employee. This duty should not be made sentimental or overaltruistic but a part of normal business necessity for the teamwork of an office.

Although it is suggested that the material gathered during the course will be found to fall naturally into one of six groups, for the particular needs of your class you may find it advisable to modify these. The material provided during the course, however, is headed for these divisions:

Part I. The Growth of Vocabulary for Secretarial Efficiency. The preparation of vocabulary material is fully outlined for the teacher in relation with Secretarial 

Experience 2-6, called "Vocabulary Experi ence for Secretarial Efficiency."

Part II. Ways of Using the English Lan guage. It is expected that the teacher will make the value of this part to the individual student an important element in teaching this course. All possible stress is laid on correct English in the textbook and Manual and in material offered in your Guide. Here is a special opportunity for work with the individual on this vital aspect. Because improvement in the use of

English depends on minute questions,

But patience and perseverance are required of both the teacher and her apprentice. these traits should find their reward in the gain made through keeping a watchful eye on the progress of this part. 

Part III. Assignments I Have Carried 

Through. In addition to Experiences and assignments numbered in the textbook and Guide for this section of the Service Book, the teacher may from time to time add definite assignments to fit the needs of a particular group of students. For instance: 

Make a two-page résumé of the first divi sion of Section 

Copy the numbered list beginning on page List the traits mentioned on pages and arrange them in alphabetical order. to

In other words, assignments can be frequently devised with two purposes in mind for your group: (1) emphasizing the meaning of a part of the text by making a different approach to the study; and (2) using the text as a basis for integrating such secretarial skills and understanding as are required for the making of a résumé; the quick and careful copying of a numbered list; the picking out, on certain printed pages, of one factor, such as the traits mentioned above. If you find your class to be especially deficient in any secretarial approach to work, you can readily mold assignments for drill in that particular approach. Some students will be found to be undertrained in condensing material into brief outline form; others, in careful checking or reading over of copy; others, in paying attention to detailed instructions. Assignments can be so given, also, as to require reassembling of facts from a fresh point of view--a task that requires intelligence and independent thinking. It will be noticed, too, that whatever is entered in the Service Book gives just that much more experience in keeping an index of such a notebook.

Part IV. Reflections of Secretarial Exper ience. Material provided for this part differs from that for Part III. In Part III will be specific instructions and information for the student. In Part IV will be the more inspirational material of value in rounding out the apprentice's understanding of her chosen vocation. In this Guide there is some material for dictation that is intended for Part IV of the Service Book. 

It will be expected that the student will assimilate material thus dictated so that notebook entries are to a definite extent part of the student's knowledge. From time to time, tests of this assimilation should be given by referring to a certain article that has been transcribed a while before and asking a definite question, just as an employer will say, "Do you remember what I dictated in that report about the fire escapes?" The trustworthy memory catches and retains important facts so that the secretary supplements the memory of her employer at his moment of need.

This part will include not only matter dictated by you or given by you to students to dictate to the class, but also the transcription of notes taken at the school assemblies and on all lectures from people whom you may call in for inspirational or practical talks, such as businessmen or women, secretaries, the school nurse, home economics teacher (on diet), and physical director (on exercise, sleep). Your personal record of the results of such lectures as are noted for the Service Books will be an excellent guide to you for future lectures. In succeeding years, for instance, you may say to a certain doctor, "I wish you might speak to my students again this year. I could tell by their notebooks that you had a way of getting over important points about sleep and recreation and eating the proper lunch." And to an office manager, "I think your discussion of the importance of small details will be particularly valuable for my students this year. I don't know when I've had a group that so persistently slights small matters." The

Part V. Collection of Handy Hints. 

instructions for the student are given in detail and make the objective of this part obvious enough to the teacher. Handy Hints will be found in the textbook and Manual and in outside reading of business or secretarial periodicals and books. If you can inspire your class to a wholesome interchange of ideas, you will be giving them a foretaste of the esprit de corps of the well-managed office. Let the class

SECTION 

41 understand that sometimes an apparently insignificant Handy Hint will save a secretary many minutes and much effort in a week's time. For instance: filling the fountain pen at a regular time daily; using one special color of paper for rush memorandums; folding a strip over the top of a setup of several manifold sheets with carbon paper to ensure even insertion into the typewriter. You yourself may have collected some especially interesting items for this part. 

Reserve a time each week for the apprentices to dictate their hints. Let this be a friendly, informal exchange, and use the competitive motivation of keeping a list on the bulletin board, showing the hint scores of the members of the class.

This should set them hunting for hints from printed articles and from people who are employers or secretaries. No spurious or irrelevant hints should be allowed; the general good judgment of the class should not be sacrificed to the spirit of competition. Toward the end of the course, all students, or those needing extra work, may be expected to list the five hints they believe will be most useful, and those they have already tried out with success. Keep magazines and business reference books about, or have them on a reserved shelf for your class in the school library, in order to stimulate the hunt. Old magazines, as well as the current issues, sometimes hold valuable Handy Hints.

Part VI. On My Own Initiative. The teach er will note the various elements of integration for secretarial experience opened to the student here. This part should be an outlet in particular for the student who keeps ahead of the majority of the class in her work. She will be the girl who can put through more work than others in the class; she will therefore be the girl who can put through more work in the office, and opportunity should be given her to let her ingenuity and her interest out to the full in this personal section of her own Service Book. Be sure that in copying matter for this part the students realize that printed errors in spelling, punctuation, or grammar should be corrected. 

The direction of Service-Book work will vary with different teachers according to the needs of their classes and the time Al- spread allowed for the entire course. ways it should be remembered that whatever can be studied, discussed, dictated, transcribed, or copied for this book will, by virtue of that very activity, be the more usefully impressed on the memory of the apprentice. 

C. How the Service Book Is Made 

The cover of the book--in fact, the whole book--should be "simple and businesslike." This will give experience in economy with supplies--an important lesson for the apprentice. And it will also eliminate the temptation to make this a fancy affair. 

In typewriting courses the decorative uses of various symbols for framing covers of reports, setting off titles, and so on, may give facility with the machine itself, but the average secretary is too busy to go into art work in her typing. Let the details of this Service Book always further grooming for business use. Although regular punched paper is used in general for the Service Book, it is still desirable to give experience here in punching some paper. 

The very fact that the holes must align correctly with the perfectly punched sheets should be an incentive to exact work. This is a minor skill, but a skill nevertheless. 

If you have no punch that is adequate, one may be borrowed from time to time from the school office, but the care of such equipment should be stressed as a part of the teaching of Secretarial Efficiency. As suggested in the Manual, colored paper may be used for one section, perhaps Part II, in order to enhance the study of grammar somewhat, or for Part V, in order to make the Handy Hints stand out for ready use.

The details of handling the index may seem confusing to the student at first. 

This gives the teacher a supreme opportunity. It is in the confusion of orders and all the newness of a first position that the beginning secretary should find full poise and a clear head. Let her foretaste possible confusion, but show her quietly how to resolve a novel task into its parts so that procedure may be calm and intelligent. In this course, we must force the apprentice to outgrow the feeling that is shown by a 'green girl" when she exclaims in the office, "We never did that in school," "1

UNIT I 

or "We didn't do it that way in school, or "I've never done anything like that before."

Help your students to be fearless in studying and carrying through the instructions regarding this index, for the directions are straightforward and essential to the work.

D. When to Work on the Service Book 

You will doubtless find it convenient to have for your own use a check list of the assignments and other material offered for the Service Book in both the Manual and the Guide. When you enter on your own list the date an assignment is due, always be sure that this due date is made clear to the student. It is valuable throughout this course to allow some work to be fitted into crevices of time over the spread of several days or a week. We are stressing two truths at once: (1) Transcription and other work must be kept up to time, and yet (2) there are always tasks that a secretary may slightly delay in favor of the pressure of successive days' work. It is for the teacher to help the apprentice by all means to plan work so that "first things come first" and nothing hangs undone beyond a reasonable due date.

E. How Much Is a Service Book Worth? 

How to discuss this part will be obvious to the teacher. We have told the student that her Service Book is what she makes it. That is true. But it is also what the teacher helps her to make it. You may find it stimulating to discuss this aspect of the course with other teachers who are using Secretarial Efficiency. An exchange of ideas and material and a discussion of the difficulties and the triumphs of your different groups will make your work in this regard more interesting both to you and to your pupils. One of the final Experiences in the course gives as an assignment the weeding out of the Service Book, so that only matter of permanent worth is left.
