Check these points with the class before they hand in their books for your inspection. Help the student to have the feeling that a secretary has when she has just set up a new file that awaits the material she will be putting into it from day. to day. There is a pleasant sense of proprietorship and anticipation in such a task, if the secretary will "only look at it that way.

The Teacher's Master Service Book 

In your own Service Book, you will accumulate the master copies for correcting the students' pages and your own transcriptions of lectures and notes on secretarial practices. You may also have material that you find here and there for special dictation or other use. It will be the Master 

Service Book for this course as applied to your type of students and the kinds of positions for which they are likely to qualify.

The various parts of this Service Book of yours may gradually expand into a most useful assistant to your teaching as follows: 

Part I. The Growth of Vocabulary for Sec retarial Efficiency. For extra work for. advanced students, have the vocabulary lists copied for you from the textbook, so that you will have them for ready use in looking through the students' books or through a single assignment that has been handed in. 

You will find it helpful to fill in definitions for your own guide. This definition work is necessary for the student, but plainly a difficult task, because of varied shades of meaning for a given word. 

It is, however, required by many syllabuses--and rightly so. If you have a genuine interest in words yourself, your class is fortunate, for such an attitude is somehow contagious, passing from teacher to student.

Part II. Ways of Using the English Lan guage. Instead of being a list of errors you yourself sometimes make, this should be

SECTION 

43 a compilation of the most frequent errors in grammar, made by all the students with whom you have come in contact, together with the rules for correcting these errors.

The rules and examples of correct usage may be given to every class as a basis for the individual lists that each student will make.

Part III. 

Assignments I Have Carried Through. This part of the Master Service 

Book will contain your transcriptions of assignments given to the students and will be used to check their work.

Part IV. 

Reflections of Secretarial Exper ience. Here you will have your notes from lectures given by visitors and for talks you give to your students. Inspirational articles that you collect for dictation may be mounted on sheets for this section. It might also include your list of outside reading for the students and reference books you yourself use.

Part V. Collection of Handy Hints. Your collection of Handy Hints should include chiefly those that you feel your students should not fail to know about. These make good "fill-in" dictation to be spread through the course. They may be accumulated from suggestions brought in by successive classes as well as from your own experience, observation, and reading.

Part VI. On My Own Initiative. The author does not pretend to suggest what you will put in this part, but it has passed through her mind that you may have students make you copies of especially good material that they submit for this part of their own 

Service Books, thus enabling you to accumulate an excellent supply of material for dictation. A student may feel honored to put her name on something she has found which you deem worthy of putting into your

Master Service Book. 

Sec. Exp. 2-5 (Service Book III). This, as has been said, is a "test-yourself" piece of work for Secretarial Experience 2-4, A to E. In assigning this Experience, point out that nothing requires more attentive reading and understanding than reading with the purpose of condensing. The important points must be retained, but in brief form and coherent order. Limit this assignment according to your time possibilities.

Sec. Exp. 2-6 (Service Book I). The fol lowing suggestions are made for discussion: 11 

1. Emphasize that as a rule when a secretary questions whether or not a word "looks right, she should look it up in her dictionary--and at that moment learn it for good and all. Too many secretaries have to say, "I never can remember how to spell soand-so" or "I always have to look up soand-so to see whether it has two l's. Let us cultivate in the students the habit of learning a word the first time they look it up. 11 

2. Whenever possible, bring out the importance of initiative and responsibility for independent work when "no one is watching. Specific drill in looking up words in the dictionary will be given in the section called "The Alphabet Is Your Friend." 

3. Of course the vocabulary number and the date should accompany each vocabulary list as typed. Further "printed" sheets may be prepared on punched paper on the typewriter with nice ruling by the individual student or may be supplied from a project for the use of any one of your duplicating machines by different students as the course goes on. You may assign to one student the duty of watching the supply of these forms for the class as an experience in watching the diminishing supplies in an office. The heading of these further sheets may be abbreviated to "Vocabulary." 

4. Here is one of many opportunities for us to stress the necessity of understanding what work is about before going ahead and of not fearing to look up or ask questions about any doubtful matter. 

5. Discuss punctuation marks that must not be struck too hard if work is to look even; for example, the hyphen to be typed in column 2 of Work Sheet, Form 2-6. 

6. Three ideals should be reached: taking instructions, dependability in keeping data, putting work through. It is realized, however, that you will have to

UNIT I 

watch the carrying out of instructions at the start of the course. It is to be hoped that the student will shortly become wholly reliable in performing this regular duty.

If a student is lax about this while the early chapters are being studied, refer her to the pages of instructions just as an employer would refer her to an office manual of instruction.

Key to Tabulation of Vocabulary Lists from Rough Draft. Sufficient detail has been given regarding the setup of this tabulation. In preparing these vocabulary lists, you should point out to the apprentice the value of parallel columns for the graphic record of material. This Experience should serve as a specific reminder during the teaching of all future chapters. 

If you wish to fix a definite or regular time for having these vocabulary lists handed in, relative to the study of each chapter (especially during the early part of the course), the habit should be established. We must throw the responsibility for the completion of work onto the student herself as soon and as consistently as possible, as one of the most vital teachings of the Secretarial Experiences. Whenever work is not ready at your reasonable call for it, the emphasis should be laid on your role as employer and the apprentice's failure to have "your" work done, for which you are "paying" her. This is one of the telling opportunities for us to help the apprentice to look at her work through the new eyes of actual employment, rather than through the "schoolwork" eyes that have been so long used. 

Key to Correcting and Grading the 

Word Lists. The following points should be considered in going over the word lists: 

1. Typing and general arrangement. 

This should show ability to use printed forms in the machine and to make up tabulations when such forms are not provided. 

2. Care in shorthand outlines.

These lists of separate words demand exact outlines that can be quickly read out of any context. 

3. Choice of definitions. Be sure that the student is not choosing random definitions just to hurry through her work.

Guard her against choosing synonyms that do not clear the meaning of the word for her. Definitions may be compared in class from time to time to encourage the good student and stimulate the less good.

The rough draft sheets may be inspected from time to time. These will show you how the student is attacking her work --a very important part of secretarial efficiency. The rough draft should not sprawl or show incoherent, haphazard study even though it is not in its final form. 

You will also ask for the final tabulations at irregular times to be sure that the students are keeping their work up to date and to be able to show the slack students how to do this. In your class discussions, you will be able to judge the general spirit of your group toward word study in respect to the secretarial traits of patience, curiosity, and willingness.

Sec. Exp. 2-7 (Service Book V). The Handy 

Hint below is given only as a suggestion for dictation. If you prefer, use one of your own that bears directly on some immediate need of the majority of your pupils, such as the fact that papers may be piled on a slight slant toward the typist for ease in taking off a single sheet with thumb-and-finger action. You can demonstrate this by "breaking the back" of a small stack of paper and then laying it down with the overhang toward you, inviting students to come up and pick a page off --or they may practice this at their desks. 

Then have the procedure described in concise English in class discussion until the description of the activity of this hint is worded clearly and it is thus shown how to put a hint into good form. 

A full discussion of the Handy

Hints contributed by members of the class should eliminate those which are of little value, others possibly being rephrased. Until the apprentices acquire the habit of looking over their hints occasionally and really using them, the class should follow instruction 8 in their Manual in regard to using the hints. In this Experience and a later one in Section 17, hints are given for dictation from the Guide. However, it is expected that the teacher will find many opportunities to dictate others or to require the students to bring in additions to their collections, as the course develops.

SECTION 

45 

Handy Hint for Dictation. 

In a memorandum treating various topies to be taken up individually by the addressee, number each point. This helps to eliminate having paragraph overlooked by a hurried reader. 

When transcribing a reply to a memorandum so numbered, use the respective figures to make certain to yourself and the recipient that you are covering each one of his points.

SECTION 3. WATCHING THE LINKS 

1. Test the trait of imagination here; both the "Chain" and the "Link" are used as a figure of speech throughout this chapter and the entire course. This principle of the Chain in business as it affects the secretary's daily interest and intelligence about her work must be stressed until the least imaginative is thoroughly imbued with the idea. In fact, the least imaginative pupil needs most of all to see this point for she bids fair to be the least imaginative secretary and must therefore be fired with the desire to watch the relation between the many pieces of work that are "going on. 11 

2. This may touch the student to a feeling for sequence. The newspaper reader who follows serial strips knows what "happened" yesterday and today and awaits tomorrow with regularity of interest. Cause and effect are in play as in business. depends on how many invitation Links were sent for the reception. Through such natural Chains we should be able to show the apprentices that business itself is natural and not a strange, remote phenomenon. 

4. An appeal to the natural human desire to put the parts of something together into their right whole. The crossword puzzle, jigsaw puzzle, the tying of an intricate knot, making a dress from a pattern--such simple "puzzles" as these show how business is the building together of details. If we can make our apprentices "catch fire" because of this principle, they will be helped to both efficiency and patience with their part of the building. 

5. For this question, carry over the idea that the apprentice from the first hour of her new position should begin to catch up the relations of the Links she is asked to handle with their respective Chains. A positive grasp of this attitude will give an assured attack, and we should prepare in every way for the recognition of cues and an immediate interest in the patterns belonging to those cues. The para- graphs of the text, headed "If You Were an 

Employer," are related to the three homely illustrations already given and may well be discussed in connection with this question. 

6. The dependence of business on words must be emphasized repeatedly, for the secretary must be proficient in her use of the all-important word medium. This question calls for the student's independent thinking on a subject that will be taken up in a later chapter and should bring out the matter of spelling, syllabication, pronunciation, grammar, and clear, correct, forceful vocabulary. 

3. Bring out the variety of Links that the postman has the responsibility of delivering--the special delivery, bank and legal correspondence, the money order, and packages of many kinds. The delivery truck carries not only outgoing orders but goods returned for credit, which in turn make extra Links for the store to handle. The florist has an order from Mrs. James for flowers for a funeral; she will receive the 

Link of appreciation from the family and the business Link of an entry on her monthly bill. The caterer carries to the wedding what was arranged for at a conference

Link with the bride; the amount he supplies understand the business uses of the word 

7. Work out the fact that the brother had imagination enough to ask what the sister wanted, that she had the temerity to say what she wanted, and that the "order" came through on time. Test the imagination and stimulate humor a bit by a light questioning of the class as to what each has been visualizing through the text as the present she received. 

8. The apprentice should clearly

UNIT I 

11 quotation, both as "the naming of the current price of any security or commodity" and "the price named." 11 

9. An unnecessary Link may waste time for more than one person, may waste paper, room in the file, postage, and may irritate a customer or client or patient.

When passing back a set of corrected letters at about this time, stimulate the interest of the apprentices to notice how much time, patience, and paper are required to right just one piece of work. Show then that, as their employer," your time has been taken in calling their attention the errors and that a letter might have missed the outgoing afternoon mail by this kind of delay on a copy to be made correct for signature. 11 11 to 

10. Needless to say, this is an important question relative to the sentence in the text, "Sometimes the completion of a Chain becomes complicated by an error or by a misunderstanding." Many secretaries fail to recognize that what is wrong is wrong--that writing the figure $5, when it should have been $50 is wrong, no matter whether this comes from an error in addition, in shorthand, or in misunderstanding a hurried dictator. It is this fine distinction that an error is an error, no matter what the cause, that we should establish before coming to the section we are calling "Right the First Time. 11 11. Bring out the fact that in business transactions complaints must be made and answered courteously. The business will suffer if an employee considers a complaint a personal affront. 

12. Find out the ambitions of your students and how much they really know about the type of work they hope to do. Let their ideals of secretarial work soar somewhat at the beginning of the course--we shall give enough detail to bring them down to facts and their own personal possibilities as time goes on. The miscellaneous questions should lead to lively variety in discussion. Have a set of rolled blueprints at hand, if possible. If a novice secretary has never seen such blueprints, they seem formidable and awkward to handle if they come to her care in any way.

If possible, let the class practice handling a large set quickly--without fumbling. 

13. This saying is a challenge to the secretary for steady, even work. Slighting or neglectful or careless work cannot qualify either a stenographer or a secretary for a responsible position. 

14. There is no end to illustrations: in making ice cream, omission of sugar which is one of the Links in the recipe; putting flowers in a vase without water. In the homely illustrations the class may offer, bring out the necessity of a clear seeing of every part of a whole. 

15. Bring out the immediate give and take in a telephone conversation, which will be studied in the chapter about telephoning. Secretaries vary in their appreciation of the function of the telephone in their business--some are given to underusing, some to overusing it in putting through their work. 

16. The first Link of a Chain, which should command the special interest of a . secretary, as she is becoming responsible for her part in a new sequence; a midway 

Link, which may need pushing ahead vigorously; and a final Link, which should be filed with the well-ordered series of Links for that Chain. 11 

17. Without breach of confidence, the school secretary may lend you a few Links or one Chain, which you can discuss with the class to show "how it goes. If the secretary herself can spare a few moments from work, the observation on the part of the apprentice will be still closer. A folder from which a sequence is removed before the class will show clearly the interlacing of the original letterheads and the carbon answers. 

18. It is important for the apprentice to realize that, as work goes through the secretary's hands, it repeats itself so that she has no excuse for not knowing and not remembering what happens. 

19. If your apprentices have studied 

Latin, let them gather the full meaning of the word comprehend through its derivation

SECTION 

47 from the Latin word having the meaning of "to grasp together," the very point made in "Watching the Links. 13 

20. If one of your students shows special interest in acting but dislikes some of the necessary drill and repetition of this course, you may be able to stimulate her to see herself as rehearsing part she is to play until she "knows her lines."

Key to Secretarial Experiences

Sec. Exp. 3-1. This Experience should ex ercise the trait of imagination, as well as emphasize the Link principle. Elasticity of application of knowledge must be experienced in this course if the apprentice is to practice the trait of adaptability. class discussion of these lists, bring out the fact that, in school, kinds of tasks fall into a regular schedule; the student knows which kinds of recitation she must prepare for and which class to go to the first hour every morning. In the office, however, the first duty of the morning cannot be so closely planned--it may be the answering of a telephone call. Secretarial 

Efficiency prepares the student for a daily sequence of duties that are marked by irregularity of order and of nature, as distinguished from ordinary school routine. 

Set the class to thinking out how certain of their illustrations in this Experience are paralleled by secretarial activities, such as completing work that hangs over from the day before or interrupting work for emergency calls. Again, help the apprentice to break away from the erroneous notion that the secretary sits transcribing shorthand notes as the major part of her work.

Many secretaries are slow and awkward about using half sheets in their typewriters, because they have not been properly trained. Quite possibly your group should have a demonstration when you assign this Experience. Show the quickest way to shift marginal stops, bail rollers, or other devices needed in this assignment. 

Sec. Exp. 3-2. Class discussion: waste of time of more than one person when an error is made in a Chain. In this case, the housekeeper must telephone, the wrong order must be called for, and the right order found or else put up in duplicate and delivered. Let the class exaggerate the number of Links even to a humorous degree, if that will help point out the importance of one wrong Link. The error may have been due to faulty handwriting of the person taking the order. Attention and interest are needed by the secretary if she is to put work through without errors. A poorly transcribed letter may have to go back to her desk to be rewritten; then it goes on to the desk of her employer for a second time--thus two Links are wasted, together with the time and the patience involved.

Sec. Exp. 3-3. In discussing the textbook diagram briefly, discover first whether the apprentices followed instructions about studying this and whether they really paid attention to the essential details, showing interest and concentration. The original diagram called for in this Experience is a test of the student's grasp of the principle of the chapter. Have as many students as possible go to the board in succession, to mark out the Links of their original 

Chains roughly but clearly. Three important aspects of class criticism are, of course: (1) Has the apprentice really started her diagram with the initial Link of the Chain? (2) Has she omitted any vital Links? (3) Has she carried the Chain through its final Link? Stress the special

Links that illustrate variety, for the apprentice must recognize her own need of versatility.

Sec. Exp. 3-4 (Service Book IV). Before giving the dictation for this Experience, ask your students to turn to the Experience in their Manuals so that they may see how the title is to be set up. In asking them to find the place, refer to it by page number and request a showing of hands when the page has been found. This will give you an idea of their speed in finding pages and suggest to you further intensive drills if the average speed is slow. In connection with the title call attention to the unusual phrasing--with line to show omission of the final possibilities for the question. Then (1) leave it to your class to recall this when transcribing later or (2) if your group needs extra help for the present, call

Discuss a frequent typing error in typing capitalized lines--showing that the shift key must be dropped after the F of IF or an asterisk instead of a hyphen dash will be printed. 

Dictate the following essay in a natural way, not in the unnatural, tickedto-the-minute way, which is never used by an employer:

WHAT WILL HAPPEN IF 

? by 

A Private Secretary (P) When a woman parks her automobile/ in a commercial parking space,/ she pays twenty-five or fifty cents/ and receives a numbered check with which she later identifies her car./ At that moment, responsibilities begin/ for both the woman and the parking agent./ She can readily reclaim her car/ if each party to the responsibility/ has been faithful in every particular./ What will happen however, if she loses her check?/ What will happen if he fails to tie the other end of the check on the car?/ What will happen if the key is lost?/ What will happen if she finds that she has not/ the fifty cents with her/ when she is ready for the car again?/ What will happen if neither the agent/ nor the woman/ can make the right change for her payment?/ (P) These are all negative questions./ Putting them in the affirmative,/ we have a more cheerful outlook for the woman and the agent./ What will happen if she keeps her check where she finds it readily?/ What will happen if he ties the other end of the check/ on the car according to his regular system?/ 

What will happen if the key is not lost?/ What will happen if they make change immediately?/ (P) As a private secretary,/ I have found that work goes smoothly/ when I have kept the future in mind/ with this important little question:/ "What will happen if ---?/ After many years of experience,/ a secretary does not say these very words to herself, but she has a natural,/ inquiring attitude toward the outcome of what she is handling./ As she feels out toward the future, she is able to make choices wisely./ She is aware of what may happen/ if she does this with a certain memorandum/ or if she does that./ she puts it on her employer's desk/ for his attention,/ what will happen?/ 

If she puts it away in her file/ by mistake/ before it has been attended to in every detail,/ what will happen?/ "What," she asks herself,/ "will happen to this if I do that?/ What will happen/ if I don't do that?"/

SECTION 4. HOW MUCH ARE YOUR TRAITS WORTH? 

 1. The natural pride of both parent and pupil is often colored by an unnecessary fear of the word stenographer and a pleasant awe of the word secretary--more especially of the words private secretary.

We ourselves gather two quite different impressions from two women who speak of "my husband's stenographer" and "my husband's secretary." We are well aware, however, that a clear-cut line can never be drawn between these two words. The stenographer" who does somewhat routine work for a learned editor must far exceed in ability and background the so-called "secretary" to the coaldealer in a small town. Though it is popularly assumed that the requirements in a stenographic position are confined to the use of shorthand and of typewriting, these cannot be entirely divorced from duties of a secretarial nature. A position as a stenographer, therefore, often leads naturally to what may more properly be called a "secretarial" position. On the other hand, the so-called secretary who is acting in a small capacity may use her shorthand or her dictating machine ability without much call upon her for really responsible secretarial duties. The apprentice must be helped to see clearly here the meaning of the familiar quotation, "What's in a name?" She should begin to adjust her ambition to the possibilities of being a stenographer in a large concern, where she can learn a great deal by experience and observation and gradually fit herself to rise to the title of secretary. Or else she should be ready to become a secretary (perhaps in a one-girl office) who has to attend to the minor and major duties with even adaptability. 

2. From the study of the first four chapters of the textbook which make up Part I, the apprentice should have gathered what the definite requirements are for this vocation of hers as to skill, understanding, and traits. 

3. The matter of grading is dis cussed briefly in the introduction to this Guide. The relation of grades this year to hoped-for pay next year makes a concrete enough appeal. Bring out that your own grading of the class, apprentice by apprentice, will take into consideration such matters as have been influencing the grades for good transcriptions or good English composition or for correct spelling lessons, but now with a difference--all these necessities are integrated not only with each other but with such requirements as 

Traits shown and developed

Time taken for completing a varied job Initiative 

Following instructions

Homework 

Extra work done by superior students 

Work habits 

General progress toward becoming the "wellbalanced" secretary 

4. (1) The first type of duty is one that demands initiative and originality. Every student should be made aware that power to think is essential to this vocation and that such power can be gained and improved only by exercise. Thinking out an original way to coax the cat into

SECTION 

51 the house or to make over a dress or to earn pin money or to write an invitation to a party-- these are matters of initiative and originality. Put these on the board, if you wish, and opposite them write matters that are roughly parallel in office life, e.g.: 

Coaxing the cat into the house 

Making over a dress 

Earning pin money 

Writing an invitation to a party 

Persuading a man to come to an appointment with employer instead of taking his time to go to an appointment 

Turning old folders in a file so that the reverse of tabs may be used and the folders are as good as new 

Earning savings for a concern by thrifty devices 

Writing a notice of a conference

This idea can be carried out by discussion of students' answers to this question when papers have been handed back. 

This not only will carry the point itself but will require the apprentices to exercise their thinking power. (2) The second type of duty is one that demands industry because of its tiresomeness. The home study of some subject not enjoyed by an apprentice may be mentioned here--the discussion leading to the fact that, even though this study is monotonous and uninteresting in itself, the student does carry it through, just as a secretary would. (3) The third type is one that is so small that it may be lost sight of. Here discussion should emphasize to class the intelligent, "sporting" spirit toward the tiny task. During the months of this course refer occasionally to the dignity of menial tasks--when a student groans at having to change a typewriter ribbon, or when the whole class is cleaning typewriters, or when you see ahead in some assignment you are making a menial task that you think will irk certain members of your class. The apprentice should learn that the haughty secretary "rides for a fall." 

5. The most obvious traits that lead to a desirable personality for a secretary may be discussed. 

6. The study of this question may be tested by a quick written quiz at the typewriter, asking for the naming of the twenty-eight traits. Let this quiz integrate not only the use of the memory but a ready setup directly on the machine--the first copy to be handed in as the final copy. The number of minutes taken for this simple quiz should be recorded on each paper; an account of speed in recollection and in typing what is recalled is an essential to your grading. 

The class discussion of traits may be kept free from a boring or sentimental or pious vein by the practical aspect suggested in the chapter title, "How Much Are 

Your Traits Worth?" The money value of traits is brought out in the text. Stressing the understanding of the definitions of the words that stand for secretarial traits is not easy for the teacher. Many traits that are commonly listed by employers overlap; many cannot be defined simply; and some, such as the word intelligence, of themselves include many others. The significance of these words should be taught through activity, as is suggested in the textbook itself by the concrete illustrations following each of the twenty-eight listed traits.

This list of traits gives a serious outline of the personality of the perfect secretary whom we have chosen as the leading character in the textbook. Do not allow your students to be discouraged by the array. At the same time, assure them that there must be a mixture of these traits to make an employable secretary. Again we have here the point that an "all-round" person is required. It is on the integration of the whole gamut of traits with occupational skill and understanding that your final recommendation of each apprentice must rest; the grades throughout the course will, therefore, take this into consideration. Throughout the course, in both textbook and Guide, application will be given in recurring detail in order to help you with this intangible but thoroughly important aspect of Secretarial Efficiency.

UNIT I 

11 

7. Emphasis to be placed on the way "good judgment, as demanded by employers, is the guide to the use of occupational skills. 

8. "Readiness" as a time saver for all concerned should be expected in classwork as a definite part of secretarial experience. Respect for the time of others should keep the apprentice attentive. "Don't keep us all waiting" has a familiar sound from early childhood, and the fruit of that warning should ripen now for use in the office. 

9. Prepare the student in detail for the difference that she must be ready to meet. At first, in an office, free time will seem very restricted: Saturday afternoon and Sunday and evenings--and probably two weeks' vacation out of the whole year.

Life will be much more manageable and agreeable for the secretary if she has established these well-regulated habits of exercise, sleep, recreation including reading, and home responsibility (if any). The apprentice may find herself gradually changing some of her recreational habits in the time she can "call her own. Wherever circumstances have kept her background meager, she must try to catch up by filling in the gaps. 11 10. During apprenticeship for secretarial efficiency, it is fair for us to give training in the balanced living that makes for a steady-going secretarial career. 

11. These subjective questions are purposely placed at the opening of the course. If any worth-while book is mentioned that you yourself have not read, bring your apprentices up into the mature circle of your living by saying spontaneously that you mean to read that soon or that you had not heard of that book but believe you would like to read it. Help the class to sort out the difference between mere popularity, desirable as it is, and having friends. 

12. Drive home this legitimate goal of the recommendation at the close of the course by having in class from your own records or those of the school office some actual written recommendations of graduates of the school, indicating as definitely as prudence allows what positions were obtained and at what pay. Make clear what part the teacher's appraisal took in the recommendation from the school. Indicate, if you can, one or more cases in which a graduate failed to get a position, probably because of lack of some trait or some skill. 

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 4-1 (Service Book IV). The in formation about traits should be made more memorable as a whole by this straight copying, which also serves at this point in the course as a test of straight-copying speed.

The instruction as to margins is given for the purpose of experience in intelligent elasticity. The apprentice must get rid of the idea that typewriting demands absolute uniformity. She must use her imagination to realize that typewriting must always serve its given purpose in the best way, whether by putting little or, as in this case, much on a single page. 

Let a student with spare time take on the extra project of tabulating the time record of members of the class for the bulletin board. Apprentices should observe where they stand in this list and should learn that speed and accuracy are at one with each other--that erasure and replacement of a letter or word that has been written too hastily slow down over-all time more than lesser speed with accuracy throughout.

Sec. Exp. 4-2 (Service Book III). Many of the purposes of this Experience will occur at once to the experienced teacher. Though it is true, as stated to the student, that you should not remind the apprentices to make their daily entries, inspection should be made after a few days to be sure that the instructions are being followed intelligently and diligently.

The entire result will be valuable to you for informing correlation with the work that is being achieved by each apprentice and with the traits that you may find lacking in a given apprentice. For instance, a student of yours who places few F's on her sheet obviously lacks friends for some reason that may give you the key to why her personality seems unsuited to secretarial

SECTION 

53 work. The question about home responsibilities may reveal to you causes for difficulties in the homework. Home conditions may affect the apprentice now and that same apprentice later as a secretary so that she "does not get on" but does not know why.

Whatever attitudes or adjustments you can foster may meet a far more vital need of a given secretary than any amount of speeding in shorthand or typewriting or even drill in spelling.

Reports from this record regarding enjoyable reading should be stimulating within the group. If meager time is devoted to meager reading, if current events are not followed with some degree of intelligence, tests on informational background may find otherwise worthy graduates falling below the simplest secretarial level. Acquaintance with literature, general knowledge, and an interest in current events are all-important assets that lead to secretarial efficiency.

This outside-of-hours aspect of the vocation of the secretary offers intensive and rewarding study to the teacher, since it plays so vivid a part in the development of that wide-sweeping essential called by the indefinable word personality. Discussion of specific motion pictures and other recreation is relevant to this course, for the nature of play does affect the nature of work. High standards for the motion picture and reading can be developed gradually in your students if in the process a genuine enjoyment they may have for something below your own standard is not belittled. Whatever they are wholly willing to advise you or their classmates to "see" or to read deserves respect and interest from you.

Work out with your students a bulletin board to be kept solely for these leisure-time activities. For this, clippings about motion pictures, books, concerts, theaters, and sports may be brought by apprentices and by you. This will be a chance for you to make recommendations in a co-operative rather than a pedagogical way. Invite apprentices to make critical comments and sign their names when they make a recommendation. Negative as well as positive criticism should be courted--all this makes the apprentice think--and think relative to other people.

At convenient times, make conference appointments (which should be kept on the dot by the apprentices) for individual discussion of these sheets, which should be mutually helpful to you and the apprentice. 

You may also ask for summaries of time or kinds of interests shown by specific entries. These records reveal to potential secretaries the habits they now have of using free time and suggest the importance of well-balanced living.

Sec. Exp. 4-3. This amounts to a review of the trait chapter, Chapter 4 of Part I, through the application of what has been learned there. The Experience will require memory, concentration, and well-typed assembling of information. Whenever typing of this kind is required, we are training our apprentices to think about their business--an important experience in itself. 

A later unit will deal in particular with the order of work, giving special instruction and drill about planning work. 

For the present you should be careful that the due dates on long pieces of work do not come too close together. But always through the course the apprentice should be thrown on her own responsibility to get work done when and as assigned. 

If you wish to have these sheets entered in Part 

III of the Service Book, instruct the class to use punched paper for this Experience. 

Note. From now on, an Experience of this nature may be required for each chapter at the option of the teacher, the results to be put in the Service Book. But we must be cautious not to overdo the emphasis on trait study in and for itself. 

Sec. Exp. 4-4. This is one of the seemingly simple Experiences that should not be passed over lightly. Its chief value is that it integrates several skills and traits important to the apprentice. A few thoroughly integrated Experiences of this sort prepare a student for her future work far more than isolated duties that she will have to learn to integrate after she gets on the job. The actual task is not hard--a typist may turn out many such forms in a morning--but it involves principles and practices that should be at the secretary's finger tips. The principle of integration will be given cumulative force as the course planned in Secretarial Efficiency

UNIT I 

progresses, and we shall see the improvement in the apprentice's handling of various aspects within a single duty. 

This Experience gives us an opportunity to find out the apprentice's opinion of herself, which has a distinct value in secretarial training and in the actual position. Unless she knows how to make the most of her best points and to keep a sharp eye on her recognized shortcomings, she cannot fully use herself. Your appraisal and her appraisal together make a valuable record for the kind of recommendation that the school can give on her graduation. she cannot see herself fairly, she should be helped to a more judicial point of view. 

Here is your chance for individual trait work, which is all-important in these last months of schooling.

If 

The inspection report itself will indicate to you whether a girl is overconfident, conceited, overmodest, given to an inferiority complex--whether she needs commendation more than do most students until she is normally aware of her ability or whether in some points she considers herself to have as an asset a trait, such as accuracy or punctuality, which you find lacking in her. As teacher-employer you will be looking through the employer's eyes at the status of each apprentice as she progresses from month to month. The criterion for this course rests on the actual fact that:

Mary's level at the close of her appren- Mary's level at the beginning of her ticeship secretaryship

The Mary whom we graduate is the Mary who sits down at her desk the first day in a position. What will her traits find her equal to?

With relation to Item 2 of the Man ual, be sure that your students understand and follow this method of making and numbering a rough list. This is one of the timesavers that will help the secretary when her employer requires her to organize rough lists into usable form. Too few secretaries turn naturally to the use of little devices for arranging and rearranging matter. Definite instruction in this type of work will be given later in the course, but habits based on intelligent principles should be formed as we go along. 

Emphasize the last sentence under 

Item 2, telling the class that improvement in traits will be a valuable part of this course and that part of your grading will rest on growth--the growth habit carried over into the office makes a secretary continuously more valuable and therefore in line for promotion.

The detailed instructions under 

Item 3 should require no class discussion before the work is started, but this is one of the Experiences for which you may profitably give minute attention to individual students because so many principles are involved. The care with which you can check individual ability in following instructions at the beginning of the course will help you, as well as the student, while the varied work charted in Secretarial Efficiency goes forward.

When the letters have been handed in, stack them and give your own demonstration of the quickest way to open a pile of envelopes. This will prepare the student for work laid out in the chapter called "What Is in the Mail.' If your class is large, open a few letters and then ask at least one student to share in the demonstration. Watching and doing are two different matters in these minor office skills.

UNIT II. THE SECRETARY KEEPS AT WORK SECTION 5. 

DICTATION MEANS RESPONSIBILITY 

Following the study of this chapter, special attention should be given to making the situations under which dictation is given and taken true to life. The students should be given practice in taking dictation in each of the six positions listed in the textbook. For the desk-edge and knee positions, you may have one student at a time take the dictation at your desk while the others are in similarly inconvenient positions at their own desks. Interrupt the class at some other work for sudden dictation with only a moment to prepare for it. Sometimes, when a student comes to your desk on an errand, dictate a memorandum or instructions. Use every possible means of training the apprentices to keep their notebooks and pens ready for instant use. 

When giving dictation, practice any of the "caprices of dictators" listed in the textbook: turn your back, mumble, hurry, drag, change your mind, ask to have a bit read back, fail to paragraph, add a postscript to one letter while giving the next, use incorrect constructions. And besides making these variations yourself, ask other people, both men and women, to give dictation, thus preparing the students for positions in which several people dictate to one stenographer. Simulate a large office with many dictators and stenographers by dividing the class into pairs--one to dictate and the other to take shorthand notes--and by having all the dictation going on at once to prepare for this not infrequent confusion. In other words, prepare your students to meet any contingency that may arise in connection with the giving and taking of dictation.

The resulting transcriptions should be perfect, as they would have to be for an employer. However, the apprentices should be allowed to ask for redictation of a word or phrase under certain circumstances, as they would occasionally in an office. A passing fire truck, a cough on the part of the dictator, an unusual word or proper name are all legitimate causes for requesting a repetition. And the apprentices should recognize the fact that it is more intelligent to ask than to guess. 

Definite suggestions regarding dictation are given in Section 12 of the introduction to this Guide. 

1. Conferences do not distract the secretary from putting her work through, but it may be noted that Miss Kane knows what this conference is about for she has found the necessary papers in advance. Note the proper height of typewriter table, good posture, light, paper holder with shelves, filing basket, telephone, appointment pad, metal window ventilator. 

2. See the textbook, Chapter 5, under "I'm Going in for Dictation." Emphasize also the sole responsibility of the secretary as compared with dictation taken by an entire group. 

3. Discuss readiness for any demand made by the employer--resourcefulness, willingness, memory, speed. 

4. Emphasize this in actual work whenever possible so that apprentices acquire the habit of "having everything with them. 11 

5. See the textbook, Chapter 5, under "As You Go In" and "Positions for Dictation. The positions are also discussed, for the teacher, in the introduction to the

Guide; they are a good example of the necessity of "learning by doing. 

6. Stress this alert patience, which so contributes to personality, by having students try dictating aloud to someone a letter replying to a friend. The 55

UNIT II 

objective view must be gained so that the apprentice becomes sympathetic with the employer and does not consider that hers is the only difficult part of dictation. 

7. Stress the idea of dictation as a flowing secretarial activity rather than as a static duty. 

8. Pupils should be ready to call attention to quality of paper, varieties of notebooks, watching for notebooks to "run out," dating methods, labeling, keeping, holding, turning page, turning book, and "important hints" as outlined in the textbook, using the blackboard in demonstration. 

9. Awareness of the Link idea should lighten the fatigue contingent on extended dictation. 

10. The teacher can help students study their own motions in making the two hands help each other when using the shorthand notebook, handling the typewriter, filing, sorting cards, sharpening pencils, changing ribbons. Ease with both hands is essential. 

11. These five questions may be dictated by a pupil for entry in the Service

Book, in order to drill in their importance. 

12. The reality of dictation must be anticipated if the apprentice is to take up even her first day's work with intelligent interest.

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 5-1. The following pages con tain material for an intensive period of dictation to be given at one sitting even though it runs over a single class period. 

Endurance for this and even longer periods must become habitual for the apprentice, who cannot afford to break down in her first position because she "has never been used" to such sustained dictation. 

Put as much variety into your manner of dictating as you can. Include interruptions if they can be managed, and follow the suggestions for making changes in one letter after several others have been given. For your convenience in handling the transcripts, the items for dictation are numbered and the paragraphs are indicated--but these need not be dictated.

Names and addresses should be given with care, as in the actual office these would be accessible to the secretary on correspondence or elsewhere. Spaces are left for you to fill in appropriate current dates.

In correcting these letters, allow some latitude for the fact that a secretary in actual employment with this concern would be familiar with specific matters.

For example, reference to goods under #6g4-37 is for the purpose of this Experience as correct with a capital G: #6G4-37. 

Of course, each figure and letter should come through exactly as dictated, and the form of the correspondence should follow that given in the office manual. 11 The students should be clearly told that for transcription of informal interoffice memorandums abbreviation of frequently used words is not only permissible but also demanded for quantity output of work. 

For example, É for cent, yd. for yard, for inch, for per cent. Secretarial judgment must distinguish between formal and informal work. This is especially important for the secretary who goes first to a position as a substitute, where she must put through a quantity of work accurately but not necessarily with fine finish. Employers frequently complain of lack of discrimination on the part of employees in this particular. 

In the following letters given for dictation, the words underscored and set off by asterisks are instructions to be given to the student at the time of dictation. The words in parentheses are your key to notations of enclosures, etc., that should come through on the transcriptions. (P) indicates a paragraph.

The Sec. Exp. 5-2. This Experience gives you an opportunity to follow the suggestion that the conditions in a large office with many people dictating be simulated. results will show whether or not the Experience should be repeated. When you have divided the class into pairs, give two letters to each pair. Since the shorthand notes for these letters are to be passed about in the next Experience, it will be convenient if you can provide the class with paper from old notebooks on which to write their shorthand. The partner who has given the dictation may check the transcription.

Sec. Exp. 5-3. For this Experience you will need only to supervise the redistribution of the shorthand notes. The transcriptions should be made during a class period if possible. The Experience of exchanging notes should be repeated at intervals if the apprentices are unsuccessful at reading others' shorthand readily the first time. Discover whether difficulty with the notes indicates that the student who wrote them should work on her outlines or the student who reads them should train her eye. 

Analyzing the faulty work of one another may teach the students important points. If you wish, one partner may check the work of the other.

SECTION 6. THE TRANSCRIPTION FALLS TO YOU  

1. Show through the answers that transcription is an example of secretarial integration. 

2. Self-study of wasted time in its various elements can be strengthened by the teacher's individual instruction and care in diagnosing the minute causes of slow turnout in both small and large pieces of work. Over-all timing is all-important. 

Three questions in secretarial teaching must constantly be asked of the apprentice: 

What have you been doing? What was it about? How long did it take you? 

3. Reading for correctness and for knowledge of content is the required combination. Students who are swift readers in everyday life often require persistent training and practice in taking sufficient time to catch errors in grammar, spelling, and so forth. Your quick student must be made painstaking also; and your overpainstaking student must be directed out of the plodding class to gradual acceleration. 

4. Correct grooming should be applied concretely to letters as transcribed. 

In daily work, failure to regard the items listed may require reference to the textbook. 

5. Emphasize the variety of possibilities latent in the typewriter as the ready servant of the secretary--the

UNIT II 

convenience of the line spacer, the usefulness of the two platen knobs and right and left carriage releasers, and so on. 

6. "Clearing the notebook" must be practiced faithfully during training, as one of the habits of a "conscientious" secretary. 

7. The suggestions in Secretarial 

Efficiency are in general practical use; other hints regarding notebook use should be given intelligent consideration, for the apprentice should be made aware that elasticity of method, provided that method is sure in its results, is one of the marks of the "perfect secretary." Correct methods must be established now for ready use later. 

8. The interdependence of typewriting and shorthand (or stenotypy) demands "good marks" in both. A natural criticism on students' papers is: You must bring your typing up to your shorthand--or vice versa. 11 

9. Discuss the danger of the phrase, "She thinks she knows it all. Good judgment in transcription grows with watching well-written letters, reading with increased attention, caring about the work and what it means to the progress of the business, and studying the requirements and ways of the employer. 

10. The material under "Transcription with and without Shorthand" in Chapter 6 of the textbook gives concrete basis for discussing the fact that many graduates will find little use for their shorthand in comparison with what they expect. Compile for your aid in making this point a list of secretaries in various types of business or professional work with a rough average of what proportion shorthand plays in their weekly work. Two points should be made clear: (1) No secretary should be discouraged or disgruntled at not receiving enough dictation to "keep up her shorthand," for she can always whip it up with intensive study; but (2) every secretary should have this tool sharp and ready for use, no matter how little it may be required. Your apprentices should be ready to deal with transcription in all such phases as are touched upon in the photograph on page 95 of the textbook and not expect to stay in the groove of transcription in its limited meaning of typing from shorthand or even from the dictating machine. Too many beginners are inclined to resent taking positions that do not offer opportunities for steady use of shorthand--in college and business school work we can show clearly that positions requiring such use are for the stenographer rather than for the secretary with her manifold types of work. 

11. Assignment of books containing the letters of notable people offers good training in varied expression. Frequently lists are printed to show what should be avoided in the way of trite business phrasing. Better than the overstudy of this negative aspect of letter writing is the study of letters written in concise, well-phrased 

English. A ready vocabulary and a good style may be applied to any matter in hand, whether it is a question of a business or a casual letter. 

12. The four points are in the first paragraph under "Quoted Matter," Chapter 6 of the textbook, beginning "she must be careful.... 11 

13. Items a to f should be gone over in class, as carelessness in this kind of transcription is inexcusable and may lead to a variety of misunderstandings. 

14. The personal element in the use of the form letter should give a freshness to typing or duplicating. The beginner in an office should be equipped with imagination, which will help her to regard form letters as so many Links going out to so many people. 

15. "Straight" copying in an office is not a typing exercise; it forms Links which to be correct must be intelligently understood by the secretary, who should be ready to detect errors of fact as well as of form. 

16. Good experience can be given your apprentices if you interrupt their ordinary periods at the typewriter with a brief bit of dictation direct to the machine. This will be typical of office

SECTION 

67 interruption that should be repeated until such work is done with reasonable perfection. Interrupted work should then be resumed smoothly. 

17. The details under "Choosing Appropriate Stationery" should be assimilated by demonstration and practice. 

18. These details may be vividly dramatized; every effort should be made to establish good habits of procedure in transcription. 

19. The idea of the "five possible partners" must become natural to the apprentice. Concrete discussion and demonstration will help sharpen the mind to the many details that must be watched for. 

20. Questions submitted may then be asked of the class, with the book open, if desired. Because the usual secretarial course cannot give time enough for repeated drill in the intelligent manipulation of carbon copies in their varied details, the facts must be learned so that they are ready for application on the job. 

21. In addition to the points in the text, emphasize the fact that the employer does not take the initiative in "passing back papers" as they are passed back by the teacher; the secretary must keep her eye on the flow with a sense of personal responsibility. 

22. The attitude of the apprentice may at times be shifted from considering you the employer to thinking of you as the reader who is to receive the transcription and thus gather an impression of the office sending it. The personal aspect of the written Link should be made an incentive to good, readable work.

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 6-1. The five letters built around the Work-sheet form should be dictated at one time and fairly rapidly. This simulates the office situation in which the employer is so familiar with a form letter that he dictates at lightning speed, requiring double alertness on the part of the secretary to keep her notes of the changes clear. For well-known cities the dictation of the name of the state is purposely omitted. If those in the letters are not familiar to your students, substitute cities in your vicinity. 

If Monarch-size paper (7" x 11") is available, the students should gain practice in typing on this shape, which is frequently used for distinctive correspondence.

Paper may be cut to this size and also to the corresponding size for simulated envelopes.

UNIT II THE LOCUST FURNITURE STORE

Date 

Dear Sirs:

The shipment of six chiffoniers,/ your invoice #W347-39,/ which arrived this morning,/ was imperfectly packed./ As a result there are knobs broken from two drawers,/ one with the maple/ and one with the mahogany finish./ Since we are short of the stock,/ we are willing to let our repairman remove the broken pieces and replace the knobs./ Will you please send two by special delivery?/ (P) This is the third complaint of the kind that we have made lately./ If the shipments/ do not come in better condition in the future, we shall be forced to buy elsewhere./ Our repairman cannot spend so much time on new stock,/ nor can we suffer the delay in offering fresh goods to our customers./ 

Facts for the Reply. 

Yours truly,

THE LOCUST FURNITURE STORES 

By 

Manager, Shipping Department (These facts are not to be given as straight dictation, but rather for the apprentices to make notes on what the reply should cover.) Tell the Locust people/ that we'll send the knobs as requested./ Say we have been having labor trouble/ but it is settled/ and future shipments should be in better condition./ 

The replies should have all the facts mentioned and contain dignified apologies for the trouble caused. Have a few of the best letters read and discussed in class. 

Sec. Exp. 6-3. Assign this Experience for a time when the apprentice has the use of one typewriter for two full hours, and call attention to the fact that in an office such a piece of work often goes on hour after hour till it is completed. Select one or more chapters from Secretarial Efficiency, such as Chapters 14 and 15, So that the student will feel the necessary pressure of a sustained piece of typing.

This Experience may be repeated in kind whenever there is time, or whenever an able student needs extra work, for tests in proficiency in typing demand such training. The grading of these papers should take into consideration both quantity and accuracy of the typing.

SECTION 7. RIGHT THE FIRST TIME 

1. Here again we come to the point that reserving a room for the right date is 100 per cent for the secretary, but for the wrong date, 0 per cent. Unlike schoolwork, office tasks must be right as a whole.

SECTION 

71 2. Usable work is the desirable work from the apprentice, who must soon turn out successive pieces of usable work with dispatch. 

3. Teachers recognize that timidity before important tasks may come from deep-seated habits and inhibitions. In cases where the school is not free to reach vital causes, assurance should be given all the more through the most thorough training possible in secretarial skill and understanding. 

4. Wider experience, more mature judgment, special ability, knowledge of the business. It is important to emphasize this idea to protect the beginner from becoming supercilious or overbearing in small matters. 

5. Point out that the secretary cannot detect slips on the part of her employer unless she knows what is right herself. 

6. The content as well as the form should of course be grasped. 

7. The imagination of the apprentice should be caught by the idea that good spelling involves more than study of columns in a spelling book. 

8. The fact that word division depends on pronunciation is too seldom recognized by the typist. Advanced students who are linguists and are interested in words may save much time in typing if the simple rules are thoroughly understood and assimilated. Obscuring the meaning of a word by false division is perhaps the error most to be criticized by the teacher, for it is the reader whom the typist must consider. 

9. Certain of these guides are unbreakable rules, and breaking them should always be deplored. 

10. Zest for the fine shades of words can be cultivated, and word study may be made to contribute definitely to the worth of a beginner who goes to serve an employer whose interest has not been concentrated on fine expression. 

11. When a student uses an especially well-chosen word in class, the teacher may indirectly commend her by using that word almost immediately in carrying along the discussion; such moments make careful vocabulary seem natural and save the student from feeling self-conscious about good selection. Errors in spelling or in making sense may come from a need of attentive reading of well-written books. This is a part of "background. 

12. Help the apprentice to see that graduated grouping of words helps to make large and small units of thought clear. 

13. The habit of "tucking in" commas after a letter has been transcribed should not be allowed. The apprentice should be trained to foresee the unit of phrase, sentence, and paragraph as surely as the unit of the entire letter. 

14. The aspects of a written Link as paragraphed may often be pointed out to the apprentice as the specific aspects leading to carrying through work and then filing the Link. 

15. Notice and approve neat corrections whenever possible, and make suggestions on corrected papers if there are correctable errors. It is an exercise in itself to be required to correct a transcription as it stands rather than to copy it. 

Error diagnosis should always be made intelligently by the student--errors can be prevented by getting at the real, not the superficial, cause. 

16. Following the study of this chapter, refer the apprentice to the restudy of the material about "snags to watch for," if she is overlooking these most obvious errors in reading over her own transcriptions. 

17. Figure responsibility is not too often expected by the young secretary who enters a position; it is as inescapable as spelling responsibility, and the apprentice should face this in advance as an aid to a good disposition toward work that involves figures.

UNIT II 

18. The secretary who really understands how to prove figures by another route from that first taken with those figures has a real principle to rely on as a check for her work. 

19. This should be demonstrated and practiced by the students, by pairing them off at different tasks such as the checking of certain Secretarial Experiences. Too few typists know how to read over material intelligently. 

20. Good affirmative repetition has its effective influence in teaching secretarial efficiency.

Analysis of errors as numbered on

Work-sheet letter. In making this assignment, discuss with the class the necessity of getting down to the bottom of each error. For instance, a transposition of letters is a plain typing error, though the word is spelled incorrectly, whereas many mistyped words go back to poor spelling.

The typing of a wrong word may be caused by poor shorthand and also by inattention to sense when typing. An error in dividing a word is on the surface a typing error, but its real origin is ignorance of the pronunciation and syllabication of the word. Apparent typing errors may come from lack of attention to subject matter or from not applying the rules of punctuation. The point of such an analysis is, of course, to help the student establish the habit of getting at the bottom of her own persistent errors so that she will know wherein she must watch herself. Matter in parentheses is suggested for the teacher's use, when papers have been handed in and class discussion is in order: 

1. Comma instead of period at the end of the abbreviation Inc. (Frequent typing error found at end of Co., also, both in letters and on envelopes.) 

2. Gentlemen should be used, instead of Dear Sir in the singular, when addressing an incorporated concern. (Point out the use of the plural salutation with 

Messrs. I. P. Crandall, I. P. Crandall and 

Sons, The I. P. Crandall Company, Crandall 

Brothers. This list may be written on the blackboard with Gentlemen or Dear Sirs beside each one, followed by Mr. I. P. Crandall with Dear Sir.) 

3. Analyzed in the Manual. (Drive home the necessity of watching for errors that really spell words but not the right words for the place. Give as an example the error in a letter transcribed: "I will try to teach you by telephone this evening.") 

4. Representative misspelled. This may be due to slack pronunciation of the word. (Care in pronouncing vowels helps spelling.) 

5. Second comma missing to set off the name in apposition with the noun preceding. (Discuss the pairing of commas, or no commas, in connection with appositives, being sure that the students know what an appositive is. For example, the book, Secretarial Efficiency, should be studied thor oughly. The book Secretarial Efficiency should be studied thoroughly.) 

6. Separate misspelled. (Expect any student whose eye failed to detect this all-too-common error to write the word ten times on the back of her transcription for her own improvement. This is the kind of learning that the student can do for herself; the teacher cannot do it for her.

While discussing this, write the word correctly on the board with the setting of the entire phrase; it cannot be seen too often.) 

7. No comma here, unless one was paired off with it before under. This single comma separates the verb are sending from its object sample. 

8. Paragraph here. (Call attention to the fact that the secretary should read far enough into this sentence to discover the change of subject, before beginning to transcribe it. The word also of itself suggests the rightness of a new paragraph here.) 

9. Misspelling of one of the ei-ie words, freight. 

10. Misspelling of word here, though a real word is spelled. 

11. Transposition of letters in Ridgewood. (Can be helped by the secretary's listing and practicing her most frequently occurring transpositions.) 

12. Department should be spelled out in such a letter. (Bring out that some aspects of letter writing are not questions of right and wrong, but questions of taste.

Watching well-written letters in such particulars makes the secretary sensitive to matters of taste.) 

13. Should read 43. (An illustra tion of the need to watch the sense of figures: they should read to make sense just as words should. If this was an error in

UNIT II 

dictation, the secretary should have noticed such an inaccuracy, if possible, when she took down the sentence, but surely when she transcribed it. If the letter had been read over properly before it was removed from the machine, this mistake could not have passed.) 

14. Comma to mark off the introductory phrase (needed here because the two pronouns do not read well together). 

15. Overnight is not a compound word. (The secretary should know the common compound words about which there is a question as to hyphenation. When she is not sure, she should look the word up and then learn it.) 

16. Comma needed to pair off with the comma after noon, separating the conditional clause from the clause introduced by so that. (Call attention to the omission of if, which is implied.) 

17. Can should be could: either "If we receive an order, we can' or "if we should receive an order, we could." 

18. Paragraph should be revised and broken into more than one sentence. This will be particularly obvious if it is read aloud. Take time to go through several possible revisions of this absurd one-sentence paragraph, showing that the secretary should sense in advance the fact that she should split this as she types. This is an example of the faulty dictation that should be transcribed correctly the very first time. 

Too many sentences of this kind are transcribed exactly as dictated and then the entire letter is copied with a revision of a single sentence. Suggested revisions:

I hope this is all the information that you require and believe from Mr. Sills's request that he has covered everything else. If you have any further questions, please do not hesitate to get in touch either with our Woodland office (see address below) or with our main office here. 

If this does not give you all the information you wish, do not hesitate to get in touch with either our Woodland office (see address below) or our main office here.

This second revision illustrates the possibility and the value of cutting down words. 

19. The information that you require is preferred to the information which you require. (The introduction of a restrictive clause by that rather than which is preferred.) 

20. Should be Mr. Sills's request. (Point out that it is especially easy to overlook the possessive apostrophe after a word ending with s, for the sound to the ear is correct, as it would not be if this were Mr. Black request.) 

21. Word division incorrect; not divided on a syllable. (A frequent error. 

Review fact that syllabication depends on syllables; therefore this word could be misread in three syllables as cov-er-ed. 

Compare with en-closed, re-ferred, by writing all three words on the board.) 

22a and 22b. Error here is use of with either and or with. Two correct forms should be worked out by the class: 

1. in touch with either our Woodland office or our main office. 

2. in touch either with our Woodland office or with our main office. 

Using the blackboard, take up other illustrations of the use with prepositions of either--or and both--and: 

1. This hat came either from Chicago or from Buffalo. 

2. This hat came from either Chicago or Buffalo (not either from Chicago or Buffalo). city. 

1. I have lived in both the country and the 

2. I have lived both in the country and in the city (not both in the country and the city). 

23. As the class should discover, no address is given as promised at the foot of the letter. How could this oversight have been prevented? If the secretary had given full attention to what she was reading over before removing the letter from the machine, she would have noticed this omission when she came to the promise.

Reading over requires concentration, especially because other work is often pressing very hard. 

24. The period is omitted after an

SECTION 

75 abbreviation, and Manager should be spelled out. (Note what a ragged, careless look this letter has with Inc. followed by a comma at the beginning and Mgr. without its period at the close. These are the little things that may lose a position for a girl or may mean that she is transferred from stenographic work to the work of a clerk. 

An employer simply has not the time to watch these details of correct writing. Such errors convey a slipshod impression of the company responsible for them.) 

25. Sep. Cov. should have been noted, as shown in the office manual. (The apprentice should be aware that writing the words under separate cover in the letter requires a note made of that fact at the foot of the letter, unless some special form is used in the office.) 

This whole Experience should give an objective attitude toward letters that are not correctly transcribed-- just as the apprentice detects errors in this letter, so the readers of her employer's letters will detect her errors, unless she catches and corrects them.

Sec. Exp. 7-2 (Service Book III). This is an exercise in individual research and should show the traits of alertness and interest in the ability to use English, both of which the secretary must have in order to succeed. 

1. Call attention to the fact that the Vocabulary Experiences throughout the textbook are more or less general, containing words that may well appear in the dictation of any employer. The lists under B of this Experience are highly specialized, though a list may overlap from one industry to another. Emphasize the fact that the greater general vocabulary an apprentice acquires the fewer new words will she meet in her first position. 

2. The transcriptions should be well arranged, but no one form for the setup need be used by all the students. The most satisfactory might be displayed and discussed.

Sec. Exp. 7-3. Dictate these two letters as any employer might, using any irregularities in manner that you choose, with the exception of mumbling. Letter A in particular should be dictated fairly distinctly so that the students may not misunderstand and so not hear the errors as such. Neither the errors nor the repetitions should be made conspicuous, however.

Key to Correcting and Discussing Transcrip tions. The date, heading, under-signature, identifying initials of dictator and transcriber, and the notation of enclosures are omitted here but should be on each transcription. For acceptable letters your signature will appear on A as "David Long" with your last initial beneath and on B as your own name on the line beneath the company name. 

Correction of errors in Letter A. (1) Singular salutation to Mr. Samuel Horn. (2) Figure only for date when month is before that figure, that is, February 10 or the tenth of February. (3 a-b) Singular pronoun to refer to singular antecedent. (4 a-b) Word important loses force the second time and should be omitted or changed here. (5) Each is singular and requires a singular verb. (6 a-b) Unnecessary doubling

SECTION 

77 of word that--a frequent error when there is a parenthetical clause or phrase. (7) Deal (singular), not welfare and usefulness (plural), is the subject of depends. (8 a-b) Whether is correct and should be used in each instance. (9) The possessive their should be used, not the objective them. If any student makes still further improvement by writing no question about their selling, she deserves a "good" in the margin. (10) Allow either salable or saleable in transcription; though both are correct, Webster prefers the former. (11) The appearance of plural verbs and nouns between the subject (way) and the correct form of the verb (is) should not confuse the secretary into transcribing 

(12) The subject colors follows the verb and is plural. are.

Correct transcription for Letter A Explanation of errors in Letter B. 

There are three definite errors in this letter: (1) February 12th instead of 12; (2) the verb in the second sentence--the together with phrase should not confuse the secretary's recognition that wallpaper is the subject, requiring the singular verb is being shipped; (3) sheaf (of order blanks) in the last sentence is the subject, demanding is as its predicate. Three words, order, ship, and receive, are repeated much too frequently. Many of these may be omitted in a revision without losing sight of the necessities of the letter. Let several apprentices read their versions or write them on the blackboard for class study and discussion. Be sure that the student with the best version has not taken overmuch time. 

Dear Sir:

We have been considering with care your letter of February 10 about our shipment of the three necklaces. If you still feel that the customer will object and that the color will be important to her, I will talk again with Mr. French. He has been away and each of us has been extremely busy so that even this serious complaint has not received our prompt attention. We realize that, no matter how you retailers handle your goods, a greal deal of your welfare and usefulness depends on our service to you. 

We are wondering whether it is too late for us to send the green necklaces and whether you wish to return the red ones. It seems to us that there is no question of their selling. The only way for you to tell whether there are any other salable colors and styles is by letting us send a few samples. We enclose a card on which are shown various colors in our new stock. 

Very truly yours, David Long

UNIT II Suggested revision of Letter B: We were glad indeed to receive your large order of February 12.

The wallpaper for your five rooms, together with the two rugs, is being shipped today. (P) A sheaf of blanks is enclosed for your future convenience in ordering. 

The proper notation should be typed by the student for enclosure of the blanks. 

As an additional project to be included with this Experience, the following postscript may be dictated on your return of the transcript of Letter A. This will run onto a second page, which is not unusual for a postscript, and it should be returned for you to approve by signing your initials as employer.

P.S. Your customer may not be interested in bracelets,/ but we have just put out a bracelet/ that is a perfect match for those red necklaces in color and design./ They are very reasonable, too, especially if bought with the necklaces. They ought to be a big seller./ 

We shall probably have the green bracelets later,/ but so far/ we have thought we would stick to red/ until we saw which color was really the most popular in the necklaces. We might even get out the same thing in blue./

Let me know about the necklaces at once./ The matter has been in the air too long already,/ and we are as anxious as you are to have it closed./
