UNIT III. THE SECRETARY HANDLES COMMUNICATIONS SECTION 8. WHAT IS IN THE MAIL? 

With regard to postal, telephone, and telegraph regulations and rates, Part 

III of Secretarial Efficiency again stress es principles instead of teaching specific factual details, which fluctuate both in kind and in cost involved. The apprentice must learn how to keep abreast of today's rules for all such service. It is assumed that many students will have had a course in what is commonly called "junior business training, which goes fully into information of this nature. The best of textbooks for such courses make excellent reference works for your students during their study of Chapter 8, for they act either as review for apprentices who have studied them or as simple and straightforward presentation for those who have missed such training. You may find that both groups need to do supplementary work to assure familiarity with essential information of this sort. Though some secretaries are required to compute the exact cost of mailing packages, of telephoning, and of telegraphing, it is true that specific lists cannot be reliably memorized and the apprentice needs above all to learn how to find the information necessitated by her own usual duties--and whom to ask and how to ask about unusual information. She should know, for instance, when it is a saving to use express instead of parcel post; what kind of telephone or telegraph service will be the most economical for the task she has in hand; and how to count the chargeable words in her telegrams. The Experiences that deal with these subjects in this unit will show you whether or not she has an intelligent grasp of the services. 

1. Emphasize that the phrase "without disturbing" is a part of this active two-person photograph. "Professor Gray" will be recognized by many in the field of education as the collaborator on Secretari al Efficiency, Frederick G. Nichols of 

Harvard University. His secretary, Mrs. 

Sears, has helped him carry through many educational projects over a period of some years. 

2. This chapter should clearly teach the apprentice that incoming and outgoing mail are not separate but in common involve the flow of Links. 

3. The material under "All Mail Re corded in the Files" should be learned in detail to establish an intelligent attitude toward mail. 

4. The active spirit of handling mail must be positively caught in preparation for such variety of demand as is difficult to provide in the classroom. 

5. Whenever possible, help the apprentice individually to the direct method of handling things, especially things involved in tasks not particularly enjoyed. 

6. Have an advanced student prepare envelopes varied as to size and content for demonstration before the class to dramatize their opening. 

7. A part of "date-consciousness, which will be dealt with in a later chapter. 

8. Call attention to the fact that checking the contents of an envelope is but a part of "becoming acquainted with" a Link. 

9. Summarized in the sentence "As soon as a Link stands still, its whole 

Chain may be kept standing still." 

10. The traits of genuine interest and ability to concentrate must be made to seem desirable goals to the apprentice. 

11. The sentence "Mail must be mailed" is not simple in its significance. 80

SECTION 

81 The necessity of "carrying through" is taken up in a later chapter. 

12. These factual matters should be related to Secretarial Experience 8-1 which demands intelligent research into regulations and mailing economies. 

13. Emphasize the living quality of lists, which should never be used automatically or allowed to become "dead" by even slight neglect.

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 8-1 (Service Book III). This 

Experience is meant, not to test the student's knowledge as such, but to make her aware of the mailing services available and how to obtain information. The dictation material offers a contest in shorthand that should come through in transcription right the first time. If you prefer for your class, a copy of the postal information may be given to one or more students to serve as a project for duplicating machine work. 

This should be done on punched paper so that each student may have a complete copy for her Service Book. DOMESTIC POSTAGE RATES, ETC. 

(These rates obtain for the year 1939. Information regarding all such rates must be kept up to date. The secretary must always be ready to make a definite declaration of the contents of anything she is mailing. When firstclass matter is enclosed in a package, the entire package becomes firstclass. Declaration of the contents of a package may save the employer postage: for instance, there is a difference in the mailing cost for typewritten matter, matter duplicated by machine, and printed matter.)

Letters and written and sealed matter, 3 cents for each ounce, except when addressed for local delivery. Local letters, 2 cents an ounce at lettercarrier offices

Air Mail (limit 70 pounds):

PROPER MANNER OF ADDRESSING MAIL 

The name and address of the sender are required on all parcel post matter, on all registered, insured, and COD mail, and on all matter enclosed in window envelopes, and should appear on all other mail, in the upper left corner of the address side.

The words Return Postage Guaranteed should be omitted from first-class matter, and from other matter the return of which when undeliverable is not desired.

Insured and COD parcels are accepted with the understanding that the senders guarantee return and forwarding postage and should bear a pledge guaranteeing such postage; they should also bear a request specifying the maximum time they are to be held at the post office of address. 

If it is desired also to guarantee forwarding postage for other than insured or COD mail, it should be marked Return and Forwarding Postage Guaranteed.

Proofs or copies of address labels should be submitted to the postmaster to assure correct preparation. (End of dictation)

Sec. Exp. 8-2. The wrapping of packages can be irritating to secretaries who discharge the rest of their work agreeably. 

Inability to accomplish such a minor task with poise puts it out of its proper relation to other secretarial duties. A few hours spent during apprenticeship will well repay the future secretary. If possible, call in someone who makes a profession of parcel wrapping, to give a demonstration for the apprentices to imitate. Ideally, each apprentice should be required to demonstrate this skill in order to profit by criticism. Often, wrapping materials are scarce in a small office, and the secretary is called on to use her resourcefulness in wrapping a parcel. 

Gummed paper, labels, sticker for fourth-class matter, various types of adequate string, paper, scales, and other necessary materials should be provided for the demonstration. The student called upon should be assigned in each case a specific zone, such as zone 2 or 5 or 7.

Sec. Exp. 8-3. This Experience should head up a discussion of the now familiar principle of "Watching the Links" as an especially active principle when mail is being opened. The following key will act as a guide to the kinds of detail that should grow out of class discussion. In your own preparation, you may find it helpful to run over Chapter 16 of the textbook, called "Carrying Through. 

Key to Letters 11 

1. Confirmation of telegram. Compare the confirmation with the original telegram; if they are alike, file them. 

2. Remittance of rent. Make note of payment on account by this tenant, and see that Mr. Young endorses check for deposit Tuesday. 

3. Business associate asking interview. Glance at calendar pad to find possible time for interview, and watch for reply to be dictated by Mr. Young. 

4. Hotel. Check this reservation with carbon of Mr. Young's request, which has been waiting in secretary's "Pending" folder. 

5. Decorator. Follow up Mr. Young's approval of date with calendar-pad entry.

SECTION 9. MEMORANDUMS DO NOT FORGET  1. This chapter regarding memoran dums is purposely set off by itself, brief though it is, because of its essential force.

The physical littleness of the usual memorandum should not lead to its neglect in secretarial training, for its importance is by no means related to the size of the piece of paper it is written on. You can bring home to the student the memorandum as a varied, live factor, if you dictate a series of brief, imagined memorandums pertaining vividly to your own college or school. For example: reminder written by the dietitian regarding some necessary repair to her kitchen; by the school secretary regarding a message left by a parent; by the principal or president or other officer or teacher regarding specific irregular demands that might otherwise be overlooked. 

2. Special work should be given the student who passes in scraggly memorandums, as they signify a careless disrespect. Item 

3 under "Outgoing Memorandums" should be the standard. 

3. These advantages are important and bear on the spirit of a later chapter called "Helping Your Reader to Think.' 

4. The apprentice will learn the relative uses of the telephone, the spoken word, and the written word in the next chapter. The convenience of the question in writing has many points in its favor and cannot be too clearly taught. 

5. Bring out the meaning of the

UNIT III 

sentence "The memorandum may be the outcome of long and weighty thought on the part of the employer," especially if there are in your class individuals who may in their first positions tend to overlook the worth of their employers in their own feeling of self-importance. Respect both for the ability of the employer in his own line of work and for his industry can be built up in varied ways. 

6. (1) The memorandum of informa tion about a letter; (2) the reminder for writing a report; (3) the memorandum of an incoming telephone message; (4) the interoffice memorandum reporting the information from a caller. 

7. "Did you write it down?" and "Did you make a memorandum that you would be sure to see at the right time?" These two questions may be applied to forgotten or neglected instructions for schoolwork. 

8. The earmarks include date, name of person whom it is for and from whom it goes, and, often, mention of the subject in the heading. 

9. Point out that here is one of the tests of the trait of judgment. 

10. The memorandum reminds and records. Awareness of active memorandums guides the secretary in her grasp of the progress of the business. 

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 9-1. These memorandums should be dictated as from three different dictators. They are all simple and should be dictated rapidly. Be sure that the transcriptions are on the proper paper. The numbers are for your reference.

4. Mr. Drake to Miss Needham./ Did the girl I sent you yesterday afternoon arrive in your department?/ Her card has not come through, and I am unable to instate her fully/ until it does./ 

5. Mr. Kingsland to Miss Roberts./ Will you come up as soon as you can/ to see me about the order you put in last night?/ The receipt of certain items/ will take longer than the week you specified,/ and possibly you will wish to make some changes./

Sec. Exp. 9-2. It is suggested that about ten students put in these telephone calls, the number depending on the amount of time each takes. The teacher should take notes in order to make up a key for discussion of the results and for their correction and grading. See that the apprentices answering the calls bring out the necessary information and that the messages when transcribed are in good concise English but contain the full information. Additional telephone forms may be set up as a duplicating machine project for one or more students.

This Experience could well be repeated several times to give more students the experience of answering the calls and to give them all experience in getting the important points in a message quickly. If it is preferred, a committee of students can choose the subjects for the calls. The lists thus made up could be kept in your own Service Book as a cumulative record of the subjects that are valuable to use in the presentation of this Experience.

As the textbook implies, it is a mistake to study the taking of telephone messages apart from the accurate taking of other messages or writing memorandums of any sort. 

Sec. Exp. 9-3. Since the apprentices are to use their own imagination in completing this Experience, the papers will be quite different. Any memorandum giving the facts clearly followed by logical suggestions for secretarial activity should be accepted. In general they should read as follows: 

1. Mr. Norris called to ask you to answer paragraph 3 of this letter one way or the other the first thing in the morning. (I would look in Mr. Johnson's unanswered mail for the letter, mark paragraph 3, and leave it with the memorandum where he will see them when he comes in.) 

2. Mrs. Hanson is proposing to buy the car she was looking at for her daughter. She wanted literature sent to her daughter, which I have done. (I would be sure to ask Mrs. Hanson for her daughter's address, look at the record to verify the information as to the type of car she was considering, then get out advertisements and mail them to the daughter.) 

3. Mr. Metcalf is able to have dinner with you next Tuesday at seven as you suggested in your letter. He will meet you at the hotel. (I would look in the "Pending" file to see what arrangements were suggested in Mr. Johnson's letter, then enter the appointment on our two calendar pads and file the letter.) 

4. Your brother says that the real estate agent you saw last night has an offer of $17,000 for your joint property on Green St. If you agree to this price, will you call the agent tonight? His number is (I would try to reach Mr. Johnson by telephone and give him this message in case he does not come back to the office before he goes home. If I couldn't reach him, I would leave a message at his home.)

SECTION 10. 

USING THE WIRES: 

WHY? WHEN? HOW? 

1. Stress the mutual activity of communication summed up in the sentence "Each bit of communication makes its special demand on the one who frames and sends it--and its special appeal to the one who receives and must attend to it." The apprentice must understand the problems and their solution as one big whole in order to handle intelligently the individual pieces of work that make up the day. 

2. As with the memorandum, the brevity of the telegram must not keep the apprentice from recognizing its importance; discussion of the seemingly little aspects should establish the right attitude toward secretarial efficiency with the telegram. 

3. Speed and accuracy in handling and full realization of the influence of a telegram on its Chain. 

4. Research into the facts of telephone service is made in Secretarial Experience 10-3. The text itself should be made to appeal to the imagination of the apprentice as she handles telephone details. 

5. Much is involved for the secretary in the employer's demand for "a pleasing voice." This cannot be too strongly emphasized. Employers frequently request candidates to call them on the telephone before coming for a personal interview. If the voice is satisfactory, the interview is granted; if the voice is not pleasing, the interview will not be granted. 

6. Discover any members of your ?? class who may be inclined to say that they "always hate to telephone, for this attitude must be changed during training. What is the basis of this dislike, and how can it be cured by the individual? 

7. Set the student to adapt herself, with intelligence and willingness, to the needs of her very first position in this particular. 

8. A spirit of mutual helpfulness about receiving messages will be fostered if the apprentice fully recognizes the importance of the telephone in transmitting many kinds of Links in connection with a single business. 

9. Of particular importance to the beginner is the paragraph about "listening with the question mark." "Do not pretend that you have heard correctly" is a needed warning. 

10. Show how the efficient "buffer" may grow into the responsible confidential or executive secretary. 

11. Many secretaries are too ready to dismiss a call with "Mr. Eastman is not at his desk now. The detective nature of secretarial work should be made to interest the apprentice. It 

12. Adequate practice in "getting" the wanted telephone number immediately is an essential in secretarial training. 

13. Point out that the interest in the Links touched on through the medium of the switchboard should make the mechanical handling and the interruption less irritating to the secretary who considers herself somewhat above such work.

UNIT III 

14. Picture humorously to the class how it would seem if any one of them should be allowed to receive a sociable telephone call at her school desk while classwork was proceeding; this should show the irrelevance of such intrusions on office time. 

15. The possibilities of observation and imitation of telephone ways should find the beginner in a position alert to perfect herself in the art; an interest in people and in their relation to the success of the business is of primary importance. This

Sec. Exp. 10-3. (Service Book III). This 

Experience is left for you to adapt to the peculiar needs of your class.

If the apprentices have had junior business training courses in these important matters, they will need to do nothing but make the brief résumé suggested. If, however, the majority of them have had no such course, they may need a class discussion and drill to help them understand the various uses of the telephone and where they should turn when they need further facts. The service,

UNIT III 

of course, varies in different parts of the country.

The selected list of numbers to be memorized may be made to serve a real need in the training of the apprentice Whenever you can, you should ask for this or that number in the course of dictation or discussion or at any time, as an employer might. 

Sec. Exp. 10-4. There can be no key to the written work for this Experience. It is suggested that a full class discussion be held on the papers so that the class will get the benefit of the observations of others. Whenever there is an occasion to dramatize a telephone conversation, be sure that the apprentices use good form. The preferred phrases should become the automatic response before an apprentice goes to her first position.

SECTION 11. THE CALLER COMES FACE TO FACE 

1. By appearance--recognizing the caller in person; by voice--recognizing him by telephone; by signature--recognizing him by mail. 

2. Among the secretarial duties are: keeping valuables in a safe, orderly arrangement and distribution of pamphlets, typing and mailing letters, signing for and caring for telegrams, meeting callers and taking messages, posting bulletin-board notices and keeping them shipshape and up to date, labeling drawer guides. 

3. Make clear that courtesy and brevity are common to the written, the telephoned, and the face-to-face communication. 

4. An apprentice group has vital need of individual personality training for this part of the approaching work. That graduate is fortunate who has had the benefit of constructive help from a wise teacher, psychiatrist, or other adviser. 

5. Here due appreciation of the

Link from the other person's point of view requires the trait called "intuition. 11 

6. The vocation of the "receptionist" may be discussed here, if it is relevant to your type of graduate, including the necessary willingness of the receptionist to do many odd jobs to fill in her time. 

7. Show the apprentice that refusing a caller is a difficult task, but one common to most secretarial positions and therefore challenging to efficient handling. 

8. If your students have studied psychology, this aspect of secretarial work may yield itself to an active exchange of ideas. If they have not had the benefit of psychological study, the reading of such a clear text as Woodworth's Psychology may help them to understand, not only other people, but themselves as well. An intelligent student of this subject should be understanding and tolerant of the shortcomings of associates and employer. 

9. Fuller discussion of this subject is given later in the text. 

10. In certain offices, the matter of handling conferences proves to be one of the most important duties of the secretary; here is an opportunity to emphasize again the fact that secretarial work is not wholly made up of taking dictation and directly transcribing that dictation. 

11. The student should note in the text both the mechanical and the personal details.

Key to Secretarial Experiences 

Sec. Exp. 11-1. This should give experience in clear thinking, following instructions, using the typewriter intelligently to display facts. This is the kind of training on the student's own ground of interest that makes it possible later for her to make out comparative data about types of stock as sold by a clothier, or whatever it may be. 

Here we can instill the principle that should serve all alike in actual business.

SECTION 11 

93 In advance of carrying this Experience through, the columns may be outlined roughly on the board during class discussion if such help is necessary to your group. Whatever can be left to their initiative, however, carries an essential element into an Experience. Column 1 should list the names, followed by columns headed by the various categories selected; some of the students may have played the game called "Categories," which uses the essence of good tabulation. as 

Sec. Exp. 11-2. This Experience lends it self to various treatments. Class discussions of the papers will add value, will extemporaneous dialogues between stu- . dents. Any time that can be given to conducting unrehearsed conversations, provided that they are taken seriously, will develop the poise and confidence necessary to a secretary.

Sec. Exp. 11-3. This Experience is planned for a class discussion in which may valuable points may be brought out in summing up the attitude toward callers. If this is impracticable for your group, however, you may ask for an original essay. It would be well to have in mind a few outstanding people, whom the students would know, to bring up for discussion in case the observations of the class have not included persons generally known. 

Chain C at the end of the Manual and the Guide will be carried through after the study of Section 11.

UNIT IV. THE SECRETARY BOTH PUTS AWAY AND FINDS SECTION 12. THE ALPHABET IS YOUR FRIEND 

1. This "alphabet sense" in its entirety must be acquired by hard study, if necessary; the apprentice without it is not eligible for recommendation as a secretary. 

2. Visualizing the alphabet can be made interesting through class competition in connection with the study of this chapter. 

3. The esprit de corps among the associates within a single office depends in part on the uniformity of procedure in small and large matters. 

4. Advanced students may prepare extra work for each other by finding information in certain accessible sources, such as the unabridged dictionary, and listing questions for each other to answer by the most direct possible routes. 

5. There is no better way to teach the alphabet to students of the apprentice age than to persuade the individual to "buckle down" and learn it. 

6. Help the class and then the

This ex- backward individual to catch and follow clues as a good detective would. ercise should be repeated by each student until facility is gained. 

7. Experience in the use of all these guides should be given, especially in the training of apprentices for superi- 

Have actively available for or positions. practice as many types as possible to give a familiar swing to this inevitable secretarial duty, and urge students throughout the course to turn in spare moments to the exercise of their "finding muscles." 

8. Elasticity may be gained through 94 clear recognition of the variety of placement and kinds of eye guides, as indicated under "Summary of Alphabetical Guides." 

9. Have one or more students put their groups of words on the blackboard out of alphabetical sequence, and have others read them out to the class in their correct sequence with all possible swiftness. Alertness of eye must be trained through frequent drill. 

10. The number of a sales person as shown on a sales slip shows the reasonableness of listing clerks by number for identification and for pay-roll purposes. Ask the class what the expression A-1 means in order to point out the beginning of both letter and figure sequences. The four types of figure use given under "Figure Convenience and Figure-letter Convenience" should be demonstrated clearly in the classroom. 

11. Exercises or "Experiences" of this kind can be made up by the students themselves for quick, correct untangling into alphabetical or numerical order, as the case may be. Such work not only gives facility in handling letter and figure sequences but adds to the "feeling" for their varied helpful uses. 

12. Courteous concern should be shown among students in keeping in order equipment that is used by the teacher and by other students. The trait of personal regard for the convenience of others can be trained during apprenticeship; if the Service Book is not in good order for your examination or papers are handed in with the pages out of order, consideration of your time and convenience as 'employer" should be pointed out to the offending student.

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 12-1. Let these Experiences and others you may devise with the reference

SECTION 12 

95 material you have at hand develop practical familiarity with the usefulness of handling books intelligently. Emphasize a thoughtful approach to the books at hand, especially the textbook and the dictionary. 

This attitude should be acquired so that no matter what line of business she enters each apprentice may be prepared to look about her and discover how to find information for that business quickly. 11 Make the service rendered to any reader by both the table of contents and the index seem as interesting as possible. 

The apprentice must learn to turn to them for help first; they offer her one avenue to "finding, which is so important in an office. This Experience is divided into 

Parts A to D. Like all Experiences throughout the textbook, these parts demand a "thinking" apprentice. She must be prepared to type information so that clear significance is given to data found; she must be prepared to give oral information clearly, concisely, and without selfconsciousness. These four parts combine written and oral demands.

Part A. The "one further step, involving arithmetic" is of course the reckoning of the number of pages per section instead of copying the page references as printed. This is planned to give experience in using information in an independent way as carefully defined in instructions.

Part B. Many of your students may go to offices where telephone directories both for large cities and for smaller towns must frequently be consulted. 

Experience makes use only of the directory in the student's own town or city, but if it is possible all the students should become familiar with a variety of telephone directories. For this Experience, try to obtain several directories for your district and a few other types, even though some are out of date. The student should be warned that there are not enough copies to go around the class and that this is therefore one of the secretarial duties that must be fitted in with other work without waiting till the last minute when all the available directories may be in Co-operation in using the same ma use. terial or same machines in an office often demands a similar adjustment, and the student must learn to be foresighted and to adapt herself to the needs of others. 

Part C. This should stimulate curiosity about reference books, which should be made to seem, not dead, but alive with necessary information. Apply this Experience to other reference books, as far as time permits, for the entire class or for students who can take care of extra work. The relating of this Experience to other studies is purposely mentioned in the 

Experience Manual. The importance of gener al informational background should be kept before the apprentice secretary.

Part D. At this point it will be practicable and profitable to handle with the class any of the texts they are now using or have recently become familiar with. 

This brings the lesson of this Experience right home to everyday secretarial practice.

In office training the phrase filing and finding is frequently and rightly used. But finding must be done in dictionaries, reference works, catalogs, directories, indexes, supply closets, desk drawers, and on top of the desk. The mind of the orderly secretary must compass the entire scene of action.

Sec. Exp. 12-2 (Service Book IV). The ob ject of this Experience is to let the student herself practice the principles that guide all alphabetical finding, noticing how those principles vary in different kinds of reference work. It will, of course, be helpful if you can put at their disposal any active file or card index or bulletins. Class discussion of different methods of alphabetical finding should bring out the value of the most direct possible approach to whatever information may be sought. Ask for demonstrations of procedure by various students.

SECTION 13. HERE IT IS! 

So far as space is concerned the reprinting of a filing course in this textbook is out of the question, especially since many students will have already studied filing and since good practical equipment is so readily obtainable. Such a reprinting would tend to emphasize the learning of an important skill at the expense of a wide variety of other secretarial essenfile. Doubtless you have already made use of one of the briefer courses in filing such as Progressive Indexing and Filing pub lished by the Library Bureau Division of 

Remington Rand, Inc.

The filing required in the course is based on the alphabetic system as the simplest and most widely used. There is little question but that eventually your students should have an acquaintance with the other outstanding systems and the specific purposes that distinguish them from each other.

In presenting this entire section

SECTION 13 

97 to your class, you will see that the text has made a point of enlarging the idea of the filing principle and has emphasized the principle of orderliness with everything that may need to be found. We should work to make real the question of "keeping things" --a question that never comes really alive until the apprentice is handling the material that she herself helps to create day by day. Whenever during classwork you can give to or draw out from students a flash of insight into this important secretarial necessity of keeping and finding, you may be sure that you have at that moment been a supreme interpreter of the spirit of what is called secretarial science." 11 

1. Daily filing must be emphasized since adequate advance experience of this important habit cannot be given. That daily filing clears the desk of unnecessary matter and that this filing prepares for future finding--these are two vital points.

The slogan "Correct Filing Leads to Quick 

Finding" is never too hackneyed to be stressed with a new group of students. 

2. Adaptability based on an understanding of the principles and the spirit of filing keeps the beginner in an office from confusion due to the fact that she "never did it that way in school. Especially, the apprentice must become aware of the wide variety of things that may have to be kept in findable order. 11 

3. Help the student to apply this advice to specific work in hand whenever possible, especially the rule about using her own best judgment; employers and office supervisors make just complaint of the girl who holds up work because, as she says, "I didn't know just how you wanted it done." 

4. The incessant filling of the filing basket cannot be simulated in a course of study, and the daily responsibility toward filing, therefore, needs to be learned thoroughly in theory. 

5. Itemizing these twelve steps forms an applied review of what may be learned in a filing course. 

6. Overlooking outstanding matters and burying them in the file are grave instances of inattention on the part of the secretary. The apprentice should be made to realize that, just as the elastic band may inattentively be slipped along on a shorthand notebook in such a way as to lock off an unfinished bit of transcription, so a file may lock off unfinished matters of real importance. 

7. Be sure that the apprentice thoroughly understands the necessity of having every paper accessible at all times, whether in the file, in active use, or in orderly pending matter. This theory of threefold accessibility is all-important for efficient secretaryship. 

8. The apprentices should be able, not only to define these words, but also to understand them so that the proper index words will be recognized at a glance in preparing papers for the file. 

9. Intelligent choice of words for indirect indexing should be stressed, but it must be understood that there is not always a need for such cross-reference. 

10. Practice in choosing major and minor captions is, of course, a part of the required course in filing. 

11. Tests of speed in alphabetizing a miscellaneous lot of papers will test the student's knowledge more thoroughly than rote reciting of the proper method for sorting. 

12. Convenience of the individual folder as an aid to finding frequently needed material should be emphasized all through this chapter on filing. 

13. The habit of keeping papers of one group in chronological order with the latest date on top should be emphasized. 

14. The three rules are: (1) Staple or paste small papers to a larger sheet. (2) Do not use clips. (3) Fasten together pages of one letter but not other related papers. 

15. For class demonstration the

UNIT IV 

teacher may set up a series of cards, varied in size and arrangement of entries, to be arranged alphabetically. Requiring each apprentice to find certain cards should convince her of the convenience of uniformity in size and setup. 

16. The out-sheet habit should be thoroughly taught in the filing course and practiced every time a sheet is taken for use from the student's own File folder. 

17. The student should pick out many definite hints from the material under "Your Hands and Your File," such as: inserting out-sheet for borrowed papers; raising card behind one removed from box; leafing over papers with eraser; using alternate hands for successive motions at file. 

18. Transfer filing of necessity involves throwing away; this calls into play the finest secretarial judgment. Also, the secretary must leave her transfer files in such shape that anyone could find a needed paper after she herself has left the position. 

19. When only a few persons in an office are trusted with the responsibility of the safe, the secretary who is one of those few not only should be trustworthy as to keeping things in it and locking it but also should make herself familiar with the contents of the safe in order to be ready to find whatever may be required. 

20. The futility of becoming angry or losing poise over a search for a lost paper should be discussed. No matter who is to blame for the loss, anything will be found more quickly if the secretary keeps her temper and her head during the hunt.

The seven elements in good hunting might well be memorized by the students. 

21. While the apprentices are still in the student class, they should constantly be made aware of the ways in which they can increase their speed without undue hurrying.

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 13-1 (Service Book III). Part A. 

For this part of the Experience you may wish to send your students to some particular reference book for their rules, or, if their previous training is adequate, you may allow them to find the information themselves.

Part B. The list given below includes most of the definite instructions for filing to be found in the chapter. The apprentices' lists will vary somewhat and need not be quite so exhaustive, but they should contain the majority of the items. (If desired, after class discussion, dictate the items from this key for the purpose of comparison so that each student may make her list complete.)

Reference works. Reference books or cata logs, if numerous, require a card index. 

Books and cards should be kept in their exact places. Bound reports, bulletins, and periodicals should be kept upright with the latest date of a series to the right on the shelf. A set of magazines may be laid flat with the latest issue on top. The secretary must keep each series complete.

Correspondence file. Clip the carbon copy of the answer to original letter, marking them both with F to show that they are ready for the file. Index for filing by underlining in red the name on the letterhead of original letter and identical name on carbon copy of reply. Keep the two together with the carbon copy on top. Captions for indexing are selected from (1) name of person or concern addressed in outgoing mail; (2) name on letterhead of incoming mail; (3) name of person or concern signing letter on incoming mail; (4) one or more outstanding names of people or concerns or subjects referred to in letter. Use the most important reference as the major caption, underlining it for direct indexing. Make out any necessary cross-reference sheet when preparing a letter for filing. If a letter is partly ready for filing but one or more items are outstanding, (1) keep it on desk, (2) put it in a "Pending" folder, (3) make a note of unfinished details in a card tickler, on follow-up sheet, on desk calendar, or on a special memorandum--always with clear reference to the letter by date and name.

Out-sheets. If a letter is needed from the file, fill in an out-sheet to substitute.

SECTION 13 

99 Out-sheets must include date of piece of correspondence and of removal, major filing caption, initials of remover, name of person for whom piece is removed. Outsheets should be colored to distinguish them from the regular stationery. Remove the out-sheet when refiling the letter. 

Miscellaneous folders. The miscellaneous alphabetical folder holds matter for which there are no more than five Links related to one person or one subject. Make an individual folder when the sixth Link is added. Make a cross-reference to the miscellaneous folder at that time. 

Transfer filing. In transfer filing, destroy only what will surely not be needed. 

Make sure that the transfer file would be. clear to a stranger. Transfer crossreferences when transferring. Change folder and drawer labels when changing the contents of a file. 

Sorting. How to sort by the shortest cuts (see textbook, page 289). How to sort numbered cards (see textbook, page 296, foot, to 297). A rubber finger pad or the eraser end of a pencil helps sort many papers. 

Cards. Cards should be used for mailing lists, records of callers, accounts, medical and dental case histories, records showing expense of labor and materials, followup work, index guides to numeric filing. 

Typewrite and write by hand evenly on the rules of cards. Figures entered on cards at different times should be uniform in alignment. Hold the card close to platen when typing. Colored cards will make certain information stand out.

The safe. Legal documents, negotiable papers, papers with cash value are kept in the safe. An extension folder labeled on the end is good for valuable papers in the safe. When a paper is filed in the safe, cross-reference it in the regular file and note on the item in the safe where the related material may be found in the file. 

Papers of a private nature may be kept in sealed envelopes, in a locked drawer, or in the safe. Boxes can be used in the safe to hold related things. The petty cash box should be kept in the safe. Keys should be kept in a box in the safe. The main supply of postage should be in a folder in the safe. Try the handle of the safe after shutting it for the night. 

Miscellaneous information. 

Leave a signal for the place of removal when a paper or a card is taken from a file temporarily: (1) Slightly lift the card or paper behind the one removed. (2) If drawer must be shut, stand a piece of paper on end to mark the place of removal. (3) Keep the place in a folder by leaving the folder open on a flat surface or by putting a paper in crosswise if the folder must be closed. Make a new individual folder at regular intervals (six months or a year) for frequent correspondents. Make every motion at the file count: open the drawer with the left hand; find the folder with the right; hold the other folders aside with the left; pick out the folder with the right; use both hands to open the folder. Always put the date in the upper right corner of papers. Keep the sheets with the top edges approximately even. Staple small memorandums or receipts to cheap typewriting paper. Do not use clips in the permanent file. Fasten two or more pages of a letter together in the upper left corner. Apply certain rules for the correspondence file to any index. 

Part C. Throughout the teaching of this chapter and its application as the course develops further, you will have most favorable opportunity to watch important traits of the individual student: sense of responsibility, ability to do independent thinking, resourcefulness, accuracy, judgment, imagination, thoroughness, speed, and understanding of the other person's point of view. The concrete illustrations given by the student should show clearly that she has grasped the very personal responsibility assumed by the secretary toward her file.

Sec. Exp. 13-2. Part A. Have several stu dents help you set up one or more sets of skeleton "letters," running to at least 100 for a set. Select a list of alphabetically varied names to be typed as if they were addressees of real letters, both on fullsize paper and on half-size used each way of the paper. For this Experience, nothing but the name and date is needed on a sheet; in a few instances the same person should

UNIT IV 

be addressed under several dates. If the names are drawn in alphabetical sequence from some list or directory, your key may be made up as you go along.

Part B. 

This Experience tests the student for independent work from start to finish and is a competitive drill that may well be repeated for review.

Sec. Exp. 13-3. 

This Experience is planned to give the students both training in alphabetizing long lists and practice in copying figures from lists. The checking should be done by the class in this way: read only the names in alphabetical order from the first list and only the states and names from the second list. When the apprentices have checked their lists for order, divide them in pairs and let each pair check the rest of the information together. 

The two keys are on first two facing pages following this page.

UNIT V. THE SECRETARY KNOWS THE IMPORTANCE OF TIME SECTION 14. 

WHAT TIME IS IT? 

of the resultant unpleasantness as possible. 

1. More confidence is placed in a person who keeps a clock right and a calendar turned to the current date. Such habits show attention to details and a sense of responsibility. 

2. The practice of writing down appointments and important duties not only relieves the memory but also ensures the prompt carrying through of tasks so noted. 

3. Although a calendar pad is used as a reminder, the apprentice should always keep in mind that its principal function is to show the current date. A calendar should never be kept open at a previous date because of duties unfinished or, at a future date because a new notation has been entered. 

4. The secretary must train herself to note all four items connected with each appointment. If, then, there is some mistake on the part of someone else, she must be able to take the blame gracefully to avoid any unpleasantness for her employer. 

5. The secretary must learn to be firm in protecting her employer's interests and conserving his time and yet avoid being disagreeable to those who wish to consult him. She must practice diplomacy. 

6. Trouble over a broken appointment must always be met with poise on the part of the secretary. Her attitude will do much toward regaining the good will of a client or a patient. 

7. When extra appointments must be fitted into a day already full, the secretary has to show wisdom in rearranging the schedule, tact in putting off those for whom her employer has no time, and a spirit of co-operation in shouldering as much 

8. A busy employer keeps his mind on the tasks at hand until the very moment he must go to a conference. The secretary must do all that she can to help him turn to his next task with the least loss of time and energy. Without too much interruption, she must discover what material he needs and what her duties will be in connection with the conference. 

9. Being aware of the time for appointments and for the accomplishment of certain tasks is a secretarial duty involving much more than just "knowing what time it is"--particularly in respect to the lunch and closing hours. 

10. The importance of dating even the smallest notation should be thoroughly impressed upon the apprentices. This should become a habit which they never outgrow. 

11. The position of the date may seem to be a mere convention to the apprentices now, but they should be led to recognize the amount of time that can be lost in searching over each one of a pile of papers for the date. 

12. The use of abbreviations allowable on certain of the work handled by the secretary is a definite timesaver. Ask the apprentices if they have to count on their fingers to find the number for the month. 

13. Like a continued story in a magazine, the Links of a Chain must follow one another in their chronological order.

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 14-1. An Experience of this sort is largely a matter of mechanical skill of a kind that is needed frequently in an office. It should take very little time, and 104

SECTION 15 

105 there is no reason why all the copies should not be absolutely correct. If your course is so arranged that you could assign the 

Experience at the beginning of a typewriting period and have it done at once, it would approach more nearly a similar office situation. There would be the added advantage that the apprentices who are inclined to be slow at such a task would see what a difference there was between their time and that of the faster workers. It should be a valuable object lesson, and individual difficulties thus revealed should be analyzed. 

The notices need not be arranged uniformly, but they should all be nicely centered with good margins. The papers should be checked to make sure that in the process of duplicating the notice nothing has been omitted from any one. The apprentices should be graded on remembering the return address in the corner of the envelopes as well as on other details.

If the results show that more work is needed, it would be a simple matter to prepare material for several such Experiences. It might be valuable also to have the dates written out first and then to ask the apprentices to write them in figures. is:

SECTION 15: WHAT SHALL I DO NEXT? 

1. Although the work of a student should not ordinarily be broken into, some irregularity in the schedule would be good training for future secretaries.

If prac- ticable, arrange for brief interruptions from outside, having a graduate come in to tell of her experiences or someone come in to dictate. Interruptions that cannot be scheduled are valuable. Every now and then have the apprentices put aside what they had planned for the day and carry out some

UNIT V 

entirely different task. This will give a glimpse of possible office interruptions and help the apprentices to meet them calmly. They should learn to carry out regular work in spite of interruptions. 

2. Discuss the fact that it is for the secretary herself to maintain a feeling of regularity in the midst of irregularities of schedule. 

3. Speed, accuracy, efficiency, orderliness, and judgment will all help the secretary to overcome a loss of time from irregular demands. 

4. In a small way the apprentice herself can divide her work now into the four degrees of immediacy and plan her time accordingly. Special help should be given individual students who are frequently late about handing in their assignments or have evidently carried through their work hastily. Such students should be made to recognize now that these habits will not do for the office. 5. Initiative combined with decisive judgment is the trait that leads secretary to go ahead with what she must do. Students who, from your observation or their own confession, are "slow starters" should be helped to overcome the difficulty. 

6. It should be clear to the apprentices that to leave work hanging undone is a sign of weakness that must be overcome. They should also see the time saving advantage of finishing each task while they "know where they are at" and what it is about. 

Manuscript for dictation: 

7. Secretarial Efficiency stresses the importance of arranging work so that it can all be done within the regular hours, but no student should graduate with the feeling that she should never be called on to work overtime to help in an emergency. 

8. The secretary must analyze her own habits before complaining of an overload of duties. In a similar way the apprentice who thinks her assignments too long should be helped to analyze herself and perhaps be urged to work "overtime" on the subject that holds her back until she can complete her assignments in the normal amount of time.

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 15-1 (Service Book IV). The transcription of the manuscript that is given here for dictation should be set out in effective display by the apprentices. 

In dictating, you may mention the Roman and Arabic numerals or, if the class is advanced, give no specific directions, letting the students plan their own display as they would many times in an office. And, too, you may or may not discuss the best way to set out the main heading and subheadings--capitals, italics, or capitals and small letters. By this time the apprentices should be able to set up welldisplayed copy direct from their shorthand. 

The material in this article is vital to the chapter itself. There should be a class discussion of it immediately following the dictation to see that the students are understanding what they take in shorthand.

THE BEGINNING, MIDDLE, AND END OF A SECRETARY'S DAY 

by 

A Private Secretary

When I worked in a small office,/ I watched with special care three points of the day:/ the beginning,/ the middle, and the end./ The achievement of the entire day seemed somehow to be measured by the way I handled these three points/--by what I thought to do and how I went about it./ If

SECTION 15 

107 my memory, my judgment,/ my initiative, my industry,/ my order of procedure, and my poise/ were in trim at those three times,/ I could count on my approach to work for the remainder of the hours./ 

I. Starting the Day/

Always I tried to get to the office before Mr. Morse came in./ Then

I followed this order with more or less regularity,/ keeping in mind that his desk and my desk/ must be the two orderly pivots/ for the day's work that we were about to put through together./ 

1. Starting at my desk I sorted the mail swiftly,/ taking out the first-class matter/ which I opened, date-stamped, and took immediately to his desk/ with any correspondence or data that he might wish to have for reference/ when he first ran through this mail./ 

2. Before leaving my desk/ I glanced at my calendar pad/ to see what was specially scheduled for appointments/ or work for the day. I have always felt that grasping the plan of the day at the start/ was like shaking hands with a newcomer with a quick, firm grip./ 

3. Before laying the new mail out for Mr. Morse,/ I straightened out his desk/ with due regard to his convenience in starting his day with a sense of order./ I believe that a secretary who has an intelligent interest/ in the unfinished work on her employer's desk/ can save him time and annoyance/ by rearranging papers so that important matters are on top/ and sheets that have strayed from each other are clipped together./ Some employers do not like their desks touched in this way,/ but many men appreciate the sense of system/ that prompts their secretaries to place things in reasonable order./ 

Mr. Morse always expected to find the important leftovers/ at the right side of his desk/ and the newly opened mail before him in the center./ 

4. In preparing his desk,/ I dusted it,/ emptied his ash tray,/ sharpened his pencils,/ placed his blank scratch pads for notes and memorandums within reach at the back of his desk,/ and set in order whatever periodicals or pamphlets were there for his attention./

UNIT V 

5. I glanced over the calendar-pad page for the day before./ 

If any notations had not yet been attended to,/ I made a note of them before turning over to the page for the current day. I looked to see what was written there for Mr. Morse's day./ 

6. From his desk, I removed what he had put in the secretary's basket/--material for the file or bearing instructions for me to carry out./ 

7. Returning to my desk,/ I dusted it, set it in order, and cared for the second- and third-class mail,/ which I usually held to take in to Mr. Morse later,/ when my first errand took me to his desk./ 

He liked to have this mail kept at the left side of his desk/ and usually took business magazines and circulars home to read./ 

8. I turned to my tickler for the day's matters/--those that 

I should attend to myself/ and those that I should be on the lookout for from others./ This gave me a clear glance ahead/ at work that belonged to that day in particular./ These were matters that I must keep in mind./ 

9. Then I began immediately to attack the outstanding tasks on my desk,/ working through everything in my notebook that was hanging from the previous day/ before I might be called for fresh dictation./

During all this time, of course my telephone would demand attention,/ I greeted my associates as they came in, and sometimes an early-morning caller would need to be ushered in to see Mr. Morse./ But I allowed no interruptions to interfere with my determination to start the day/ for both my employer and myself/ with alert attention./ While I sharpened pencils,/ removed the cover from my typewriter,/ cleaned the machine,/ and seated myself for the first attack,/ I was thinking about the urgent demands,/ new work ahead,/ old work to be finished,/ and matters to be followed up./ This first half hour set the key./ Before I was plunged into detail itself,/ I could look steadily across the whole day with a sense of poise and intelligence./

SECTION 15 

109 

II./ Holding the Day Together/

The middle of the day always brought its own peculiar demand on me./ 

As the lunch hour approached,/ I was hungry and felt my shoulders inclining to sag/ because the weight of the day's work seemed on them all at once./ 

Late in the morning, tasks seemed to accumulate/ and often little appeared to have been accomplished./ I had to train myself not to look at the clock overmuch during that last hour before lunch./

The hour away from the office always refreshed me but often made me sleepy./ I found that the kind of food I ate affected this condition./ Whenever possible, I either kept out in the fresh air/ or stretched out for five minutes on the wicker couch in our dressing room./ I made every effort to have what I ate or did with that hour/ fit me to return to the incomplete work on my desk/ with fresh vigor and a good disposition toward the new attack./

By watching my attitude toward work immediately after lunch,/ I discovered that it was too easy to dawdle/ during the first fifteen minutes,/ considering what to do or how to do it./ So I learned to slip into my chair and take up my first afternoon work/ with especially alert attention/ in order to set myself in a good tempo for all the work that lay ahead before night./ 

From experience/ I know that the lunch hour can be a stout thread to hold the day together/ if a secretary ties the knots securely both before and after./ 

III. Before Closing the Door/ 

Except when overtime work was absolutely necessary,/ I made it a point to leave the office promptly, but I held myself responsible for leaving everything in proper order for the night./ The specific items that I attended to before I closed the door/ ran something like this:/

A./ Making sure that Mr. Morse had signed the outgoing mail,/ putting that mail into its envelopes,/ and laying it where I would be certain to take it to the mailbox myself./ Even in offices where an office boy came regularly to my desk for mail,/ I found that there was usually a residue that needed to be mailed by me./ Sometimes this last-minute outgoing mail needed to be registered or insured,/ and I had to take it to the nearest post office./ I usually met other secretaries leaving their offices/ with sealed envelopes of various sizes in their hands,/ ready for this final mailing of the day./

UNIT V 

B. Preparing for the next day by leaving my own desk ready for a clear-cut morning attack./ I always liked to know in advance/ the direction I was going to take when I headed into a new day./ 

In fact, I was always particular about leaving my work on Saturday noon/ so that everything possible was cleared out of the way for a right start/ on Monday morning./ 

C. Caring for mechanical details,/ including 

1. Covering my typewriter from the dust the janitor might raise/ 2. Putting valuable documents and money into the small safe/ 

3. Being sure to lock the safe/ 

4. Shutting the windows/ 

5. Turning off the lights/

And as I left the office for the night,/ it was my responsibility to be sure that the door was locked./ This was my last moment of attention to my position as secretary./ I always required it to be an alert moment/ so that I would not have to say when I was halfway down the street,/ "I wonder whether I locked that door."/ And when that lock clicked, I let it lock away my concern for business./ I was free from secretarial responsibilities for the night or for the week end,/ and my position and I would both be better off/ if I threw myself wholeheartedly into that freedom./

Seo. Exp. 15-2 (Service Book IV). Part A. 

For these copies, onionskin paper or other very thin manifold paper should be used, and, ideally, the thinnest carbon paper of good quality. The use of such carbon paper should be "experienced" at least once during the course, but if necessary as a matter of economy a few sets of five sheets each may be used by the class in turn, although the last to use a set will be considerably handicapped. Since erasing carbon copies written in single space is difficult, it may be wise before undertaking this Experience to discuss with the class and demonstrate to them the points given on pages 153 to 154 of the textbook. This is an instance of the absolute necessity of an erasing shield.

THE OLD SAILOR 

by A. A. Milne 

There was once an old sailor my grandfather knew

Who had so many things which he wanted to do That, whenever he thought it was time to begin, He couldn't because of the state he was in. 

He was shipwrecked, and lived on an island for weeks, And he wanted a hat, and he wanted some breeks; 

And he wanted some nets, or a line and some hooks

For the turtles and things which you read of in books. 

And, thinking of this, he remembered a thing

Which he wanted (for water) and that was a spring; 

And he thought that to talk to he'd look for, and keep (If he found it) a goat, or some chickens and sheep.

Then, because of the weather, he wanted a hut With a door (to come in by) which opened and shut (With a jerk, which was useful if snakes were about), And a very strong lock to keep savages out.

He began on the fish-hooks, and when he'd begun 

He decided he couldn't because of the sun.

So he knew what he ought to begin with, and that 

Was to find, or to make, a large sun-stopping hat.

He was making the hat with some leaves from a tree, When he thought, "I'm as hot as a body can be, And I've nothing to take for my terrible thirst, So I'll look for a spring, and I'll look for it first." 

Then he thought as he started, "Oh, dear and oh, dear! 

I'll be lonely to-morrow with nobody here!" 

So he made in his note-book a couple of notes: "I must first find some chickens" and "No, I mean goats."

He had just seen a goat (which he knew by the shape) 

When he thought, "But I must have a boat for escape.

But a boat means a sail, which means needles and thread; 

So I'd better sit down and make needles instead."

He began on a needle, but thought as he worked, That, if this was an island where savages lurked, Sitting safe in his hut he'd have nothing to fear, Whereas now they might suddenly breathe in his ear!

So he thought of his hut and he thought of his boat, ... 

And his hat and his breeks, and his chickens and goat, And the hooks (for his food) and the spring (for his thirst) 

But he never could think which he ought to do first.

And so in the end he did nothing at all, But basked on the shingle wrapped up in a shawl.

And I think it was dreadful the way he behaved-- 

He did nothing but basking until he was saved! ...
