Sec. Exp. 15-3. Although the entire group might profit by a general discussion of these tabulations, they should serve as a basis for private conferences with those students who seem unable to arrange time to the best advantage--those who carry out tasks as they come with no thought for the future. Possibly you may need to ask some apprentices to continue to keep accounts so that they will be led to see their failings in regard to the use of time. 

SECTION 16. 

CARRYING THROUGH 

1. Telegrams, agreements, appointments all depend on time in their relation to Chains. The secretary knows that the

Links for which she is responsible must be carried through or the wheels stop running. 

2. The apprentices must realize that the three practical aids--the tickler, calendar pad, and memorandum--are impractical unless the secretary heeds their reminders. 

3. Again the apprentice must understand that no aids are effective unless the secretary herself takes the constant responsibility for them. 

4. The intelligent secretary asks necessary questions before she goes ahead. 

5. A secretary who fails to carry through even the smallest order, may cause a break in an important Chain. 

6. The right attitude toward the menial task must be developed by the apprentice. She must bear in mind the statement in the text that she is truly above her task when she does it without annoyance. 

7. The employer appreciates a secretary who always anticipates his needs, and the secretary herself avoids many lastminute rushes by maintaining this attitude. 

8. If she is aware of them, the secretary as part of her duties should see that business promises made by her employer in dictation, over the telephone, or in any other way, are carried through. 

9. Courtesy and tact are required of the secretary when she must remind others to carry out promises made to her employer.

SECTION 16 

113 

10. Questions, like promises, place a responsibility on the secretary, even though she is not directly responsible for answering them. 

11. The duties of different secretaries vary as to the extent to which they can carry through Chains without the employer's attention. Every secretary should do as much as she can to be helpful--finding information requested, making memorandums, and particularly seeing that she herself carries out the mechanical part of the 

Chain without error. 

12. The apprentice on going to her first position should not be concerned as to whether she is to be called a "stenographer" or a "secretary," but she should determine to become genuinely interested in the business, no matter what her title. 

13. The apprentice should train herself to check off all items attended to, even though she feels sure of remembering that she did them. By this means, she will frequently save confusion and time in trying to recall what has been done. 

14. A secretary who is really interested in her work will want to know what Links of a given Chain have been carried through by others as well as by herself so that she will have a complete picture of all the Chains of the business that she is sharing.

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 16-1 (Service Book IV). The ar ticle, "May I Ask a Question, Please?" is given below. You may use one of the following methods of seeing that each apprentice has a copy on punched paper, the method to depend on what type of experience would be most valuable to your class at this stage. (1) Dictate the article to be transcribed as with previous dictation. (2) Dictate it to a dictating machine if one is available, requiring each apprentice to note on her transcript the over-all time. (3) Make a master copy from this Guide (or have a student do this) for each apprentice to copy as a straight typewriting project to be timed. (4) Assign the copying of the article to one or more students as a duplicating machine project to provide copies for the entire class. 

"MAY I ASK A QUESTION, PLEASE?" 

by 

A Private Secretary

Asking a question is an act of intelligence/--that is, if the question is intelligently asked./ A question well asked/ may bring out important, relevant facts./ A reasoning spirit of inquiry is dignified./ There is scorn attached to the person about whom the phrase is used:/ She thinks she knows it all./ Such a secretary does not investigate/ and does not grow in her ability to carry through work by herself. She has to be given explicit directions/ that will be sure to cover every point./ The question mark is a door that is opened often by the secretary with initiative./ 

Now, one of the people of whom the secretary must ask questions is her employer./ She will ask him a question only when it is necessary to have his own answer/ as her guide in carrying on her work./ This means that she is bound to use her own common sense,/ her own initiative,/ and her own best judgment, as far as they reach, and she must know when she need not

UNIT V 

bother her employer/ because some associate or intermediate superior officer/ can answer the question./

With respect to questioning an employer/ a secretary will have several points in mind:/ 

I ask?/ 

1. What do I want to know and why?/ 

2. Is it necessary for me to ask my employer?/ If not, whom shall 

3. When shall I ask?/ 

4. How shall I ask? And how much shall I ask?/ 

5. How shall I carry myself, when I ask?/ 

6. What shall I do with the answer?/

The secretary does not sit down, of course,/ to go over these questions deliberately with relation to every slight query./ But she comes to acquire a swift, keen judgment,/ both of the necessity and of the fitness of a question./ 

Suppose, for example, that Mr. Fielding,/ the adjuster for a firm,/ asks for an appointment with Mr. Brown the last of the morning./ Mr. Brown's secretary, Miss Slade,/ feels sure that Mr. Brown will wish to see Mr. Fielding, but she does not know whether he will be free./ She therefore goes to 

Mr. Brown's desk to ask him./ Involved with this simple act is her experience with the foregoing six questions./ 

1. What do I want to know and why?/ Miss Slade wants to know whether 

Mr. Brown's conference,/ already called for 10:30 A.M.,/ is expected to last until noon;/ if so, Mr. Fielding must be put off./ 

2. Is it necessary for me to ask Mr. Brown himself?/ Miss Slade knows that Mr. Brown is the sole judge/ of the length of time that this conference promises to take/ because it was called by him./ If, on the other hand, Mr. Brown had been called to this conference, let us say,/ by the treasurer of the concern/ on some matters as yet unknown to Mr. Brown,/ Miss 

Slade might rather ask the treasurer's secretary to find out,/ if possible,/ how long he believed the conference would last./ In either case,/ Miss Slade is now able to judge whether or not Mr. Fielding may be told that Mr. Brown can probably see him/ at about 11:45 A.M./--the time asked for by Mr. Fielding./

SECTION 16 

115 

3. When shall I ask?/ Miss Slade may consider it prefectly advisable to ask Mr. Brown immediately./ She may even hold Mr. Fielding or his secretary at the other end of the telephone./ She knows that when Mr. Brown is at his desk/ he is ready for essential interruptions from her as a rule./ But notice those words/ as a rule/--because they are an important part of the procedure of interrupting./ Suppose that Miss Slade finds that Mr. Brown is at his desk/ but that he is trying to close in his work on a financial statement/ to be presented at the approaching conference./ This entirely alters Miss 

Slade's reply to question 3./ She returns to the telephone and says,/ "Mr. 

Fielding? I am sorry to have kept you waiting,/ especially as I find it impossible to interrupt Mr. Brown at the moment./ May I call you just before 10:30 

A.M., when I shall be able to catch Mr. Brown for you?"/ 

4. How shall I ask? And how much shall I ask?/ In this case, then,/ at 10:25 A.M. Miss Slade goes to Mr. Brown's desk. She phrases her question simply,/ directly,/ and inclusively:/ "Will your conference at half-past ten o'clock/ be over in time for you to see Mr. Thomas Fielding this morning/ at quarter to twelve o'clock?"/ Notice that she calls the attention of Mr.

Brown's busy mind/ to the fact that he is having his conference shortly,/ that he must weigh its probable length,/ and that this is Mr. Thomas Fielding/ (the adjuster)/ and not Mr. Henry Fielding/ (the automobile salesman)/ who wishes to see him. That brief question of hers holds all this information as a basis for his answer. If a question is worth asking, it is worth asking well./ 

Further than this,/ Miss Slade will think ahead far enough to follow his answer with/ "Then shall I remind Mr. Fielding to bring the data/ for the complaint that came to your desk this morning?/ I will transcribe the memorandum you have dictated for him/ and get it to him before he leaves his department,/ if you wish."/ This second question shows that Miss Slade knows what was dictated to her/ and has her mind on the Links which the dictation will lead ahead to./ She is attending to this new Chain/ and does what she can to speed the forging of the next Link/ when these two men are getting together on other matters/ anyway./ 

5. How shall I carry myself when I ask?/ The words that carry a question are important/ and to be carefully chosen, it is true./ More than that,/ the person who asks a question is important as far as the following are concerned:/ manner of entering the room,/ posture,/ voice,/ attention to answer,/ taking leave./ Miss Slade's mission is clear to her mind, and she can be

UNIT V 

natural and self-assured./ She opens Mr. Brown's door quietly,/ walks with ease to his desk,/ holds her head alert,/ awaits his sign of recognition,/ uses a low, unhurried voice with clear enunciation,/ attends carefully to his reply,/ and, unless he indicates that he has need for further help from her,/ turns and leaves the room with poise./ She is not self-conscious because she is going about her business and her mind is on that./ 

6. What shall I do with the answer?/ In this instance, she will go back to her desk,/ intending to call Mr. Fielding immediately,/ but let us imagine that an incoming call rings her telephone./ It is a call that necessitates her looking up an address in her file./ No matter how many interruptions lie in wait to divert her attention,/ she must still hold in mind/ that Mr. Fielding needs to know about his appointment at quarter to twelve o'clock/ and that she has promised to put through the memorandum dictated to him./ By the time she is free to telephone to Mr. Fielding,/ it may seem advisable to read this memorandum to him from her notebook./ 

There is one more part to question 6./ Mr. Brown has said that he will see Mr. Fielding at quarter to twelve o'clock./ Therefore Miss Slade has the duty of going to Mr. Brown's conference/ and reminding him that Mr. Fielding is waiting,/ when that hour has come./ Such an interruption is always made unobtrusively and depends on the relative importance of the two conferences/--a fine distinction that a secretary must learn to draw./ A secretary helps to make her employer's day easier./ If she is both precise and tactful,/ she increases the value of his hours./ She 

Watch Miss Slade a little more,/ as she handles this situation./ has by this time transcribed the memorandum read an hour before over the telephone./ On this she has put Mr. Brown's initials with hers beneath./ She hands this to Mr. Fielding,/ attached to a copy of the letter to which the memorandum refers./

Notice that all this procedure hangs on the one question raised by Mr. Fielding/ about an appointment/ and the two questions asked by Miss Slade of Mr. Brown./ Questions lead to forming Links in the office;/ Chains require thinking, and thinking often involves asking./ Miss Slade did not "guess that it would be all right" for Mr. Fielding to see Mr. Brown./ She did not know;/ therefore, she found out./ Miss Slade had not worked for Mr. Brown a week before he exclaimed to her,/ "Oh, I see that you know enough to ask questions!"/

SECTION 16 

117 

Key to Parts of the Experience 

The apprentices should have a week for the assignment after they have their copies of the article in order to follow the instructions in Part C. In discussing the questions, you will have many opinions differing from the key suggestions given here. These should be merely a guide to the proper analysis.

Part A. Question 1. Poorly word ed from point of view of both clarity and correct phrasing. Part about pamphlet probably need not have been asked at all. 

Since Mr. Brown is to call again, it need not be asked at once. Possible form: "Mr.

Gordon Brown of the printing company called to know whether or not you have come to a decision on the new work. I said we would notify him within a few days. Was I right in telling him he could have the pamphlet proofs this afternoon since you are working on them now?" 

Question 2. A good question. Need not interrupt employer unless he is to be tied up for the rest of the morning. 

Question 3. An intelligent question, for there should be no mistake in a medicine and the secretary has obviously done all she can to answer it herself. 

Probably can wait to be asked. 

Question 4. An unnecessary question. Since the employer gave the secretary the book to which he referred, it is clear that he merely miswrote the year. If she is in any doubt, the secretary could at the next opportunity say that she made the change. 

Question 5. An intelligent question showing that the secretary knows what is going on in the office and has a clear idea of her employer's schedule. It must be asked at once. 

Question 6. A poor question. The secretary should have looked at the appointments before asking this question. If she did not know how long the previous appointment would take, she should have promised to call Mrs. Allen back when an opportunity came to ask her employer that question. 

Possible question: "Will your appointment with Miss Sargent on Thursday at half-past ten o'clock take more than half an hour? 

Mrs. Allen would like to come at ten o'clock."

Part B. 

Question 1. This question could wait for almost the full hour in the hope of a normal interruption to the conference. The secretary might write out the question and hand it to her employer if she knows that the suggestion will be entirely new to him. Possible form: "The mayor has asked you to serve on a committee to survey the traffic problem. He would like an immediate answer so that he can have the list in the afternoon papers. 11 Question 2. In many cases the secretary in this situation would suggest that the salesman leave printed matter this time and make an appointment for later when she knows her employer will be free. If it so happens that the salesman is from out of town and cannot come again soon, she might interrupt her employer with this question: "Would you care to take a few minutes to look at this window fixture [giving him marked pamphlet]? The salesman is leaving town this noon, and the fixture does seem to be what you have been looking for."

Question 3. This question should be asked at once, but, in asking, the secretary should volunteer to look up trains, notify her employer's wife, and possibly go to the bank for money. If she has the needed timetable at her desk, she might take a few minutes to find the best train before informing her employer of the situation. 

Possible form: "Mrs. Banks has just telephoned to say that she must leave immediately for Peterborough. She wants you to look up trains and meet her at the station with some money. Shall I get her for you now, or will you wait until I find the information about trains? Shall you need to have a check cashed?" 

Question 4. The secretary in this situation should take the name and telephone number of the caller and promise to let her know the answer. This may require considerable tact on the part of the secretary, but a request of this kind should not intrude on an employer's business hours. It should be asked only when the secretary

UNIT V 

knows that her employer can for a moment dismiss business thoughts. Possible form: "Mrs. Blank came in for your donation to the hospital fund. Shall you give five hundred dollars as usual? She asked particularly to be allowed to publish your name this year. 11 

Question 5. This personal situation is particularly difficult for the secretary who has a feeling of responsibility both for her employer's interests and toward her family. Before going to her employer, she must make a hasty plan as to how much of the work in hand she can finish in a few minutes, what she might be able to turn over to office associates (if she has any), and what she will have to turn back to her employer to do, or leave undone. 

Possible form: "I am sorry to interrupt you, but Mother has just called, asking me to drive her to Belleville to my grandmother, who is critically ill. I can finish the letters in a few minutes, and Miss Jones will copy the article. Can the statistics wait, or may I bring them back for you to do?"

Question 6. This situation requires an immediate interruption. Possible form: "Mr. Blank is calling from Springfield. He would like to talk with you about so-and-so at 5:30. Can you stay to see him, and shall I call Mrs. Employer to say that you will be late?" 

Note. If you find that the apprentices ask a great many unnecessary questions, you might make a list of such questions to discuss with the class. 

Sec. Exp. 16-2. The first part of the Ex perience demands "finding out" a fact as quickly as possible. This textbook uses from time to time common abbreviations that should become familiar to the apprentices, with the expectation that the students will look up any abbreviations which they do not know. Forms for invitations such as the one asked for here are not given space in this book, since standard suggestions are studied in the usual courses in English. If your students need to review their knowledge of such forms, add that factor to this Experience as a piece of definite research for immediate use. 

This will offer experience in reference work for the right form to use for a given purpose.

Discuss the Links involved in giving this luncheon: getting into touch with the speaker and settling on a convenient noon for him; making up the list of guests to be invited; keeping a check list for the replies as they come in with either a "yes" or a "no"; telephoning to the place of meeting for a small room, stating the number attending, including the host and the speaker; and after the luncheon paying the bill. 

Each guest has to handle the Link of giving a reply; and, if he accepts, his secretary will enter the engagement on his calendar pad, as the Link to remind him.

Sec. Exp. 16-3. Part A. In this Experi ence, as in more involved Chains, stress should be laid on the secretary's own responsibility for carrying through.

SECTION 17. EFFICIENT EQUIPMENT AS AN ACCELERATOR 

 1. Although this section emphasizes equipment, stress should be laid on the fact that its practical usefulness depends on the skill of the secretary. 

2. Familiarity with the equipment on the market is a necessary asset to a secretary, but she should also know how to make the best use of it and how to care for it. 

3. Discuss the fact that efficient equipment increases the speed of the secretary only if she herself uses it efficiently. 

4. The apprentice should realize that in going to a position she must be ready to make the equipment provided fit her needs. Only when she discovers an essential lack should she begin to ask for new equipment. 

5. If the secretary understands the nature and purpose of varied mechanical devices, she will not need to say, "I have never used one of these." 

6. Devices of many kinds and sizes should be familiar to your apprentices, as pointed out in the introduction to this Guide. 

7. If the secretary in charge of a supply closet, large or small, has her supplies in such order that she can lay her hands on what she wants "almost in the dark," she will avoid having to move one thing after another to find what she needs. 

8. For her own sake as well as that of her employer, the secretary should practice reasonable economy with supplies. An employer notices the difference between an extravagant and an economical secretary, even though he may not say so. 

9. Bring home the ordering of supplies by showing to the class actual purchase orders from the file of the supply store or school offices. Point out that these have been kept in carbon. 

10. If the secretary is aware of the small ways and means to lessen noise, she may be able to save wear and tear on her own nerves and on those of others. 

11. The secretary should go to her position prepared to notice what her secondary duties may be. She will rarely be told what they are. 

12. Call to the apprentices' attention that unsharpened pencils and an unemptied ash tray often "get on an employer's nerves. The secretary should never forget her small tasks in her absorption in the large tasks. 11 13. In the use of color for distinctive purposes, the secretary should keep each purpose definitely in mind, or the object of this device is lost. 

14. The discussion of this question should encourage apprentices to be "curious" about the files, machines, methods of work, and so forth, in a new position; but they must be warned that too much curiosity on the first day may create an unfavorable impression. 

15. The makes of typewriters and their respective advantages will no doubt have been discussed previously in class. By this time, each apprentice should know which she prefers and why. 

16. An opportunity should be given the apprentices to renew their acquaintance with machines that have already been studied and to become familiar with others. 

17. Even though the secretary may rarely have full responsibility for the choice of stationery, she should know what is in the best taste and be able to buy wisely when this is one of her duties.

UNIT V 

18. Urge the apprentices to apply each of the seven tests for choosing a pen before purchasing. The pen that the student buys now should be selected with the idea that it will last many years. 

19. The apprentice should know the types of these smaller articles that are available and be able to choose wisely.

Tours to supply stores, either by the individual or by the class as a whole under your guidance, should make this chapter real. The illustration on page 377 of the textbook should be discussed in detail in order to arouse the student's interest in small aids. 

20. The apprentice must not think that she should buy many different kinds or sizes of labels in order to be well supplied. She should be resourceful with what is at hand. 

21. Acquaintance with various fasteners leads the secretary to choose the right one for each specific purpose. 

22. In many offices there is little need for a cutting board, but the secretary must have a good pair of scissors. 

23. The apprentice should not think that she needs every kind of container, but when she takes a position she should be able to choose properly for whatever has to be kept. 

24. The date stamp must be emphasized as the secretary's most important time indicator. This must always be set at the correct date. 

25. The list of reference matter in

Chapter 17 might even be memorized by the apprentices. Copies of as many good reference books as possible should be available to the apprentices during training so that they may become familiar with their use. 

26. If the best is impracticable in the office to which she goes, the apprentice should be prepared to modify her idea of furniture "necessary" for the secretary's health. 

27. Dusters and material for cleaning her typewriter are the definite responsibility of the secretary, no matter how much of the office cleaning is done by others. 

28. The apprentice should have clear ideas about the most convenient arrangement of an office so that her good judgment may prompt her to make intelligent suggestions for necessary improvements.

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 17-1 (Service Book V). Dictate the following Handy Hints, and add others that will facilitate filing. We should remember that even the most commonly known hints are necessary for the beginner. 

1. If the drawers of your file do not run in and out readily,/ find out where and how to oil the little wheels and runways./ Do not use too much oil on a file, a typewriter, or any office equipment,/ as oil gathers dirt./ 

2. Adjust the "follower" at the back of a drawer when necessary/ so that (1) you will not find your guides and folders slumping/ but can keep them at a useful upright angle/ and (2)/ you will not find your folders too crowded for convenience/ in taking them out or replacing them./ Expanding, hammocklike inserts/ are convenient to hold in place groups of folders, circulars, and catalogs./ 

3. Lift the entire folder out when inserting a paper as well as when removing one./ It is a mistake to attempt to save time/ by trying to drop a paper into

SECTION 17 

121 a folder as it stands in the drawer./ Lay the folder on a portable file shelf hooked onto a drawer/ or on some other flat surface/ that is large enough to prevent spilling papers out of their order/ and is at an easy height for your eyes./ 

4. It is common practice to keep at the front of a folder/ a single sheet of information that must be very frequently referred to./ Print a conspicuous 

KF/ (Keep Front)/ with red pencil in the upper right corner of such a sheet as a reminder that it is to be kept out of its normal/ alphabetical/ position. 

When you remove such a paper from its alphabetical place,/ be sure to put in a cross-reference sheet/ leading to the special location./ 

5. The top of your file acts as a convenient flat shelf for storage./ Reference books may be kept there between book ends./ Whatever is out in the open should be kept dusted and in order./ 

6. To avoid eye strain,/ try to have your files where there is proper daylight or proper artificial light./ The use of a filing stool is advisable for working at the lower drawers/ to prevent awkward reaching/ and to vary the position of the body during long periods of filing./ 

When reports are ready, have a classroom exchange of hints gathered from other sources.

Sec. Exp. 17-2. The individual scrapbook to take away from the course will prove valuable, and the Experience itself integrates scrapbook use, indexing, acquisitiveness, and judgment, emphasizing at the same time the gradual learning by experience necessary in every secretarial position. For this last purpose this project should be carried throughout the remainder of the course. The books should be called in first about two weeks after the Experience has been assigned, to make sure that a good start has been made. 

If this cannot be an individual project, an accumulation of stationery samples should be a class project so that the apprentices will become familiar with actual supplies. In any case, you may expect from year to year to have students contribute to a school collection, which should become of increasing instructional interest in your course. 

Discuss with the class the relative merits and purposes of the samples, discovering with them when some inexpensive paper that may "look all right" offers poor economy and when a moderately priced paper will be as satisfactory as the most expensive. 

It will be vividly to the point if you can dictate to the class a wholesale or retail price list of the very materials they are using in this course--the regular typing paper, scratch paper, typewriter erasers, fragile carbon paper, pencils, shorthand notebooks, manila folders. Stress in the best way for your particular class the value of the materials they are using, whether they pay for them directly or indirectly.

Such a knowledge should breed a respect for supplies that will be carried over into the office.

Sec. Exp. 17-3. Part A. Practical appli cation of this Experience regarding the supply closet can be made: (1) by class inspection and criticism of the way in which matter of all kinds is being kept in the classroom; (2) by a tour of inspection

UNIT V 

behind the counter of the school supply store, where supplies in quantity should be seen stacked in accessible order; and (3) by a visit about the offices of the school, where individual secretaries should be found keeping their supplies in different ways according to their respective needs.

Part B. (1) In advance discussion of this Experience the student should understand clearly that this imagined supply closet cannot be set up to perfection but that the graduate should carry into the office a "feel" for good supply keeping. It is important for the student to recognize that, for the most part, things of a kind should appear on a single shelf. The students may be instructed to copy from Secretarial Experience 17-3 in their Manuals the last sentence under (1) to appear as a

Handy Hint in their Service Books. (2) Lists should be exchanged among students so that they may profit by others' ideas and may check each other's errors in alphabetizing or in omitting items. A few of the best indexes may then be posted on the bulletin board.

UNIT VI. THE SECRETARY UNDERSTANDS THE WORTH OF WORDS AND FIGURES SECTION 18. PLEASE PUT IT IN WRITING 

 11 

1. There are many points to be discussed with relation to the need for "putting it in writing, but most of them lead to the fact that important Links of business should not be trusted to the memory. 

2. The apprentices must form the habit of making notes of the smallest details. For this reason the words Put it in writing cannot be used too often during the study of this section. 

3. The apprentice must be impressed with the fact that in all important matters a signed statement protects her employer and often herself. 

4. A matter of such importance that it must be telegraphed needs to be verified by the signed letter of confirmation; a telephone conversation often needs such a letter to eliminate the possibility of misunderstanding. 

5. The apprentices should recognize that skill in handwriting and printing is needed by the secretary as much as skill in shorthand and typewriting, even though the former skills are used less often. 

6. Apprentices who have much difficulty in reading the writing of others should make opportunities to practice such reading frequently. An employer cannot be patient with a secretary who wastes his time and hers in deciphering his writing. 

7. Bring out the fact that in business many people are known only by their signatures. For this reason the signature must not be varied. An amusing comparison might be made by considering what the results would be if a person frequently changed his appearance. 

8. While the student is learning the protection provided by signed papers, she must also realize the responsibility involved in signing her own name. She should make it a rule to read all papers thoroughly before she signs them. 

9. The apprentice should be made to see the printed form as a concise way of setting down information, if such information is complete and correct. 

10. When a secretary is asked to take notes at a conference, she should be doubly conscious of the responsibility of her position. She must train herself to get down the important points, sometimes with exact wording and at other times in outline form. 

11. The secretary's responsibility does not end with the last word of a conference. She must remember to carry away necessary reports and be able to carry through many of the details she has entered in her notebook during the meeting. 

12. Making an accurate copy of material already in readable form calls for particular attention, for it is easy to let the attention wander. The apprentices should not allow themselves to fall into that habit, no matter how accurate their work is. Any copying requires intelligent attention. 

13. Clarity of setup is as important in a long manuscript as in a short advertisement. The reader should be helped to read by the form in which the secretary puts the material. 

14. When a secretary has a long piece of work to get through, she should take time to arrange herself and her papers so that she can do the most efficient work. If the apprentices follow the outline in the text a few times in their own work, they will 123

UNIT VI 

soon see how they can save time by efficient planning before they start. 

15. The eight simple indications of changes in rough draft should be familiar to all the students. Other marks should be added to the list as these are encountered. 

Key to Secretarial Experiences Sec. Exp. 18-1 (Service Book III). 

Part A. 

The student should be able to read attentively and follow explicitly the instruction about the insert to be made. At this stage of the course, any student failing to follow so straightforward a direction, because she "didn't notice it" or "didn't think it meant that," may be helped perhaps by being asked to return to the Experience as though she had never seen it before and then follow it through with "her mind on her job."

Part B. This work should stress again the variety of skills desirable for the secretary.

3. The Western Union money-order blank is to be filled in with the student's city or town for "Office" and the sum of money in words and then in figures within parentheses, the message to read, Payment for purchase referred to in your letter of yesterday. Student's name, address, and telephone number to be entered. 

4. Show the apprentices that a nice left alignment is expected in each column of work-sheet Form 18-2e. Should a student type a requisition that cannot be corrected legibly, she should set up a new slip. Warn the class of this in advance, stating that blanks numbered in sequence are so printed for the purpose of numerical filing and it is not expected that such blanks will be wasted as an allowance for "copying over. 

This is a clear example of "right the first time." 

5. Since a subject file is not being kept, these cross-reference sheets, Work-sheet Forms 18-2f, need not be filed by the student, though one may well be kept in the student's collection of stationery and printed forms, as a sample of a standard cross-reference sheet that might well be copied in the office. Possible subjects from this article for cross-reference:

Starting the Day, Preparing Employer's Desk, Holding the Day Together, The Lunch Hour, Before Closing the Door, Preparing for the Next Day. 

6. Each form should have space at upper right corner marked for date, followed by lines for name of borrower, department, and name and date of borrowed catalog. Α few setups may be printed on the blackboard for class criticism until one best one is settled upon. This may be used for a duplicating machine project, if desired.

Summary. This Experience should be reviewed as a whole to show the students the variety of useful printed forms available and the need for completeness and accuracy in filling them out if they are to serve their respective purposes. Since the forms used in this Experience commonly cause anxiety to beginning students, you may wish to make up similar problems to repeat this drill.

Sec. Exp. 18-3. 

Part A. The principles

SECTION 19 

125 illustrated in the text may be more thoroughly learned by their use on pages of the students' own typing. Dictation of straight matter may be given with the requirement that errors (if any) should not be erased but should have corrections indicated with the proper marks. Such work trains attention for catching errors, which must be recognized as a secretarial duty. In relation to the study of this chapter, we may again teach the apprentice that the employer's standard of typing is "usable" work, which must be either "right the first time" or corrected to be right. In your own correcting of papers during the remainder of the course, you should feel free to use proofreader's marks to indicate the nature of errors, for example, by running a ring around a comma that has been struck for a period, by using the delete sign, the space sign, the close-up sign, the transposition sign. These most common marks may be met by the secretary whose employer uses them on her first typed draft of an article that he has in the making.

Part B. The real purpose of Part B 

is to acquaint the apprentice with this ready guide in the desk dictionary, in case of later need.

SECTION 19. 

HELPING YOUR READER 

TO THINK 

1. As the course nears its end, the apprentice should study her finished work with an increasingly critical eye, keeping in mind the principles of clearness, comprehensiveness, and exactness. 

2. The apprentices should recognize that, besides the ability to write good letters, the secretary who composes letters for her employer must have a thorough understanding of the business. 

3. The ability to write letters that "sound like the employer" cannot be developed by memorizing his letters. The secretary must try to put herself in the place of her employer, so that her letters will reflect his point of view. 

4. The student should study the possibilities of the typewriter for effective display. 

5. Setting out items within an otherwise straightforward piece of typewriting offers a good illustration of using variety to attract the eye. 

6. A taɔulation should be thought of as information stripped of all its language accessories, such as conjunctions and articles. 

7. Much information that the reader could not take time to assimilate from a straight piece of typewriting may be gleaned from a tabulation. 

8. To understand a graph requires even less time on the part of a reader than to understand a tabulation. Like the tabulation, the graph must be exact in detail in order to convey the correct information. 

Key to Secretarial Experiences (Note. The three Experiences for Section 

19 may seem simple to the student at this stage in the course. Their importance to mature thinking lies in their direct correlation of the study of English with a variety of often repeated secretarial duties, each of which involves specific expression. 

Secretarial Experience 19-1 trains ability to decide upon a single form and to hold to that form consistently throughout the "display" of one matter. Secretarial Experience 19-2 gives all too little experience in segregating single items for ease in separate handling and in making clear and courteous transcriptions grow out of concise skeleton dictation. Secretarial Experience 19-3 gives important practice in independent "digging out" of proper mail signals from notes in shorthand; the paragraph on "Buildings" which is typically "handed over" to the secretary to embed in a dictated letter gives an exercise in a muchneglected kind of what might be described to the students as "harlequin" dictation.

Such transcription tests attention and ability to follow instructions.)

Sec. Exp. 19-1 (Service Book IV). Class discussion of this application of trait

UNIT VI 

necessity to ordinary daily living would best be handled with a light touch. It is advisable for the secretary to recognize, however, that she should observe and imitate the best in her associates in the office and that she in turn will be observed. 

This Experience should show the ambitious student the difference between skilled typing of copy already set in good display, such as a good typist can do, and intelligent arrangement of ideas in good display, such as may be required of the secretary.

Sec. Exp: 19-2. The spirit of this Experi ence involves many principles of secretarial procedure. Assurance must be gained in transcribing "right the first time" from dictation that is not given in entirety. If any apprentice has difficulty with this, she may be advised to practice taking shorthand notes of swift radio speeches, catching the gist and then transcribing full sentences direct from the notes.

Sec. Exp. 19-3. In dictating these letters, omit mention of the mail signals Key to Letters for Dictation given in parentheses, since in each case the apprentice should note the need for the signal without help. Either Subject or In re or Re should be considered acceptable on letters requiring a subject line, as the distinction between the two is made by individual employers, not by definite rules, with the exception that legal work favors In re. Should a useful signal be entered on any communication, it may be approved, of course, even though it does not appear in the Guide. 

1. To Mr. James A. Farnham, Jr., 952 Market St., San Francisco, Calif. 

Dear Jim:

In going through a lot of pamphlets,/ I found the enclosed notice of a course in psychology for executives./ I immediately thought of your desire to gather some definite information/ about how to get to be an executive./ As a rule, I don't think that a person can be taught to be an executive./ If the ability is there, it will grow with experience./ However, if anyone knows anything about training for important positions,/ it is this professor./ He has been an executive himself and still hobnobs with them,/ even though he has gone into teaching./ The prospectus for the course reads very well./ 

If you do take it, I'll be interested to hear what you gain from it./ (P) With kindest regards to the members of your family, I am (Enc. 1) 

Sincerely yours,/ (Note to teacher. At the end of the dictation ask to have this letter marked 

Personal. This should appear on the envelope only.)

Attention of S. G. Davey 

Dear Mr. Davey:

I should like to call your attention/ to a condition that many of our employees feel presents an unnecessary menace to motorists./ At the corner of our parking space is a tree/ which completely obstructs the view of the main thoroughfare from our drivers/ and in turn conceals our drivers from those on the highway./ Will you not at your convenience come and study the situation,/ preferably during the five o'clock rush hour?/ We feel confident that you will agree that the tree ought to be cut down./

SECTION 20. ACCOUNTS MUST BE ACCOUNTED FOR  

1. The apprentice must go to her first position with the firm conviction that she will not let herself lapse ethically in the slightest way. Discuss the fact that one small dishonest act seems often to lead to a second, thus tending to develop a trait which is difficult to overcome. 

2. Practice in handling a petty cash account is given in a Secretarial Experience. The apprentices should understand the principles of a petty cash account thoroughly before carrying through the Experience. 

3. Various forms of checkbooks should be shown to the class and discussed as to methods of use and convenience. 

4. A discussion of counter checks should include the warning to use them only when necessary and to be certain to record them. 

5. The apprentices will be helped in their study of this chapter if you can obtain samples of various banking forms. 

Particularly they should remember to look for the identifying number of the bank on a check. 

6. This process should be gone over with the students until they all understand it thoroughly. Reconciling the bank statement is a frequent secretarial duty; the student should here learn the procedure for her personal account as well. 

7. Careful checking and rechecking should be stressed in all discussion of the secretary's responsibility toward money matters. She must cultivate attention in reading across from the data she is using to the copy she is making. For instance, she cannot be depended on to make up a pay roll merely because her typing and her totals seem right. Her employer must depend on her to check across from record cards to entries with unerring accuracy. The beginner should understand that superficial verifying of typing is not a saving of time.

The employer pays his secretary to relieve him of the necessity of "looking over" her work. 

8. Ordinary ability to deal with figures and intelligence in respect to the subjects treated in this chapter are necessary for every apprentice, even though she knows she is not to handle accounting in the large sense of the word. She must be ready and willing to do what is required of her, even though she "doesn't really like arithmetic."

Key to Secretarial Experiences 

Sec. Exp. 20-1. Before the apprentices begin to make their accounts, they should be given an opportunity to ask questions about the sample account in the textbook, pages 

485 to 488. The procedure there must be thoroughly understood if the Experience is to be worth while. Emphasize particularly the principle that once a petty cashbox has been started, the list of expenses plus the cash on hand should always equal the original amount received. Students and often

UNIT VI 

secretaries themselves have varied and irregular habits regarding receiving and spending cash for personal expenses. For example, many of them are used to receiving a stated amount each week regardless of what they have left from the week before; others must make a request for money when they need it, but these requests are usually for an even number of dollars, such as $1, $5, or $10. Therefore, even those students who are in the habit of keeping a cash account are accustomed to starting a new week of accounting with what they happen to have left and adding to it what they may receive. This is a perfectly correct procedure for a personal account, but one that makes it all the more difficult for these students to understand the need of what might be called the "fixed totals" for the petty cash account. 

If there is an adding machine available, it would be well to have the apprentices use it to check their figuring to gain experience. Show them that they can strike a subtotal of the Paid column and add the cash on hand to check the account. 

If this figure equals the Received column, the account is correct and needs no further checking. In this example, of course, the students will first have to find the cash on hand by subtracting. The key is found on page 131. 

Sec. Exp. 20-2. If you find that the apprentices need more practice of this sort, let them make several other lists of their own amounting to various sums. 

Key to Tabulation

Sec. Exp. 20-3. Although this Experience may seem long and exacting in its detail, it should not prove too difficult, when taken step by step.

Check No. 245 SECTION 21. WILL YOU FIND OUT --? 

1. An apprentice who does a hard piece of research for the first time should remember the phrase intelligent determination. 

2. The twelve habits will be found to involve many of the secretarial traits discussed in other connections. The apprentice should see by this that the search for information calls into play all her faculties. 

3. Any help you can give the apprentices by having in the classroom the reference books mentioned in the text will be worth while. The students should also make special effort to look up the various books for themselves. 

4. Comparatively few persons look in the index of a book before trying to find what they want by turning pages. The index habit should be discussed and practiced by the apprentices. Now that they are acquainted with a large part of Secretarial Efficiency, a study of the index should show clearly how a topic leads to desired information and teach that a good index is planned for its helpfulness to the reader. Secretarial Experience 21-1 outlines such study.

SECTION 21 

133 5. Familiarity with the types of informational literature is essential to the student, even though she cannot become immediately familiar with the actual books. 

6. In books to which they refer frequently, the apprentices should make a habit of keeping markers of some sort where they are most needed. 

7. The Service Book will have been discussed earlier in the course, but it should be reviewed here from the point of · view of its permanent value to the secretary.

UNIT VII. THE SECRETARY OFFERS AN ACCEPTABLE PERSONALITY SECTION 22. 

CONFIDENTIAL MEANS CONFIDENTIAL  

1. In a discussion of this subject, the fact that the secretary may not know precisely when and why it is important to keep a matter confidential should be brought out. To avoid danger, the apprentice should determine to say nothing of the business outside business hours. 

2. Many of the matters to be found on this list will be the hardest for the young secretary to keep to herself, for "news" is exciting to her. 

3. Urge the apprentices to practice this habit of dropping business when they are away from it. 

4. The advice in Chapter 22 in the last paragraph under "Turning a Deaf Ear" should be emphasized as something to be remembered always. 

5. The secretary must feel that, if a bit of information leaks out merely because of her facial expression, she is fully responsible. She should recognize that this is sometimes a more insidious way of talking than the use of words. 

6. No doubt, the apprentices have already experienced the trouble of telephoning in a room with other people. Students should be advised to accept such situations now in order to study how to carry on necessarily guarded conversations. 

7. The Personal signal on a letter should never escape the secretary's notice.

The apprentice should understand that this rule holds even when an employer is away, unless she has been otherwise instructed. 

8. When an employer conducts certain personal affairs through his secretary, the latter may perhaps think that she is at liberty to mention them to his friends. The apprentice should be disabused of any such thought. 

9. In summing up the work of this. chapter, attention may well be called to the fact that a person who otherwise keeps confidences betrays her trust if she feels that she "must tell her one best friend."

Note. The suggestion may be made to the class that, when a man writes to relatives or friends whom he addresses by the first name, it is still valuable to have the name and address on the letter for filing purposes. If an employer objects to this, the name and address can, of course, be entered on the file copy. At the close of the dictation, discuss the letters from the "confidential" point of view. Bring out various reasons why each one is confidential. For instance, the canceled engagement of a club speaker should not be made known until a substitute is procured; the loan to Charles Macdonald must be kept secret; the fact that Mr. Waterman is on the point of investing in some oil stock, even though the name is not given, might cause him annoyance from brokers if nothing else.

SECTION 23. WHO'S WHO IN THE OFFICE 

The ap- 1. In any but the smallest office there is bound to be an exchange of information, papers, and so forth, among various members of the secretarial staff. prentice should not go to a position with the notion that she will need to deal with one person only. 

2. Apprentices can now form the habit of learning the names of people with whom they come in contact as preparation for the necessity of doing so in an office. 

3. The proper personal as well as business attitude toward her fellow workers should be instilled in the mind of the apprentice who is seeking her first position. Hastily,/ 

4. From observation of your students in their relations with fellow students, you will be able to single out those who need to consider seriously what their attitude toward future associates will be. 

This question offers a pertinent opportunity for the college teacher to speak of the responsibility of the college graduate in her bearing toward those less privileged. She can go far to remove that sus picion, often amounting to distrust, which arises from jealousy, envy, and an inferiority complex on the part of associates who have had fewer advantages. 

5. The apprentices must learn to be open-minded to the ideas of others and at the same time to be tactful in carrying out their own ideas when the latter are better for the business in hand. 

6. In the discussion of the appearance and habits of the secretary, call attention to the fact that the apprentices themselves often misjudge people on first meeting them because of some superficial characteristic that should not be in evidence. 

7. Emphasize the fact that "right living" is essential to anyone who hopes to work up to a position of trust. Exercise and rest must find a place in the secretary's schedule. 

8. Today in educating the secretary, we cannot afford to overlook the practical, forward trend of the art of psychiatry, wisely practiced. In leading institutions of learning, complicated cases are being dealt with professionally. Deans and advisers are recognizing that what employers are demanding under the inclusive name of "personality traits" requires

SECTION 23 

141 specialized attention. The study of "attitudes" and "behavior" is an everyday matter in its significance for the secretary who is to be recommended for and to hold a position. We learn that many secretaries in mid-career are finding it necessary to seek consultants in mental health. Personal tangles too deep-seated and intricate for her to solve or even to recognize for herself may make a secretary "feel that everyone in the office is against her, that she "just can't do anything that seems to suit her employer," that she "is not appreciated," that she "is just going to make up her mind not to care, that she is "bound she will make a change, no matter what happens. 11 11 11 

Physical ailments naturally accompany such an emotional state--ailments that may be relieved, perhaps, but not cured, save by a change of attitude. Such a change frequently necessitates a long course of well-directed "study" or treatment. In the office, one sees not only the inefficiency but the unhappiness of the secretary who resents criticism, takes correction of her work subjectively rather than objectively, is "hitchy" on little or no provocation. A skilled young typist who has not learned how to adjust herself to the necessities of office life can be observed making unaccustomed typing errors over a period of some hours because she could not 'stand" having an error pointed out and does not know how to "get hold of herself." She may be overconfident about her work and feel loss of faith in herself because she finds that she cannot always be perfect. Quite as probably, however, she may have an inferiority complex, so that she is cowed by correction, to which she is supersensitive.

Secretarial Efficiency emphasizes for the student the necessity of many traits that depend on the confidence and understanding that grow out of emotional stability--accuracy, adaptability, alertness, co-operativeness, poise, speed, willingness. Mature "self-management" is the first qualification for secretarial work.

This is fully implied on pages 538 to 540 and 579 to 580 of the textbook. The graduate from this course should have been helped in every intelligent way available to "handle herself." It may be noted that often a case most needing help cannot improve because the person finds it by the very nature of the need difficult to yield to the idea of self-improvement, even under the most patient and most experienced of guides. Whatever can be done in the right way to help the emotional life of the apprentice may count far more than factual information regarding her duties, which she can readily learn, if she is a vigorous, undisturbed employee. The psychiatrist is, as we know, the recognized specialist in emotions. 

9. Even during training the apprentice can learn the value of complete relaxation in the middle of the day to lessen the tension of working hours. 

10. Orderliness for her own convenience alone is not enough for the secretary. 

She must be aware of the need for leaving her work in such order that another person could take it up with clear understanding and the least possible loss of time. 

11. Some secretaries tend to assume too much authority in the absence of an employer; others need to be trained to use initiative when they are sure that they understand what should be done.

Key to Secretarial Experiences

SECTION 24. WHEN THE EMPLOYER NEEDS A SECRETARY 

1. The possibilities of securing a position in your vicinity should be thoroughly considered with relation to the discussion in the textbook. 

2. The apprentice should know that her responsibility does not end with the filing of an application and must be warned against "hounding" an employer or a placement agent to whom she has made application. 

3. Many apprentices will need to develop the trait of self-assurance for personal interviews. Students should not carry the trait so far that they overstate their qualifications for a position. It is important, too, that in interviews they make sure what the specific requirements of each position are. 

4. The four traits must be

143 constantly kept before the apprentices in their search for positions. Wise advice is needed as to when to take work that is available and when to wait for better to turn up.' 11 11 something 5. The questions listed may well be remembered by the apprentice each time she seeks a new position. 

6. The matter of salary is again something that must be balanced with other factors in considering a position. given preliminary to the dictation of this manuscript. Those in the Manual should be sufficient; but if your group requires advance guidance, the instructions may be discussed after the dictation. When instructions have been printed clearly, however, we must urge the necessity of independent understanding such as must be followed in the office.

Give a definite time limit for handing this assignment in. The time allotted may extend over several days, as a piece of work may spread over a period of time in an office, or, if you prefer, let this assignment serve as experience in a piece of rush work and expect it within a minimum of time. It is not unusual for an editor to telegraph for a manuscript to be sent im- 

7. The students must not leave school with a feeling that they are above positions where they may gain experience and be "tided over" until better opportunities offer. They must be willing to begin | mediately. Apprentices who find accurate at the bottom of the ladder and must recognize that it is their own qualities that will help them climb. 

8. Temporary work need not be scorned unless it will interfere with obtaining a permanent position--and of that it is often difficult to judge. 

9. Every apprentice should leave school with a determination to continue her study whenever she can. Discuss with the apprentices the need for careful planning of free time when such study is carried on in conjunction with a position. 

10. The students must recognize the danger of losing ground while they are neither studying nor working and be encouraged to use every means possible to keep themselves "up to the mark. 11 

11. Discuss the fact that employers naturally become suspicious of secretaries who make frequent changes in their first few years of work. 

12. If the apprentice has set forth her qualifications for a position honestly, she should go to her first day with the assurance that she will fill the position satisfactorily.

TWO FEET TO STAND ON: 

LEARNING AND THINKING 

Which woman will make the more efficient secretary: the one who has learned about secretarial work but does not apply a thinking mind to the use of that learning, or the one who has had little or no learning but is a thinking person? The answer may take you by surprise. It is: Neither. In order to have poise in her position, the secretary must stand firmly on two feet, learning and thinking. An active mind cannot take dictation without learning shorthand. An expert in shorthand cannot transcribe dictation without intelligent thinking. "Learning without thought," wrote Confucius, "is labor lost; thought without learning is perilous."

In the business world stenographers may be divided into three groups: 

The Group A girl has acquired skill and knowledge and, what is more, shows ability to think.

The Group B girl is an automaton whose shorthand is merely a mechanical skill because she does not think when she is transcribing notes.

The Group C girl likes to think things out but does not possess standard speed or accuracy in shorthand.

SECTION 24 

145 

Let us look at this Group A girl first. She thinks while she is taking dictation so that she may be ready to ask necessary questions about any detail that she does not understand and may keep her notes intelligible. Then, she thinks while she is transcribing and makes every motion count, in accordance with the ways of an intelligent person. She reads over her work to catch every possible mistake. She thinks matters over. She thinks matters out. She thinks all her work through to a careful finish. This Group A girl has taken the study of spelling seriously; she looks up words about which she has a reasonable doubt. She watches punctuation, grammar, paragraphing. To all these details she gives thought. She bends both mind and skill to her work. 

This type of girl you will find reading worth-while books and taking courses of study outside her office. This helps to keep her mind active. She stands on her technical learning and on her thinking. She keeps an even balance through her day's work. A Group A stenographer is a secretary in the making, and she stands securely in line for increase of responsibility and corresponding increase of pay.

The Group B girl is a well-organized human being from the point of view of mechanical skill, but she does not act with intelligence. She tries to excuse herself by saying, "I guess I wasn't thinking when I did that." She keeps up with the dictation of her employer, but she has no coherent idea of what he has been saying. He may suddenly interrupt himself to ask, "Does that last paragraph seem clear to you?" Miss B weakly confesses, "I don't believe 

I noticed what you were dictating." Her shorthand outlines may be quite perfect, but she was not thinking. Without thought her labor is lost. Her employer has reason to expect that her mind is on the work in hand, in tune with his, but he finds her lacking in attention.

Miss B's employer has asked her to send a copy of every communication addressed to the Glover Company to the general manager of the company for which she is working. She does this with automatic perfection with letter after letter.

Then her employer dictates a telegram to the Glover Company--a telegram of few words but of urgent import to both companies. Miss B transcribes the telegram perfectly but neglects to send a copy of this communication to the general manager. Her halting excuse is, "But it never entered my head to make a copy of a telegram for him. These matters of thought that fail to "enter her head" keep Miss B from joining the Group A employees. 11

UNIT VII 

Now the Group C girl finds thinking superior to learning. Her hands cannot keep up with her brain. Work that she calls finished is blurred with errors because of her inadequate shorthand or typewriting. Because she is not skilled, such work is turned out at an uneven and unreliable speed. Her shortcomings mean that her work falls short of what her employer expects. 

It is true that Miss C notices how a certain deal is progressing for the company, remembers facts that came to her attention months ago, and reasons intelligently about questions that affect the welfare of the business. 

In all this she is the equal of the superior Miss A. She may have the pleasing personality that is known to be essential to the secretary. She has practical ideas about arranging her work, about the ventilation of the office. 

She makes a valued suggestion about the color of paper for certain kinds of manifold copies. She thinks out a profitable advertisement for the concern. 

She is clearheaded, well-informed, quick to catch an idea. 

Yet what her employer needs is an assistant who not only understands his purpose in some crucial letter but also is definitely able to take down that letter word for word and then transcribe her notes in well-ordered form and with necessary dispatch. He must depend on her for the grind of detail hour in and hour out all the day through, and the perfection and quantity of her output have to be his serious concern. Miss C is not dependable, for she lacks proficiency in the technical skills that she should have learned as the medium of stenographic work. 

A carpenter may think out an acceptable addition to his own house. 

His wife says that she likes the idea. But no amount of planning will make that addition serviceable unless the carpenter knows how to use a hammer, a saw, and a plane for the purpose he has in mind. 

An employer tells a business agency that he has a stenographic position to be filled. By preference the agent recommends a candidate who combines the learning and the thinking that Miss A has to offer. A ready vocabulary, dependable spelling, and correct grammar help Miss A to fill the requirements. High attainment in both learning and thinking must be reached before an applicant can expect to obtain a position. The two together form her safeguard and her secret to actual enjoyment in office work. "Learning without thought is labor lost; thought without learning is perilous."

147

Dictation. To Mr. Emile B. Roulier, 498 25th St., Denver, Colo. 

Dear Mr. Roulier: For some time I have been looking for a suitable radio for our home. I have seen and tried out so many types/ that 

I am now quite confused,/ and it occurs to me that you/ may be able to offer good advice./ (P) As you know, we are all fond of music/ and want an instrument of the finest kind./ Our requirements for the machine itself are:/ selectivity for local stations,/ clear foreign reception,/ and a victrola attachment./ In addition,/ we wish to be particularly sure that the instruments of the orchestra/ come through without distortion of tone/ and that the best volume for reception/ corresponds with the best volume for our living room./ 

This, as you know, is fairly small./ (P) In expecting so much of one radio,/ of course we are prepared to pay what we must for the right machine./ Naturally,/ since we began inquiries,/ salesmen have besieged us both by telephone and in person./ This has added to our general confusion over the whole matter./ (P) I wish you were near enough to drop into our home/ and hear the different instruments under consideration./ Since you cannot do this, I would be extremely grateful for a word or two/ giving your opinion of the various makes./ Sincerely yours,/ what they can offer now as qualifications and what further training and experience they should expect to accumulate before obtaining the more highly paid positions. It is of the utmost importance that the apprentice fill in her blank completely, following all instructions.

SECTION 25. AWAKE AT THE WHEEL  

1 to 6. The questions on this chapter are designed to suggest consideration of secretarial efficiency as a whole. 

The discussion should serve not only to review the entire course, but also to sum up the outstanding qualifications that the apprentice must possess in order to be successful in her chosen vocation. Secretarial Experiences 25-2 and 25-3 and Chain

G combine to emphasize the composite picture of the efficient secretary.

Sec. Exp. 25-1. In assigning this Experi ence, you will probably find it wise to instruct the students to bring to class their ideas about what should be destroyed. In this way, you can guard your group from destroying material such as you believe may up this important subject as we draw the course to an end. Following is the alphabetical list of the traits common to both chapters: accuracy, adaptability, alertness, attention, co-operativeness, courage, courtesy, dependability, health, initiative, judgment, memory, neatness, orderliness, poise, promptness, sense of humor, speed, willingness. 

You will of course find wide variation in the products from this Experience. 

The approach should be much more mature than during the study of the same subject earlier. For Item 2, the student should have learned from the chapter called "Helping Your Reader to Think" that the treatment in Chapter 4 is more concise and telling. 

Sec. Exp. 25-3. These essays should be judged more for content than for literary style. It is useful for the secretary to be able to put her thoughts into good English, but all secretaries cannot be expected to have a gift for creative writing.

INTRODUCTION TO CHAINS 

Thinking out the word portrait should help the students clarify their ideas of the vocation as a whole. They should echo but not copy Secretarial Efficiency, giving independent reflection of what they have learned. Chain F serves as a definite review and summary of the text itself. If a classbook or magazine is published by the school or college, the best of these essays should surely have a place in it.

Chain F at the end of the Manual and the Guide will be carried through at any time near the close of the course to serve as a review of probably the most important principle of secretarial efficiency.

KEY AND GUIDE TO  

!!

For a general discussion of the 

Chain Principle, see introduction to Guide,

Section 6, "The 'Chain' Principle Vital to Secretarial Efficiency. Detailed instruc tion for each Chain is given to the student in the Manual; detailed helps for guiding the procedure, together with the necessary material for dictation, are given to you in this Guide. Each Chain involves principles recently studied and review of principles studied earlier in the course. Link by 

Link, these realistic Chains should integrate what has been learned. These are carried through to their reasonable and necessary conclusion by dealing with inter esting situations that call for concrete thinking and illustrate essential traits in their relation to actual duties requiring both understanding and skill. Virtually every task, however large or small, is but a Link in a Chain that becomes a transaction only in its entirety. All secretaries experience that fact; it is a principle of the social structure of life itself. 

Through these Chains, emphasis is placed on training the secretary to keep these Links connected in her mind and in her activities so that she bears an alert part in furthering the progress of an entire Chain toward completion. 

Take up the study of each Chain slowly, as they deserve careful attention from the individual student. One person may assimilate one kind of duty more quickly than another, but there is nothing here 149 that may not be expected of even a stenographer. Here we teach single duties, but in their natural relation to one another. Do not let the apprentice be so busy on the small parts of a Link that she sees neither the Link nor its place in the Chain. To make the class fully Chain-conscious, first be sure that they are thoroughly familiar with the material in Chapter 3 of the textbook. Then check their understanding of each Chain as it is worked out by discussing the following: 

1. The situation that has arisen, which must always be recognized 2. The way it is to be met and why 

3. Where the Chain "stands" at a certain time, which should be remembered or quickly found out by using the file 

4. What is being awaited (as "pending"), which becomes a definite responsibility of the apprentice 

5. What has been illustrated as to the use of skills, knowledge, and traits as a vital part of the success of the Chain 

6. The necessity of "seeing the point" of each separate Link and its relation to the Links before and after 7. The fact that seemingly slight Links should have their written record in the filed story, because such Links may be needed a year hence by someone who did not handle the matter but may find reason to use the Links for important reference Each Chain should be followed through to a finish, either actually or through discussion of the remainder of the Links, so that there will be no impression that a Chain can be left hanging uncompleted. As a review check, a summary or digest should be made at the completion of the Chain, with the whole story carefully filed together. 

In each case the Chain is ready for assignment after the procedure for the Links involved has been studied in the text and the groundwork has been laid by the

The principles of secretarial procedure can be taught without going to remote matters. 

This first Chain, therefore, deals with the apprentice herself, just as the next Chain deals with blankets lost in the laundry-two situations that of themselves give a clear basis for action.

The apprentice should pay strict attention to the neatness of the creases in folding a letter and an enclosure for the long envelope; the careless student must learn that these little matters are a part of the efficiency for which the secretary is paid. 

If you wish to have these letters "mailed" in a classroom mailbox, someone may be assigned for the distribution. should be pointed out that the incoming mail for the office may come to Miss Atwell's desk for her to sort and distribute to the desks of her associates. The appren- 'tice should realize that, when there is not a special mail clerk, the duty of distributing the mail regularly falls on some one person whose important responsibility it is to see that mail is not held up but reaches the right desks as promptly as possible. 

5. If you have a rubber date stamp, this should be used.

Key to Transcription of Rough Draft of Agreement (Work-sheet Form A-1, A-2). 

WHEREAS I, Alice M. Atwell, have become an apprentice in the (name of college), I hereby subscribe to the standards of apprenticeship for secretarial efficiency on this twentieth day of November in the year nineteen hundred and thirty-nine.

I will make it a part of my daily practice to give earnest consideration to my work so that my first day in a position will find me trained for all that I shall have to accomplish. 

Because I am aware that as a secretary all my work should be acceptable for immediate use, I will subscribe to the rule of care in every detail of every piece of work.

I will endeavor to increase my knowledge of words--their meaning, their pronunciation, and their spelling--adding to my own vocabulary as I learn.

I will watch my mistakes in order to see why I have made them, so that I may keep from falling down at the same points again. 

I will keep my papers in order as I go along, because this will quicken my efficiency now and will also equip me with habits which will later make my actual office work smooth and efficient.

I will try to understand why certain assignments and instructions are given so that I may follow through tasks because of the reasons behind them.

I will do my best to cultivate an even disposition, refusing to show irritation at little annoyances, remaining poised when things go wrong, keeping a low, self-possessed voice, and being courteous to those with whom I come in contact.

I will resolve to find nothing necessary to my career too hard to surmount, nothing too difficult to conquer.

I will welcome criticism and take pride in my work so that my own standards of accuracy, thoroughness, honesty, and dependability will be worthy for me to carry over into my first position. 

Because I am ambitious to become a successful secretary, I am signing this agreement, after careful reading.

Witness: 

Signed:
