letter from the amount of her shorthand notes and the fact that when the secretary put the paper for this letter into her ma chine she immediately decided that she would use single-space with a fair margin. (k) Good taste in using abbreviations should be cultivated by watching. Here there is no question but that a certain dignity and courtesy are obtained by the expenditure of the instant of time it takes to spell out the word Manager. 

4. Making a telephone call. At this point the letter is presumably on Mr. Merrick's desk; if you care to, you may have groups of students dramatize the next steps. 

You (or some other "Mr. Merrick") can use a buzzer (or bell) to call the appointed secretary. The latter should be sure to pick up her notebook and pen as she goes to the employer's desk. In dramatizing the telephone conversation, bring out to the class how few words will carry through Link 2. 

This study in letter writing integrates the study of 

English with this Chain. Here is a letter that has been "received,' and something is going to be done about it. Now what are the factors that make this a mailable letter that carries its point? The points that may be brought out under Items (a), (b), and (c) regarding the tone and the appearance of the letter need no comment here. Under (d) the spelling of thermome ters, direct from shorthand, might be mentioned. This is one of the words that the typist trips up on, often omitting a sylla- | hear, but brief conversations could be givble, especially if she is a person who does not visualize words or, as we say, see them in her mind's eye. The word too is also in this letter. This will not present a spelling difficulty, but the typist must use her intelligence in choosing the right spelling for it. Call attention to the

IT 

5. Introducing a caller. Link 3 is a conference, which the secretary does not en, with Mr. Dunn asking the secretary if he may see Mr. Merrick and the secretary announcing that Mr. Dunn is here and then telling Mr. Dunn he may go into the office. 

There will be few words, but the secretary's carriage and manner of speaking and her dispatch in getting Mr. Dunn before Mr. Merrick

are important. Much can be done to make this usual situation vivid to the apprentice as we go through Chains in which it is bound to occur. 

6. Taking a message. 

If you wish to give all the students the experience of taking down the telephone message, you yourself could be Mr. Dunn at this point and ask different students to "answer the telephone," requiring them all, however, to take down the message. Discuss with the class the question of how few actual key words need be taken down for such a message. 

Have someone put on the blackboard her shorthand notes for this and then cut through with colored chalk what is not necessary, pointing out that often a long message cannot conveniently be taken verbatim. 

Call this "short shorthand" if you please; the gist must be taken. For example: Dunn 

7 blankets found will be sent Hales' house immediately. 

7. Writing a memorandum. In the chapter of the textbook, called "Memorandums Do Not Forget" memorandums are fully discussed and opportunities for drill in their use are indicated for the student, but the memorandum naturally enters Chains preceding that chapter. The question may arise as to the use of a small telephonepad piece of paper for the transcription in 

Link 5. Such small pieces of paper may be used if they are later stapled onto full sheets for filing. Otherwise, half sheets are more convenient. ... 

8. Taking a letter. In dictating the letter for Link 6, assume the role of 

Mr. Merrick and do not dictate the letterhead, the date, the address, the words following the complimentary closing, or the word paragraph, which many men seldom indicate. Start dictating, as Mr. Merrick would, with the words: "Mr. Hale--before this letter gets into the mail The remainder of the address and the salutation itself will be left to the student, as indicated in the Manual. This dictation leap from the bare last name to the body of the letter is the familiar one for our apprentice to "experience." She must have what little practice we can give her in filling in addresses from correspondence received-or looking them up, when they are lacking.

Do not precede this dictation with any instructions for the student. She should use only her Manual and the office manual to which she is referred as a test of her ability to make correct transcriptions without specific instructions for each individual letter. (See letter top of page 155.)

In going over this Link and the other written Links, you will find several snags for the student. There must be the notation on the letter of the copy to be sent to the accounting department; that notation must be check-marked as done on the file copy; and the sentence concerning the credit marked on the copy that goes to the department. In regard to punctuation, the typed phrase of course must appear with its two commas even though they do not appear with the shorthand sign for the phrase itself. This is an excellent example for us to keep in mind. The secretary may have her outline for the phrase correct; her accurate typing of the words from the outline may be swift; but those commas must fall in line around the phrase by an association established through observation and drill. These commas are, so to speak, typing accessories that do not appear in shorthand. In other words, the secretary must type "more than meets the eye. This fact is given to the student direct in the chapter called "Right the First Time" and is here repeated because it is a principle to be kept before us in the conduct of the Chains.

When you hand back these transcriptions, make an opportunity for discussion of the English of this letter--its freedom from worn-out or stiff business phrases as a response to the easily phrased letter of 

Mr. Hale. This kind of letter breaks away both from the conventional mold and from the smart correspondence that wears too careless an air for dignity. Elastic writing demands elastic thinking; whenever we open the eyes of the apprentice to ease of expression, we are fostering her trait of fluidity of thought. 

9. Mailing Link 6. The students are purposely told only to "prepare" the original letter for the mail and the copy for the accounting department. In handing back the letters and the envelopes, discuss the following points unless the students' work shows that there is no need for discussion.

Discuss with the students the fact that the steps in the Chain were very simple and that any one was quickly accomplished. The secretary has been busy with all the Links; two of them she carried out alone. In point of time, her share of the work was probably considerably more than her employer's. She has been keeping at work on this Chain while her employer worked on others.

The end of this Chain should be clear--a complete Chain with Links (in this particular instance) forged in quick and satisfactory succession. Throughout the work on this Chain, we should carry over into the classroom the office efficiency of caring what happens, of concern that seven blankets went astray but are now reported found so that a good customer may be met with a reply to his satisfaction. 

Traits in this Chain. Either during the conduct of this chain or at the close, we should not neglect to question as to what secretarial traits, skill, and understanding Mr. Merrick's secretary especially exercised. These may be treated informally, as the word traits should not become a burden to the course. For example: 

Getting along with Mr. Merrick, Mr. Dunn, Mr. Dunn's secretary phoning 

Poise when Mr. Merrick is irritated 

Speed in turning off a brief memorandum 

Ability to spell

Knowledge of the principles of filing Dispatch, together with courtesy, in tele- With this Chain, we are establishing a grasp of the principle for the more difficult ones to follow. 

Special note to teacher regarding 

To effect conference with Mr. Dunn 

To start investigation 

To report blankets found and sent 

To notify of and record report 

To acknowledge complaint and state blankets on way and bill credited the Chain principle applied to local industries. In certain communities, one or more dominant industries may employ most of the students whom you train. Where this is true, you will do well to get one or another local firm to provide material, which you may use for Chains dealing with these industries, thus giving the apprentice a preview of the exact work she may be training for. For such material, ask a director of employment or an office manager or some officer whom you know personally, showing him one of the Chains in the Manual so that he will understand what you mean by the "whole story" of a single transaction. He should see the advantage to his concern of having new secretaries familiar with his type of business. 

This material, with fictitious names, can be used for dictation and for discussion. It may be that one of the employed graduates of your school can make copies of sequences, recalling what she herself would have been glad to understand and know before she started to work. These sequences will bring three special advantages to your course: (1) They will appeal because talk of this industry within the general vicinity is familiar to the student and therefore, not remote from her interests. (2) They will give an intimate experience in the kinds of Links that form the Chains of the specific industry. (3) They will build up a pertinent vocabulary--words that should be written with ease in shorthand, spelled correctly, and understood in their relation to the given industry, whether it concerns automobile manufacture, chemicals, insurance, shoes, produce, rubber, publishing, or furniture. 

Too much cannot be done in preparation for the actual work your apprentices will undertake.

Chain D (to be carried through after study of Section 21). This Chain should not be allowed to seem formidable to the student. 

Taken one at a time, the Links will be found to be simple enough, provided that the directions are followed step by step. 

Here we offer an endurance test to give experience in carrying through a single long piece of work and a foretaste of involved tasks that are carried out over a period of time and are considered ready for the employer only when entirely completed. For this reason, time should be allowed for the 

Chain as a whole. Since reference works available to your students may vary somewhat from those here required for one or another Link, you may find it necessary in some instances to substitute books containing like information. The Chain, however, purposely uses a wide variety of books, each illustrating specific methods of finding and a distinctive type of reference work. In addition to the general acquaintance with the various books, the student should gain from the Chain (1) ability to go with unfailing directness for information to any sort of book; (2) wisdom in turning to the preface of an unfamiliar book of reference to learn how best to use the book; and (3) knowledge of just which book will give the best information for a given purpose. 

The report should be graded on the three points of instruction, as given in the Manual: (1) information obtained; (2) complete source reference, which will vary with the books available; (3) intelligent use of all possible guides to the most direct finding. Though it would be desirable to know the over-all time taken for this project by each apprentice, this is hardly practicable; the nearest we can come to gauging the swiftness of the individual in this type of work is to give a reasonable due date and to recognize that the student who outlines the best methods presumably carries through the whole Chain in the least time. To carry through the imagined purpose of this investigation, you should designate which apprentice has been chosen for the position of secretary to 

Professor Atwell, showing what definite qualifications led to your choice.

Summary. For further drill, other projects of the kind can readily be devised by you or by the apprentices themselves, by turning to any reference work and finding an interesting item of information to be assigned for research. Even a single Link will test clarity of attention and a grasp of ways and means of finding. 

Nothing can teach the apprentice the nature of finding better than going to the sources and working out Links of research, thus delving into available authoritative material for its own sake. During work on this Chain the apprentice should be aware

that she is using principles such as would be applicable in finding information not only for a professor but also for a man in any line of activity who needs to have his time conserved by an able assistant. 

Chain E (to be carried through after study of Section 21). After the apprentices have read the first paragraph about this Chain in their Manuals, dictate these instructions as from Mr. Grimes: "Next week, Wednesday, is Mr. and 

Mrs. Annis's fiftieth wedding anniversary. 

I think we should send them a large basket. of flowers. Will you talk with that flower shop where I have an account and ask what they can do for about twenty-five dollars? 

You'd better ask for the manager himself. 

Let me know what he says before you order.

In the meantime, send around notices to the staff, asking what they wish to contribute. 

I'll make up the difference. Write a handwritten note to go with the flowers, which we all can sign. See to collecting the money, and deposit it to my account."

Since most of these Links are to be composed by the apprentices, there will be no uniformity of form. The points that should be covered in each Link appear in the list below for your convenience in checking the Links written by the students.

Summary. In checking and subse quently discussing the Links, allow considerable variation in form. Make comparisons among several of the best examples of the written Links. No Link could have been omitted, but Link 3 might have been an oral report to Mr. Grimes and Link 4 might have been combined with Link 7. In that case the secretary could have done one of two things: (1) made a rough tabulation to take around to the staff announcing the plan and entering their contributions at once; or (2) shown Mr. Grimes the reports on the slips instead of making the tabulation. The former plan would waste time as she must make the rounds again with the letter, and the latter plan would be less convenient for her employer. Unless an apprentice has a really good suggestion, the possibility of shortening the Chain should be vetoed.

Chain F (to be carried through near the close of the course). This final Chain is not, strictly speaking, a Chain. It rather brings the study of Secretarial Efficiency to a close with emphasis on a personal understanding of the vital principle that we have called the "Link-and-Chain" principle. To allow time for this comprehensive study, it should be assigned well in advance of the due date at the end of the course. A positive awareness of this Link and Chain principle should make itself evident during the first day in a position, by the very way in which the apprentice (who is now called a secretary) "takes hold of" her work.
